# Algorithm research report (2026-11-16)

Output of the monthly research agents in `.github/workflows/algorithm-audit-drift.yml` (prompts in `.github/prompts/`). Everything here is **unverified**: web claims, not measurements. A finding means *re-look*, not *wrong*. Procedure: `R/package_metadata/new_feature_plans/v1_0_5/algorithm_audit_runbook.md`.

## 1. Faster-algorithm findings
### Batch A.1: alternating IRLS and Newton on θ → Group the θ score/information sums by distinct y value: compute digamma/trigamma(y+θ) once per distinct y (D values, typically D ≪ n for counts) and weight by counts. This is the representation glmGamPoi credits for its fast overdispersion estimation, and the same distinct-y table pattern is already used in fast_zinb.cpp and fast_hurdle_negbin.cpp.
- **Current:** Each outer iteration does one Newton step on θ, calling fast_digamma(y_i+θ) inside a per-observation loop and fast_trigamma_vec over all n values of y+θ. That is O(n) special-function calls per outer iteration.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI's sizes. The glmGamPoi paper reports 6–13× for its whole pipeline versus edgeR/DESeq2 on single-cell data (not comparable). Here the gain is bounded by the special-function share of each outer iteration, which the IRLS O(inner·n·m²) inner loop likely dominates. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same estimator; sums change only by floating-point reordering (tolerance-equal, not bit-identical). It adds a small amount of code, and the win may be negligible for small n or large m. This is a micro-optimization, not an algorithm change; the catalog keeps the alternation itself.
- **Suggested action:** Profile univariate_negbin_tstat / multivariate_negbin_tstat on a KK21 count fixture. Only if the θ-step special functions are a visible share of time, reuse the distinct-y table helper from fast_zinb.cpp.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::univariate_negbin_tstat`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::multivariate_negbin_tstat`
- **Sources:** https://academic.oup.com/bioinformatics/article/36/24/5701/6029133

### Batch A.1: alternating IRLS / Newton (glm.nb) → fastglm::fastglm_nb (fastglm 0.1.4, CRAN 2026-11-11, GPL (>= 2)): the same alternating NB2 MLE in C++, with step-halving and different initialization. EDI's own C++ NB fit (fast_neg_bin_internal) is the in-house alternative.
- **Current:** MASS::glm.nb (alternating IRLS for β and theta.ml Newton for θ), with a column-drop retry on error. Used on the KK21 stepwise fallback path.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The vignettes report convergence, not timing. Convergence: 200/200 for fastglm_nb vs 165/200 for MASS at n=200, p=9, θ=1, with agreeing fits matching to max |Δℓ| 1.5e-6 (nb-convergence vignette). The stability benchmark reports 570/600 vs 425/600 clean fits across 12 scenarios (n=40–200). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a reliability gain, not a demonstrated speedup. It would add a dependency (license compatible), or the path could reuse EDI's existing C++ NB fit instead. The path is a cold fallback, so priority is low. The benchmarks were written by the fastglm author (not independent), and the success criteria differ between the packages.
- **Suggested action:** Low priority: consider routing the KK21 fallback through EDI's own C++ NB fit (or fastglm_nb) to cut glm.nb non-convergence on the fallback path. Re-run KK21 count-matching fixtures if changed.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_robust_regression.R::robust_negbinreg`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/nb-convergence-fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/nb-stability-fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastglm

### Batch A.2: brute-force series expected information → Compute the expected information analytically. The β-block is a closed-form weight times x xᵀ. The θ-related terms reduce to a scalar series over tail probabilities, Σ_j c_j(θ)·Pr(Y≥j), as in Eq. (2.12)–(2.13) of the source; the zero-truncated version renormalizes by 1−P(0). The series is built in one pass with the pmf/cdf recurrence, stopped adaptively once the tail mass is negligible, then added as a single rank-1/diagonal update per observation. Cost is O(n+·(Y_eff + p²)).
- **Current:** TruncatedNegBinCount::expected_hessian sums over y=1..max(10000, μ+12sd) for each observation. For every y it builds a 1-row functor and calls the full hessian, which costs O(p²) and heap allocations per term. Total is O(n+·Y·p²) with Y≥10⁴. No caller exists.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. Analytically the per-term work drops from an O(p²) Hessian plus allocations to O(1) scalar work, roughly p²× fewer flops, and Y_eff ≈ μ+k·sd instead of ≥10⁴. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The function is dead code, so this only matters if the expected information is ever wired in. The alternative is to delete it. The tail series needs a stopping rule with a tail-mass bound so accuracy holds near the Poisson boundary (large θ). The source itself says truncation is out of its scope.
- **Suggested action:** Delete the dead expected_hessian, or, if expected information is ever needed for the truncated NB, rewrite it with the tail-probability series and an adaptive cutoff instead of per-y Hessian calls.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_negbin.cpp::expected_hessian`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2407.05824v1

### Batch A.3: column-pivoted QR least squares → Normal equations: symmetric_crossprod, then LLT or LDLT, as fast_ols_internal already does. Fall back to the current ColPivHouseholderQR when the factorization reports failure, the β is non-finite, or a cheap pivot-ratio check flags near-singularity.
- **Current:** ColPivHouseholderQR on the n×p design for every smart-cold-start OLS solve, about 2np² flops with BLAS-2-heavy pivoting. It runs once per cold-started fit, including every cold-started resampling replicate.
- **Claimed speedup:** Eigen's dense decomposition benchmark (single-precision float, one thread), 10000x8 column: ColPivHouseholderQR 36.05 ms vs LLT 6.79 ms and LDLT 6.81 ms including forming AᵀA, about 5.3x. At 10000x100 it is 163 vs 30 ms, about 5.4x. EDI's sizes (n in the hundreds to low thousands, p≈2–20) are not benchmarked. The saving on a whole fit is smaller, because the start is one of several O(np²) passes. Not quantified for EDI. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Normal equations square κ(X). Eigen's least-squares page calls them the fastest but least accurate and says they are not good for even mildly ill-conditioned A. For a start value this only perturbs the starting point. Concave families converge to the same MLE within tolerance. On nonconcave families (NB, ZINB, beta, Weibull) a perturbed start could in principle change which local optimum is reached, so an A/B on the fixture corpus is needed. LDLT does not reliably flag rank deficiency (catalog §1 Incidental 7), so the QR fallback trigger must be explicit, for example the LLT info() check plus a diagonal-ratio test. The robust-regression path reuses the QR start to cache the XtX_inv_diag_j scalar (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md, ~line 1017), so that path would need the same scalar from the Cholesky factor or should keep QR. Deterministic, and no license issue (Eigen MPL2, already used).
- **Suggested action:** Fold this into performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155 (linear-algebra choices at actual sizes). Micro-benchmark try_safe_ols_solve with ColPivQR against crossprod+LLT with a QR fallback at n∈{100,500,2000}, p∈{2,5,10}. Measure the share of a cold-started fit spent in the start, then A/B converged estimates and iteration counts on nonconcave families before switching. Keep ColPivQR wherever its rank-revealing behaviour or the cached diagonal is actually used.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::try_safe_ols_solve`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::safe_ols_solve`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::ols_smart_cold_start_beta`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::ols_smart_cold_start_beta_on_log1p`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__LeastSquares.html

### Batch A.4: damped Newton with step-halving → Gradient-regularized Newton step (AN2CE/AN2CER, Gratton–Jerad–Toint): solve (H_k + sqrt(κ_a σ_k ||g_k||) I) s = −g. A negative-curvature/eigen step is added only when needed, using the shift (σ_k||g_k||^{1/2} + [−λ_min(H_k)]_+) I. This gives a concrete, essentially parameter-free τ rule for the 'add τI' branch of algorithm_choice_audit_catalog.md §1 TODO-9. A positive-definite system also allows LLT instead of FullPivLU (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155).
- **Current:** Observed Hessian with a FullPivLU solve and no descent-direction or positive-definiteness check. The step is halved down to 1e-4; on failure the driver hands off to L-BFGS from Newton's endpoint. warm_start_hessian is (void)-ignored.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified relative to EDI's current loop. Section 6 of https://arxiv.org/html/2302.10065 reports iteration-count performance-profile areas on 119 small OPM/CUTEst problems (2–49 variables): AN2CE 0.91 and AN2CER 0.88, against AR2 0.92 and trust-region TR2M 0.91. Reliability was 96.6% for both AN2C variants, 97.5% for AR2 and 95.0% for TR2M. AN2CER needed a single linear solve in about 93% of iterations. The source shows parity with standard safeguarded methods, not a win over them; the expected gain for EDI is fewer wasted halving cascades and fewer L-BFGS hand-offs on NB/beta Newton paths. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** On a strictly concave likelihood near the optimum the regularization vanishes as ||g||→0, so the same MLE is reached and quadratic local convergence is kept. On non-concave surfaces (NB θ, beta φ) the iterate path changes, so a different local optimum or a different set of failed fits is possible. That needs the A/B harness already required for TODO-9. It is deterministic, adds no license issue (it is an algorithm, not a library), and costs about one extra p×p solve occasionally, which is negligible at EDI's p. The paper shows no advantage over plain Levenberg–Marquardt or a trust region at small dimension, so any standard safeguard should do. This finding only supplies a tested τ rule.
- **Suggested action:** When prototyping algorithm_choice_audit_catalog.md §1 TODO-9, consider the √||g|| regularization rule (with the [−λ_min]_+ shift on detected indefiniteness) as the τ choice. Factor with LLT, and keep step-halving and the L-BFGS fallback as the outer safety net. Judge it by halving-cascade and L-BFGS hand-off counts in the A/B harness. Do not expect a speedup on well-behaved concave fits.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_likelihood_newton`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_likelihood_newton_then_lbfgs`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.10065 https://arxiv.org/html/2302.10065

### Batch A.5: Fisher scoring (VGAM IRLS) → fastglm::fastglm_ordinal(nonparallel = ~terms) (fastglm ≥ 0.1.3, CRAN 0.1.4 dated 2026-11-11). It is a compiled C++ ML fit of the same partial-proportional-odds cumulative-link model (logit/probit/cloglog): observed-information Newton steps with step halving (max.halves), convergence certified by expected information, and robust or clustered vcov in C++. It could serve as the first rung, or as a faster first attempt before vglm.
- **Current:** Partial proportional odds with nonparallel terms is fitted by the ladder VGAM::vglm(cumulative, parallel=…) (R-level IRLS/Fisher scoring) → ordinal::clm(nominal=) → MASS::polr → weighted surrogate.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The ordinal vignette and NEWS give no timings against vglm, clm or polr. For context only, fastglm's benchmark vignette shows its C++ GLM fits about 2–4× faster than glm.fit at n=1,000, p=30 (logistic 0.67–1.26 ms vs 2.75 ms); that is a different model. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** It is the same PPO likelihood, so a regular interior MLE should match vglm within tolerance, but no agreement table was found. fastglm requires the cumulative predictors to stay ordered at the observed rows, while vglm can return improper (crossing) fits. On the problematic cases the ladder exists for, the two may therefore converge, fail, or report convergence differently, which could change which rung's answer EDI reports. Default SEs come from expected information; vglm's IRLS also uses expected information, but EDI's own SE source must be kept consistent. It adds a new Suggests dependency (GPL-2|GPL-3, compatible). The package is a very recent release, so its stability is unproven. Results are deterministic (no RNG).
- **Suggested action:** Re-look: on EDI's PPO fixtures (n tens to low thousands, K 3–7), A/B fastglm_ordinal(nonparallel=) against VGAM::vglm for coefficient and SE agreement, convergence on the improper-fit cases, and wall-clock time. If it agrees on all regular cases, consider it as a faster first rung, with vglm kept as the fallback.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_partial_proportional_odds.R::fit_vgam`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastglm https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/ordinal-fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/news/news.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/refman/fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/benchmarks-fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/VGAM/NEWS

### Batch A.6: IRLS with step-halving (constrained) → Formulate the log-binomial MLE (concave log-likelihood, linear constraints Xβ≤0) as an exponential-cone program and solve it with a primal-dual interior-point conic solver (ECOS, GPL-3; Clarabel, Apache-2.0). This needs no feasible start, because the slack-variable formulation removes that requirement. The identity link is also a convex program, using log terms in exponential cones.
- **Current:** Fisher-scoring IRLS with step-halving, infeasibility rejection, active-set projection and a KKT gate at the bound (v1.0.5 TODO-128). It starts from a possibly infeasible point.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not a raw speedup at EDI sizes. Schwendinger, Grün & Hornik 2021 (Comput Stat, §4.6.2; n=500, 1000 reps; 10 and 100 covariates): only ecos, ipopt and auglag (with tuned settings) returned the optimum in every case, and their timings were 'rather similar' at 10 covariates. At 100 covariates ecos was 'among the quickest' and clearly beat ipopt/auglag. glm/glm2 were also among the quickest but 'tend to return suboptimal solutions' at the boundary. The paper gives no ratio versus IRLS at p≈10, and reads it only from a figure (not quantified in text). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same MLE: the problem is convex with a unique MLE when X has full rank. The win is reliability for boundary MLEs, which EDI's IRLS reaches only by halving against a −∞ barrier. Interior-point methods converge to the boundary only to within the solver tolerance, so the KKT certificate and the boundary SE handling still need care. Adding a new dependency (ECOS is C and GPL-3, fine for EDI; Clarabel needs a Rust toolchain) is a significant cost, and per-refit overhead inside parametric-bootstrap or CI-inversion loops at p≤10 is unmeasured. It is deterministic. It overlaps the existing constrained-solver Prototype (catalog O4 / row Z TODO-32), which listed active-set, EM, adaptive barrier and COPY but not conic programming.
- **Suggested action:** Add 'exponential-cone interior point (ECOS/Clarabel)' as an option row in catalog O4 and include it among the candidates for the row Z constrained-solver Prototype (TODO-32), A/B tested on boundary-MLE fixtures for converged rate and wall-clock at EDI's n and p. Do not adopt it without that measurement.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_log_binomial.R::generate_mod`, `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_binomial_identity.R::generate_mod`
- **Sources:** https://d-nb.info/1231156651/34 https://github.com/embotech/ecos https://github.com/oxfordcontrol/Clarabel.cpp

### Batch A.7: L-BFGS / Newton–Raphson (IRLS) → Make "irls" (Newton = Fisher scoring for the canonical logit link, already implemented in C++) the wrapper default, matching the C++ default and the catalog's description of L-BFGS as the 'non-default alternative' (catalog §1 row :109).
- **Current:** The R wrappers default to optimization_alg = "lbfgs" (helper_glm_fit.R:852, :923; .normalize_optimizer_algorithm(..., default = "lbfgs")), while fast_logistic_regression[_with_var]_cpp itself defaults to "irls". The KK21 / KK21-stepwise designs call fast_logistic_regression_with_var for each covariate (p = 2) to build covariate weights. On separation the wrapper drops the largest coefficient and refits.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The sources give quadratic local convergence for Newton on a strongly convex objective (Wikipedia, Newton's method in optimization) and the equivalence IRLS = Newton for logistic regression (Wikipedia, Logistic regression). At p = 2 each Newton step costs O(n) plus a 2×2 solve, against L-BFGS's superlinear rate with a line search. Typical counts are about 5–8 IRLS iterations against tens for L-BFGS (catalog estimate, not a measurement). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same MLE within tolerance, but not bit-identical (different iterate path), so KK21 weight tests need tolerance checks. Under separation the two optimizers stop at different large-but-finite β, and the separation check (EDI_SEPARATION_THRESHOLD) and drop order could differ on edge cases. Deterministic, and no license issue. The tuning axis mentioned in the catalog may already override this default; check before changing.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look. Confirm whether the default "lbfgs" in the R wrappers is intentional (the C++ default and catalog row :109 say IRLS). If not, switch the wrapper default to "irls" and A/B the KK21 weights and the separation-drop outcomes. Fix the catalog/inventory wording ('C++ default L-BFGS') either way.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_glm_fit.R::fast_logistic_regression`, `R/EDI/R/helper_glm_fit.R::fast_logistic_regression_with_var`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton%27s_method_in_optimization https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistic_regression

### Batch A.7: L-BFGS (single start) → Stringer (arXiv 2310.01589v2, Nov 2024): exact gradient of the AGQ approximate log-likelihood for one grouping factor. It handles the mode through implicit differentiation of the stationarity condition and the Cholesky factor through reverse-mode AD. It is optimized with L-BFGS (LBFGSpp) from a GLM start, then one Newton step on a finite-difference-of-exact-gradients Hessian for the Wald SEs. With AGQ, far fewer nodes than 20–60 non-adaptive nodes would be needed.
- **Current:** L-BFGS from one start on a fixed (non-adaptive) Gauss–Hermite marginal likelihood (n_gh = 20; 60 for Poisson since f15b4423), with an analytic gradient. Adaptive GH (TODO-153) is held back partly because the catalog (§4, adaptive GH row) says the exact AGQ gradient must differentiate through û_g(θ) and is not an envelope identity, and it estimates only a 1.2–1.6× gain.
- **Claimed speedup:** Paper §4–5: usually 2–4× faster than lme4 (BOBYQA) and GLMMadaptive. For the scalar random intercept (m = 1000, n = 5, σ² = 2) it is typically 2–4× faster than lme4. On toenail data (m = 294, n ≤ 7) it is up to 7× faster than lme4 and 2.5–10× faster than GLMMadaptive. These are relative to other packages, not to EDI's non-adaptive-GH L-BFGS, so the gain over EDI is not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** AGQ with k nodes approximates the same marginal-likelihood MLE differently from non-adaptive GH with 20/60 nodes. Estimates change within quadrature error (more accurate at large σ), so this is an accuracy-motivated estimator-approximation change, as TODO-153 already frames it. It needs a per-group inner Newton for û_g on each evaluation. The paper's code license (github.com/awstringer1/aghmm-paper-code) was not checked, but the method can be reimplemented from the paper. It is deterministic. It does not address the single-start issue.
- **Suggested action:** Add Stringer 2024 to the TODO-153 (adaptive GH) plan and catalog §4. It answers the 'exact AGQ gradient is not an envelope identity' objection while keeping L-BFGS (same LBFGSpp library), so the catalog's 1.2–1.6× estimate should be revisited. The gain is node reduction (60 → ~5–11) at the same or better accuracy. Prototype on the Poisson GLMM first, where 60 non-adaptive nodes are now required.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::fast_logistic_glmm_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::fast_poisson_glmm_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::fast_clogit_plus_glmm_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_ordinal_clmm.cpp::run_fast_ordinal_clmm`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2310.01589v2

### Batch A.8: LU inverse (FullPivLU) → One factorization. Either reuse the FullPivLU (lu.inverse()), or, for an SPD information matrix, LDLT/LLT with a pivot-based check of definiteness/condition followed by solve(Identity).
- **Current:** A FullPivLU factorization used only for the isInvertible() check, followed by information.inverse(), which runs a second, partial-pivot LU. That is two O(p^3) factorizations.
- **Claimed speedup:** Eigen's dense decomposition benchmark (float, 8x8; the page says to roughly double the times for double): LLT 0.05, LDLT 0.07 (1.3x), PartialPivLU 0.08 (1.5x), FullPivLU 0.10 (1.9x). The current FullPivLU + PartialPivLU pair is therefore about 2.6x one LDLT on the factorization part at 8x8, before counting the inverse back-substitution. Not quantified at the whole-fit level, where the O(np^2) Hessian build dominates. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Gain per call is microseconds and negligible against the O(n p^2) Hessian build unless n is small. LDLT/LLT are not rank-revealing the way FullPivLU is (Eigen rates FullPivLU accuracy 'Proven', LDLT 'Good'), so the invertibility test would need a pivot-ratio or rcond criterion. Results change in the last bits compared with today's .inverse() output, and the code comment says the current form deliberately preserves pre-guard numerics. The cheapest no-risk version is lu.inverse(): it drops one factorization and stays bitwise close to FullPivLU accuracy. MPL2 (Eigen) is GPL-compatible, and the change is deterministic.
- **Suggested action:** No new work item. Attach this small-size benchmark to v1.1.0-TODO-155 (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md) and, as the minimal fix, switch invert_free_information to lu.inverse() so it factors once. Consider LDLT only with an A/B on the existing vcov fixtures.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::invert_free_information`, `R/EDI/src/fast_negbin_regression.cpp::fast_neg_bin_with_var_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/fast_beta_regression.cpp::fast_beta_regression_with_var_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/fast_ordinal_regression.cpp::fast_ordinal_regression_internal`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__TopicLinearAlgebraDecompositions.html

### Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson (IRLS) quasi-likelihood → Call the package's C++ kernel fast_logistic_regression_cpp(X, y, estimate_only=TRUE), which compute_treatment_estimate_during_randomization_inference (line 257) already uses for the same Bernoulli quasi-likelihood on y∈[0,1]. This is the same estimator (same score equations, same concave QMLE), solved by C++ IRLS with a Cholesky-family (LDLT) step.
- **Current:** When estimate_only=TRUE and harden=FALSE, compute_estimate (inference_proportion_fractional_logit.R:160-170) fits the fractional-logit QMLE with base R glm.fit(family=quasibinomial()): R-level IRLS with a pivoted-QR WLS solve each iteration. The docstring calls this the 'fast path'. The nonparametric bootstrap and jackknife drivers call compute_estimate(estimate_only=TRUE) once per replicate (inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R:484/498/619/645, inference_all_abstract_jackknife.R:185), so with harden=FALSE this R-level fit runs in a B-replicate loop.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not measured for EDI. fastglm's CRAN benchmark vignette (logistic, p=30, n=1,000, median of 5 runs) reports glm.fit at 2.75 ms vs a C++ Cholesky IRLS at 0.67 ms (~4×) and pivoted QR at 1.26 ms (~2.2×). The gap grows with n. At EDI's smaller n, glm.fit's fixed R overhead likely makes the ratio larger, but no source quantifies that. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** (1) glm.fit has step-halving and the C++ logistic kernel does not yet (catalog §1 TODO-2, Adopt, pending), so a separated or near-boundary resample could fail to converge where glm.fit succeeds. Land TODO-2 first, or fall back to glm.fit when the C++ fit reports converged=FALSE. (2) glm.fit's pivoted QR silently aliases collinear columns (NA coefficient) while the C++ LDLT does not detect near-singularity (catalog O2), so aliased bootstrap designs need the existing QR-drop guard. (3) Results agree only to IRLS tolerance, not bitwise. This affects only the non-default harden=FALSE path; the default hardened path already uses C++.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look: route the estimate_only/harden=FALSE branch of FracLogit compute_estimate to fast_logistic_regression_cpp(estimate_only=TRUE), matching the randomization-inference path, with a glm.fit fallback on non-convergence. Benchmark one bootstrap at EDI n before adopting. Also check other classes' estimate_only 'fast paths' for the same glm.fit pattern.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_fractional_logit.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_fractional_logit.R::generate_mod`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/benchmarks-fastglm.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastglm

### Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson with random-restart multistart → Make the first attempt survreg with init omitted (NULL). survreg then uses the distribution's documented init(x, weights) mean/variance estimate, which is deterministic and data-driven. Before any random jitter, add deterministic starts that are scale-aware: intercept = mean log-time, log-scale from the residual SD (the existing weibull_smart_cold_start pattern applied to lognormal and loglogistic). Keep the seeded jitter only as a last resort, scaled by the start's SE or by |start|.
- **Current:** Weibull: a C++ fit, then survreg with init set to that fit. Otherwise, and as the Weibull fallback: survreg from init = rep(0, p+1), then up to 50 restarts with init += rnorm (unscaled, seeded jitter). The zero vector overrides survreg's own default start.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. Expected to come from fewer failed survreg calls before a converged fit. A zero intercept is far from log(time) for typical time scales, so the zero-start first call is the one most likely to fail. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The likelihood is nonconcave (non-Weibull AFT), so a different start can land on a different local optimum. This needs the A/B harness, as the existing verdict already says. Deterministic in all cases (no RNG unless every deterministic start fails). Hot path: KK21 and KK21-stepwise survival weights in their R fallback path (the C++ kk21_survival_weights_cpp is the default), and the .extract_* start helpers.
- **Suggested action:** Fold into multistart_nonconcave_likelihoods.md as the concrete 'deterministic data-driven starts' prototype. First start init=NULL (survreg default), then a moment start, then scaled jitter. A/B the convergence-failure rate and the attempts per fit.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_robust_regression.R::robust_survreg_with_surv_object`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/refman/survival.html

### Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson (survreg) → Interim fast path for the unclustered case, mirroring the coxph.fit path in the same file: call survival::survreg.fit(x = cbind(1, X), y = Surv(...), weights =, offset = 0, init = warm, controlvals = survreg.control(), dist = survreg.distributions$weibull, scale = 0) on the prebuilt matrix. Fall back to the formula path on error. Same Newton–Raphson, same likelihood, same start. The native weighted C++ Weibull kernel (bayesian_bootstrap_performance.md item 2) remains the end state.
- **Current:** Each Bayesian-bootstrap draw builds a data.frame and a formula and calls survival::survreg(weights=, dist='weibull', init=warm). This repeats model.frame/model.matrix construction per draw. The Cox sibling already skips this through a coxph.fit direct path.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. No source benchmarks survreg.fit against survreg. The gain is removal of per-draw formula and model-frame overhead, multiplied by B. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** survreg.fit is documented under the survival package's 'Internal survival functions' topic, so its interface is less stable than survreg's. Pin it with a fixture test against survreg. The clustered (robust=TRUE) case still needs the formula path or a manual sandwich. Moot once the native weighted kernel lands.
- **Suggested action:** Add as an interim sub-item next to the coxph.fit interim in bug_fix_plans/v1_0_5/bayesian_bootstrap_performance.md item 2. Benchmark per-draw time on a Weibull Bayesian-bootstrap fixture before adopting.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/globals.R::weighted_weibull_bootstrap_surrogate_fit`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/refman/survival.html

### Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson (stratified Cox) over design ladder → Build the candidates lazily: a generator or iterator that yields the next rung's design only after the previous fit was rejected. The fits are tried in the same order and the same design is accepted, so the result is bit-identical.
- **Current:** shared() calls private$cox_design_candidates(w_r, X_cov_r) when harden=TRUE (the default). That builds all ≤13 candidate designs up front: a QR column-drop pass, then 12 thresholds, each running drop_highly_correlated_cols (a correlation matrix) plus another hardened QR. Only then does it loop over fast_coxph_regression_cpp and break at the first usable fit, which is normally the first candidate.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. Building the ladder eagerly costs up to ~12 × (O(n p²) correlation + O(n p²) QR) per call, plus R overhead. That is comparable to or larger than the one O(iters·n p²) Newton fit that usually succeeds. The saving is multiplied wherever shared() runs per randomization or bootstrap replicate. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** None statistically: same rung order, same accepted fit. A small code change. Check whether the lwa-Cox sibling (inference_survival_KK_lwa_cox_ivwc_abstract.R:208, which already iterates for X_full in cox_design_candidates) has the same eager pattern.
- **Suggested action:** Profile shared() on a KK strat-Cox randomization run to see what share of time goes to cox_design_candidates. If it is material, make the ladder lazy, gated by the existing fixtures (results must be identical).
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_KK_strat_cox.R::shared`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_KK_strat_cox.R::shared_combined_likelihood`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazy_evaluation

### Batch A.13: risk-set rescan (Breslow likelihood) → Standard sorted reverse-cumulative-sum risk sums (Breslow ties: one risk sum per failure time), i.e. sort once by (stratum, time) and take within-stratum reverse cumsums of exp(eta - max eta); EDI already implements exactly this in C++ compute_cox_neg_ll_only (fast_coxph_regression.cpp, backward accumulation, max-eta shift). Expose it (or a vectorized R order/rev-cumsum equivalent) for the fixed-beta NLL.
- **Current:** Pure-R Breslow NLL: loop over unique strata with which(strata_id == s) (O(n) per stratum), then per unique event time rescan the risk set y >= tt; no max-eta shift (returns NA if exp(eta) overflows). For KK matched-pair strata this is ~O(n^2). Inventory calls it dead code, but it is live: kk_cox_fully_fixed_null_fit (inference_survival_KK_strat_cox.R:4) calls it as the entire null fit when ncol(X_fit)==1, from the real-data LR spec (:599, inference_survival_KK_lwa_cox_one_lik_abstract.R:220) and per parametric-bootstrap draw (:659, lwa :183).
- **Claimed speedup:** not quantified by the source; complexity O(n^2) -> O(n log n) (O(n) after sorting) for KK pair strata (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Identical Breslow partial likelihood (same tie rule); numerically more robust (max-eta shift avoids NA on overflow at extreme delta in CI searches). Needs a small Rcpp export or R rewrite; inventory row must be corrected from 'dead code'.
- **Suggested action:** Correct inventory (live, used by KK strat/LWA Cox p=1 null fits incl. param-bootstrap draws); replace the rescan with the existing C++ backward-cumsum NLL or a vectorized R reverse cumsum, and verify equality to 1e-10 on fixtures with ties and strata.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_coxph.R::.cox_neg_loglik_breslow_r`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/glmnet/vignettes/coxdev.html

### Batch A.13: two-start constrained refit → Single refit with a certified convergence check (max |gradient| below tolerance, as clm's Newton uses gradTol), running the second start only when the first fails the certificate. Justified because the basic cumulative-link log-likelihood is concave with a unique global optimum (Pratt 1981; Burridge 1981), and fixing beta_T restricts it to an affine subspace, which preserves concavity.
- **Current:** Every cloglog constrained null refit (real-data LR and each parametric-bootstrap draw) runs two full L-BFGS refits (warm start and full-fit-with-delta nudged +1e-3) and keeps the lower NLL: 2x refits.
- **Claimed speedup:** not quantified by the source; ~2x fewer null refits by construction when the first start converges (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same unique constrained MLE within tolerance. Relies on a gradient-norm stop rather than the current f-plateau L-BFGS stop; if the solver stops early (the failure that motivated the 2-start, see ordinal_cumulative_link_null_refit_multistart.md), the fallback must trigger. Does not apply to the stereotype model (nonconcave).
- **Suggested action:** Resolve the existing 'Investigate' verdict: run the second start only when the first refit's max |gradient| exceeds tolerance (or switch to Newton per catalog §10 item 1); A/B the LR p-value calibration at the null on the fixtures that motivated the multistart.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_cloglog.R::fit_null`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/vignettes/clm_article.pdf

### Batch B.1: TMB automatic differentiation + nlminb (glmmTMB) → Thread estimate_only into the glmmTMB call as se = !estimate_only, so estimate-only replicates skip sdreport. Optionally also set glmmTMBControl(conv_check='skip', eigval_check=FALSE) on estimate-only replicates. Keep parallel=1 when the replicate loop is already parallel; the catalog §10 incidental finding already flags thread oversubscription.
- **Current:** Each Bayesian-bootstrap replicate calls glmmTMB::glmmTMB(..., weights=row_weights, control=glmmTMBControl(parallel=num_cores)) with the default se=TRUE. fit_zero_augmented_model (inference_count_zero_augmented_poisson_abstract.R:1084-1107) and inference_count_hurdle.R:434-444 never receive estimate_only, so TMB sdreport (Hessian/vcov) runs on every replicate, including estimate-only ones. The inventory text 'with full sdreport unless estimate_only' does not match the code: estimate_only only skips the summary() SE extraction (lines ~175-178).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The glmmTMB reference manual documents se as 'whether to return standard errors' and eigval_check as possibly 'very slow' for many fixed-effect parameters, but gives no timing. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Identical point estimate: the same nlminb fit, only post-fit SE work is skipped. Studentized/BCa bootstrap variants that need per-replicate SEs must keep se=TRUE (estimate_only=FALSE). This is an interim fix; catalog §10 TODO-3 (weighted C++ kernels, theoretical 10–100×) remains the structural fix and makes it moot.
- **Suggested action:** Small low-risk change: pass se = !estimate_only (and skip conv/eigval checks) in the estimate-only weighted glmmTMB replicate paths, and time one B=200 run before and after. Also correct the inventory 'details' text, which wrongly says sdreport is skipped under estimate_only.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_count_zero_augmented_poisson_abstract.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_zero_augmented_poisson_abstract.R::fit_zero_augmented_model`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_hurdle.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/glmmTMB/refman/glmmTMB.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=glmmTMB

### Batch B.1: L-BFGS (single start, joint) → C++ EM with SQUAREM acceleration for ZIP/ZINB (Lambert-style E-step posterior of structural zero, IRLS M-steps, θ by 1-D update), as now shipped in fastglm::fastglm_zi. Use it as a reference implementation and comparator (or EM warm-up start) in the em_algorithm_zero_inflated_mixtures.md A/B, not as a dependency.
- **Current:** One crude cold start (ZINB: π=0.5, θ=1; ZIP: zap_smart_cold_start), then joint L-BFGS plus boundary rescues. No EM path exists; EM is only planned in em_algorithm_zero_inflated_mixtures.md.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for ZI. fastglm NEWS 0.1.1 adds SQUAREM 'to speed up EM convergence' and gives no ZI timings. Its vignette reports coefficients and log-likelihood matching pscl to ~1e-5 for the ZI fit. The SQUAREM paper reports large gains over plain EM on Poisson mixtures (e.g. ~40× fewer fixed-point evaluations on the Hasselblad example), but that is not a comparison with L-BFGS. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same MLE only if it converges to the same local optimum. The likelihood is nonconcave, so the A/B must check this. SQUAREM loses strict monotonicity unless safeguarded. EM crawls near the π→0 / θ→∞ boundaries (the catalog already notes this), so it does not remove the boundary rescues. fastglm is GPL (≥2), compatible with GPL-3 if code were borrowed. This is new software evidence for an option the catalog already lists (EM + SQUAREM), not an overturned rejection.
- **Suggested action:** Record fastglm_zi (0.1.4, SQUAREM-EM in C++) in em_algorithm_zero_inflated_mixtures.md as a ready-made GPL comparator for the TODO-5 head-to-head (EM start vs conditional-likelihood start vs current). Benchmark at n=50–2000, p≤10 before any change. No code change now.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_zinb.cpp::fast_zinb_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_zero_augmented_poisson.cpp::fast_zap_internal`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastglm https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/news/news.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/count-firth-fastglm.html https://arxiv.org/pdf/1810.11163

### Batch C.1: Gauss–Hermite quadrature + L-BFGS → Two published ways to do AGQ that address those objections. (a) Frozen-mode AGQ, as in GLMMadaptive: the modes and curvatures of the random effects are located only every few iterations (update_GH_every, default 10 in the EM phase; once per outer quasi-Newton iteration), so the inner objective is a fixed-node rule with EDI's existing cheap exact gradient. (b) An exact AGQ gradient by implicit differentiation of the mode condition, reusing the Cholesky factor from the mode solve (one forward/back substitution) and warm-starting each group's mode from the previous outer iteration (Stringer, arXiv 2310.01589v2).
- **Current:** Fixed, non-adaptive 20-node Gauss–Hermite (60 nodes for the Poisson GLMM since f15b4423) with an exact fixed-node gradient and L-BFGS. The catalog (§4 T1 and new-TODO 5) discounts adaptive GH (TODO-153) to about 1.2–1.6× for clusters of ≤2. Its reasons: a 1-D Newton mode solve per group on every line-search evaluation, and an exact gradient that must differentiate through û_g(θ).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified against fixed-node GH. Stringer reports about 2–4× over finite-difference-gradient (GLMMadaptive) and derivative-free (lme4) AGQ, at m=1000 groups of size n=5. GLMMadaptive's page gives the rationale for refreshing modes only every few iterations (lower computational cost) but no number. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** AGQ approximates the same marginal likelihood differently from non-adaptive GH-20, so results move (toward the exact MLE) and are not bitwise identical; it needs the A/B harness. Frozen modes give a piecewise objective: a final mode refresh and re-convergence is required, and the line search must not cross a refresh. Stringer's smallest simulated group size is 3; KK pairs (size 2) and singletons are not benchmarked. Determinism is unaffected, since per-group mode solves are independent. The main expected gain is still accuracy at large σ, plus fewer nodes than the 60 now needed for Poisson. Only papers are involved, so there is no license issue (EDI already uses LBFGSpp).
- **Suggested action:** Amend TODO-153 (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md) so it names fast_weibull_frailty.cpp and fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp, and records these two implementation routes: frozen-mode AGQ with the fixed-node gradient, or the implicit-differentiation exact gradient. Then revisit the catalog's 1.2–1.6× estimate with a perf A/B at EDI sizes (pairs of 2, n≈50–500, σ up to e^1.5).
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::fast_clogit_plus_glmm_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_KK_cond_logit_glmm_abstract.R::shared`, `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_KK_combined.R::InferencePropKKGLMM`, `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_KK_combined.R::simulate_under_lik_null`, `R/EDI/src/fast_weibull_frailty.cpp::WeibullFrailtyNormalLikelihood`, `R/EDI/src/fast_weibull_frailty.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_wf`, `R/EDI/R/helper_survival_fits.R::.fit_weibull_frailty_rcpp`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01589 https://arxiv.org/html/2310.01589v2 https://drizopoulos.github.io/GLMMadaptive/articles/Optimization.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=GLMMadaptive

### Batch C.1: adaptive Gauss–Hermite quadrature (ordinal::clmm) → Add row (or group) weights to the native _glmm_engine.h GLMMObjective and fast_ordinal_glmm objective, and use them for the weighted replicates. For pair-level bootstrap weights that are constant within a group, this is w_g times the group's log-integral and its gradient. The replicate would then use the same estimator family as the point estimate (non-adaptive GH-20, the same links, EDI's cloglog convention without the TODO-31 link-swap probe).
- **Current:** Each Bayesian-bootstrap replicate of KKCLMM (4 links) and KKGLMM calls ordinal::clmm(y ~ X + (1|group), weights, nAGQ=20) in R. Each call goes through the R formula/model-frame path; NEWS: C kernels since 2010, nlminb optimizer since 2013. On failure it runs a clm fit plus a 5-start clmm multistart, and when vcov is not finite it refits at nAGQ ∈ {7, 11, 15, 1}: up to 10 clmm fits per replicate. The point estimate itself comes from EDI's native C++ GH kernel (fast_ordinal_clmm_cpp / fast_ordinal_glmm_cpp), which takes no row weights.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The expected gain comes from removing R model-frame and formula overhead and the multistart/refit ladder per replicate, inside a B-loop. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The bootstrap estimate changes from clmm's AGQ-20 to EDI's non-adaptive GH-20. That arguably fixes a mismatch with the point estimator, but results move and the change needs the A/B harness. The weight semantics must be decided: weighting each group's integrated log-likelihood is not the same as weighting observation log-densities inside the integral, and clmm's exact semantic was not confirmed this run (the manual PDF could not be read). The current nAGQ=1 (Laplace) variance fallback is itself an estimator change that would go away. The fallback ladder's robustness would need native equivalents (the CLMM kernel is single-start).
- **Suggested action:** Investigate: time one KKCLMM Bayesian-bootstrap replicate (clmm path) against an unweighted fast_ordinal_clmm_cpp fit at EDI sizes to bound the gain. Confirm clmm's weight semantics. If the gain is large, plan a weighted native kernel with an A/B against the clmm path. Also update the stale inventory row for compute_weighted_clmm_estimate, which is no longer a fixed-effects surrogate.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_ordinal_mixed_fit.R::weighted_ordinal_random_intercept_fit`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_KK_clmm_abstract.R::compute_weighted_clmm_estimate`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/NEWS https://cran.r-project.org/package=ordinal

### Batch C.2: non-adaptive Gauss–Hermite quadrature → Adaptive GH with an exact gradient of the AQ log-marginal likelihood (Stringer, arXiv 2310.01589 v2, 2024-11-11). It differentiates the mode implicitly (implicit function theorem), reusing the inner Newton's Cholesky factor (one forward and one backward solve, ∂û never formed), plus closed-form third derivatives for the Bernoulli case, and feeds the result to quasi-Newton L-BFGS via LBFGSpp, the same family EDI already uses. The paper covers a single grouping factor, which matches EDI's pair/singleton random intercept.
- **Current:** Fixed, non-adaptive GH (n_gh = 20; 60 for the Poisson GLMM since 2026-10-24) with an exact analytic gradient and L-BFGS/Newton. The catalog (T1) priced adaptive GH (v1.0.5 TODO-153) at about 1.2–1.6× and called the exact gradient through û_g(θ) the main obstacle, noting that lme4 avoids it with derivative-free BOBYQA.
- **Claimed speedup:** The paper benchmarks against GLMMadaptive (finite-difference gradients) and lme4 (BOBYQA), not against fixed-node GH. Sec. 4.3.2 / Fig. 2: typically 2–4× faster at m=1000 groups, n=5 per group. Toenail data (Fig. 4d): 2.5–10× vs GLMMadaptive and up to 7× vs lme4. Against EDI's fixed 20 nodes the gain is not quantified: the paper finds about 7–17 adaptive points are needed for stable inference at n=3–9 per group (App. C.1, Sec. 5), so the node saving over 20 is small. Over the 60-node Poisson default it is larger, roughly 3–8× fewer nodes, minus the per-group mode solve. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This approximates the same marginal-likelihood MLE with a different quadrature, so estimates match only to quadrature tolerance, not bitwise. It needs an A/B against a high-K reference, as the catalog already says. EDI's clusters are m_g ≤ 2, smaller than the paper's n ≥ 3; the paper says small clusters are where quadrature is least accurate and k must be larger, which shrinks the speed case for the 20-node kernels. The Poisson, ordinal, Weibull-frailty and CLMM kernels each need model-specific third derivatives. The new evidence changes TODO-153's feasibility (an exact gradient with L-BFGS is now a published, tested recipe), not its verdict. Deterministic, with no RNG involved. The paper's code license is not stated; the algorithm itself can be reimplemented, and LBFGSpp is MIT.
- **Suggested action:** Add Stringer 2024 (arXiv 2310.01589) to v1.0.5 TODO-153 and catalog T1 as the exact-gradient route for AGQ with L-BFGS, replacing the 'needs dû/dθ, lme4 sidesteps with BOBYQA' obstacle. Lower the expected node saving for the 20-node kernels (about 7–17 AGQ points are needed at small clusters), and prioritise the Poisson GLMM (60 fixed nodes) and the K=7 hurdle kernel (accuracy) as the first AGQ targets.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::GLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::value`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::LogisticGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::value`, `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::PoissonGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/fast_ordinal_glmm.cpp::OrdinalGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::neg_glmm`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_combined.R::shared_rcpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_KK_clmm_abstract.R::shared_rcpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_GLMM_weibull_frailty_normal.R::shared`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01589 https://arxiv.org/html/2310.01589v2

### Batch C.2: non-adaptive Gauss–Hermite quadrature (K=7) → AGQ (K≈5–7, centred and scaled at the per-pair conditional mode) with the exact implicit-differentiation gradient from Stringer 2024, keeping L-BFGS.
- **Current:** Own non-adaptive K=7 GH (default n_gh=7, never overridden by the R caller), outside TODO-153's scope. Catalog item 5: Investigate the accuracy, then Prototype AGQ.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this model. The win is accuracy at about the same cost; the paper's timings (2–4× vs GLMMadaptive/lme4, Fig. 2) are for Bernoulli models and do not transfer. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Changes results to quadrature tolerance and needs model-specific third derivatives for the zero-truncated Poisson. Clusters are m ≤ 2 after zeros are dropped. Best done by porting this objective onto _glmm_engine.h, as catalog item 5 already proposes.
- **Suggested action:** When v1.0.5 catalog item 5 (hurdle K=7 accuracy) is picked up, cite arXiv 2310.01589 as the gradient method so the AGQ prototype can keep the analytic-gradient L-BFGS path instead of falling back to finite differences.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp::HurdlePoissonGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_cond_poisson.R::fit_hurdle_for_matched_pairs_rcpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_cond_poisson.R::shared`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2310.01589v2

### Batch D.1: bracketing + bisection (Monte Carlo test inversion) → Keep the fixed-sample (CRN) bisection, made conservative as in Glazer & Stark: p = (1+#{T≥t})/(1+N), endpoint returned on the rejecting side. This argues against prioritizing the Garthwaite–Buckland driver; it is not a new algorithm to adopt.
- **Current:** Doubling bracket plus bisection on a common-random-number p(δ). Row D's verdict is Prototype for Garthwaite–Buckland (Robbins–Monro) as a faster replacement driver (garthwaite_buckland_ci_search.md), and the catalog lists it at ~3–20× (unmeasured).
- **Claimed speedup:** Glazer & Stark, lizard data (n=30, N=6000), 100 runs: the new method took 0.33 s versus 0.47 s for Garthwaite's algorithm (≈1.4× in favour of fixed-sample bisection). Garthwaite's interval was approximate, [−0.29, 11.0]; the new one was conservative, [−0.20, 10.875]. Caveat: their per-δ evaluation used the mean-difference shift shortcut. For non-affine statistics, each bisection step costs N refits while each RM step costs one, so the comparison has not been measured there. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a re-look at the GB Prototype, not a speedup to adopt. The source reports that Robbins–Monro intervals are approximate, not conservative. It also reports that they are sensitive to the starting point, and that O'Gorman (2014) found the two Garthwaite variants giving very different bounds and recommended averaging 8 runs. That erodes the claimed 3–20× and loses the determinism and conservativeness that the current CRN bisection already has. GB may still win in refit count for expensive non-affine statistics (e.g. the param-boot LR); that is where an A/B should focus, if anywhere.
- **Suggested action:** Re-check the premise of garthwaite_buckland_ci_search.md (v1.2.0-TODO-21) against Glazer & Stark 2025. Either narrow its scope to non-affine, refit-heavy statistics (e.g. the parametric-bootstrap LR) or downgrade it. Record that Robbins–Monro gives up conservativeness and start-independence.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_ci.R::compute_ci_by_inverting_the_randomization_test_iteratively`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::compute_rand_bootstrap_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::invert_rand_bootstrap_test_bisection`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::expand_rand_bootstrap_bound`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_param_boot.R::compute_lik_ratio_bootstrap_confidence_interval`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2405.05238v3 https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05238 https://cran.r-project.org/package=permutest

### Batch D.1: bisection (Monte Carlo test inversion) → Glazer–Stark fixed-sample inversion. Draw one sign-flip and permutation matrix per CI search and reuse it for every δ, with the conservative p = (1+#)/(1+N). Their Theorem 1 then gives coverage ≥ 1−α, and bisection runs on a deterministic function. The catalog's TODO-6a (common random numbers) is the same idea; this adds a published validity proof and a GPL-3 reference implementation.
- **Current:** Each p(δ) evaluation draws fresh sign flips or permutations (sample_int_replace_cpp inside compute_rand_pval_matched_pairs; RNG seeded once per CI call, not per δ). Bisection then runs to a δ-width ≤ min(pval_epsilon, 1e-6), up to 100 iterations; the inventory row still says ≤50 steps with |p−α|<pval_epsilon, which is stale. The p-value is mean(|T_b|≥|T_obs|), with no +1.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for quantile regression. The evaluation count per bound stays about the same, but bisecting a noisy p(δ) to a width of 1e-6 spends many of the ~2×nsim_rand-rq-fit evaluations resolving Monte Carlo noise. On a fixed sample, those evaluations converge to a single breakpoint and can stop once the bracket holds one jump. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The Monte Carlo realization changes, so results differ by Monte Carlo noise. Adding +1 to the p-value makes the interval slightly wider (more conservative). That second change is an estimator change, and only in EDI's favour for validity; it needs an A/B. Determinism improves.
- **Suggested action:** Use Glazer & Stark 2025 as the supporting reference when prototyping catalog TODO-6a (common random numbers for the Zhang quantile CI). Also fix the inventory row's stale stopping-rule text (the code now uses a 1e-6 width and 100 iterations).
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_quantile_rand_ci.R::ci_exact_zhang_combined`, `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_bisect_ci_boundary`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_quantile_rand_ci.R::is_a_quantile_rand_ci`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2405.05238v3 https://cran.r-project.org/package=permutest

### Batch D.1: bracketing + bisection (Monte Carlo test inversion) → Xu (Dec 2025) inverts exactly with rational-function statistics. Each studentized draw is a ratio of an affine numerator to a null-dependent SE, so it is a rational (square-root-of-quadratic) function of δ. Crossings with the observed pivot are roots of a low-degree polynomial per draw. Sorting and sweeping them gives the exact step-function p-value curve, with no bisection.
- **Current:** The closed-form breakpoint path runs only for type == 'percentile' (inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R:176). The 'studentized' and 'symmetric-percentile-t' BRT types on affine classes (mean diff, OLS, Lin) fall back to a doubling bracket plus bisection: 10–20 evaluations, each ≤ B refits.
- **Claimed speedup:** Xu claims 'an order of magnitude' over grid search, not measured; the paper reports no timings of its own. Cost would be O(B·n·p²) for the per-draw coefficients plus O(B log B) for the sweep, against 10–20 evaluations × B refits now. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Each class needs a derivation of how its per-draw SE depends on δ (homoskedastic versus HC SEs give different polynomials). Root-finding must handle denominators equal to zero, and the absolute value in the two-sided case. Gains appear only if the studentized BRT types are actually used. Xu releases no code, so it would be implemented from the paper.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look: check whether the studentized BRT pivots on OLS, mean-diff and Lin are rational in δ. If they are, consider extending closed_form_ci_from_affine_null_draws to rational breakpoints (Xu 2025, Algorithm 2) as a Prototype.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::compute_rand_bootstrap_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::closed_form_ci_from_affine_null_draws`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2512.14024v1

### Batch E.1: Monte Carlo permutation p-value → Anytime-valid sequential Monte Carlo testing by betting (Fischer & Ramdas, JRSS-B 2025, arXiv 2401.07365). Test martingales bet on whether each permuted statistic is ≥ or ≤ the observed one. The p-process Q_t = 1/sup_{s≤t} W_s is a valid p-value at any stopping time, including a budget cap. The binomial and binomial-mixture strategies stop early for rejection as well as futility. The framework contains Besag–Clifford as a special case. The formulas are closed form, so no external code is needed.
- **Current:** Fixed r (default 501) draws for every standalone randomization p-value, generated up front as an n×r matrix. Sequential Clopper–Pearson stopping runs only inside CI searches. The planned alternative is Besag–Clifford (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-156); the catalog (§7 P4) claims it gives '5–10× fewer draws when the result is clearly significant or clearly null'.
- **Claimed speedup:** Section 7.4 of https://arxiv.org/html/2401.07365v5 (Fisher sharp-null example, 53 units, p = 0.026, α = 0.05): binomial strategy mean 85 permutations (median 53), binomial mixture mean 147 (median 97), Besag–Clifford h = 10 mean 200, all with essentially full rejection. Section 7.2: power similar to Besag–Clifford with 'substantially fewer' permutations, especially under strong alternatives, where Besag–Clifford saves nothing because it stops early only for futility. Smaller savings and some power loss at α = 0.01. Against EDI's fixed 501, that is roughly 3–6× fewer draws in that example, on the paper's numbers; this is not an EDI measurement. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This changes the reported p-value: Q_τ = 1/max wealth instead of 2·min(ge,le)/r, so it is an estimator change and opt-in only. The efficient strategies are tuned to α (binomial p and c < α), so the p-value is mainly useful near the chosen α. Stochastic rounding, which restores power, is randomized and the paper advises using it only in automated pipelines. Exactness is kept as finite-sample super-uniformity, not an exact MC estimate. Draws are made in serial index order from per-index seeds, so the stopping time is thread-independent if the stopping check stays in that order. It needs streamed batch generation instead of one n×r matrix, the same plumbing TODO-156 needs. It also bears on the catalog's Besag–Clifford claim: the paper says Besag–Clifford does not stop early for rejection, so the '5–10× when clearly significant' estimate in §7 P4 / TODO-156 looks overstated for that direction.
- **Suggested action:** When TODO-156 (Besag–Clifford) is designed, add the Fischer–Ramdas binomial / binomial-mixture betting test as a compared option for the standalone p-value. Reuse the betting machinery v1.2.0-TODO-34 already plans for the CI search. Correct the catalog §7 P4 claim that Besag–Clifford saves draws when the result is clearly significant. Keep fixed r as the default; ship any sequential rule as opt-in after the A/B correctness check.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::compute_rand_two_sided_pval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::rand_pval_from_tail_counts`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.07365 https://arxiv.org/html/2401.07365v5

### Batch E.2: sequential Monte Carlo randomization p-value → Fischer & Ramdas 'Sequential Monte-Carlo testing by betting': an anytime-valid test martingale on the indicators 1[T_b ≥ T_obs]. The binomial strategy has p = 1/⌈√(2πe^{1/6})/α⌉, and the binomial mixture has closed-form wealth (1 − Bin(ℓ; t+1, c))/c with c < α. Stop when wealth ≥ 1/α (reject) or < α (futility). The p-process 1/max-wealth is valid at any stopping time.
- **Current:** Draws come in batches of max(25, 2√r) (default r=501). EDI stops once a per-look 99% Clopper–Pearson band for the running two-sided p-value excludes α/2 (compute_two_sided_pval_with_sequential_mc plus InferenceExtSequentialMCPval). Error across looks is not controlled, and the reported p-value comes from the drawn prefix. For the GEE family each draw is a full GEE refit.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not benchmarked against EDI's CP-band rule. Section 7.4 of https://arxiv.org/html/2401.07365v5 (Fisher sharp null, α=0.05, 1000 runs) reports mean/median permutations of 85/53 (binomial) and 147/97 (binomial mixture), against 200 (Besag–Clifford h=10, which failed to reject 3 times) and a fixed T=1000. Section 7 text says the method stops earlier than Besag–Clifford under both null and alternative with 'quite similar' power. At EDI's default r=501 this suggests on the order of 2–5x fewer GEE refits than a fixed r when the result is clearly significant (inferred, not measured). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The reported p-value changes: 1/max-wealth is not of the form (b+1)/(r+1), and it is conservative relative to the fixed-r MC p-value. Alternatively, EDI could keep the betting rule only as the stopping criterion and report the prefix MC p-value, but then the anytime-validity guarantee applies only to the decision, not to that p-value. It is decision-oriented, tied to α, as Gandy 2009 is in the catalog. The catalog rejected Gandy's method for 'more bookkeeping; boundary must be computed', but the binomial mixture boundary is a closed-form binomial CDF, which removes that objection. Its gain over the current rule is that it controls resampling error across looks, which the per-look band lacks. It is deterministic given the same draw sequence (draws are per index, so thread count does not matter), and implementing it needs no code dependency.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look under performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-156 and catalog NEW-seq-mc-stopping. Compare the binomial-mixture betting rule with the current CP-band rule on draws used and decision agreement at r=501, α=0.05, in the GEE and other refit-per-draw classes. Decide whether to report the betting p-value or use it only as a stopping rule.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_mixin_kk_gee_shared.R::compute_rand_two_sided_pval`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2401.07365v5 https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.01675v3

### Batch E.2: within-pair sign-flip randomization draws → Koning & Hemerik (Biometrika 111(2):441–458, 2024): replace the random sample of sign-flips with a deterministic, well-designed subgroup of the sign-flip group. The test stays exact because invariance under the group implies invariance under any subgroup, and it is fully replicable.
- **Current:** r independent Monte Carlo draws, each with one MT uniform per pair (coin flip) and one per reservoir unit (Bernoulli). The p-value is (b+1)/(r+1)-style, plus exact enumeration for small m planned (catalog §7 TODO-6).
- **Claimed speedup:** arXiv v1 abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.00967v1): in simulations the subgroup test matches the power of a random-sample test with 'about half the number of transformations'. The paper gives no runtimes, and the result is not shown at EDI's pair counts. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** It is an estimator/reference-set change, not a speedup of the same computation, so p-values differ from the current MC draws. It applies only to the sign-flip symmetric part: pairs with prob_T = 1/2, and reservoir units only when Bernoulli(1/2). Other prob_T values, the unpaired singleton branch, and the KK sequential designs that build the pairs are not group-invariant in the same way. The gain is power per draw, which matters where each draw is a model refit, and it is deterministic with no RNG. Any seed-pinned tests would shift.
- **Suggested action:** Note as an opt-in alternative next to catalog §7 TODO-6 (exact enumeration for small m). It could fill the gap for moderate m, where 2^m is too large to enumerate but refit-per-draw tests make r expensive. Do not change the default.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/generate_permutations.cpp::generate_permutations_matching_internal`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.00967v1 https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:oup:biomet:v:111:y:2024:i:2:p:441-458.

### Batch F.1: Fortet/McCormick linearization + branch and bound → Add HiGHS as a free MILP backend for the same linearized models (a 'highs' option in EDI_OPTIMAL_ROI_SOLVERS via ROI.plugin.highs, or the `highs` CRAN package directly). HiGHS is MIT-licensed and actively developed (highs 1.15.1-2 on CRAN, 2026-11-02). On Mittelmann's MIPLIB2017 benchmark (28 Oct 2026, 240 instances) HiGHS-1.15 solves 158–179 instances, which puts it in the same tier as SCIP (136). GLPK is no longer included in that benchmark. HiGHS does not solve MIQP, so the low-rank MIQP option in catalog table (a) still needs a commercial solver.
- **Current:** Product-linearized MILP solved by GLPK through ompr/ROI. EDI_OPTIMAL_ROI_SOLVERS = glpk/gurobi/cplex, so GLPK is the only free backend. The in-file benchmark shows a branch-and-bound cliff (n=20 3.5 s, n=25 311 s), which sets linearization_max_n = 20.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this problem. No fetched source benchmarks GLPK against HiGHS on MILP. Mittelmann (https://plato.asu.edu/ftp/milp.html) lists HiGHS at a shifted geometric mean of 763 s serial and 549 s parallel, but has no GLPK column. The JuMP forum view that 'there is very little reason to use GLPK over HiGHS' is an opinion with no numbers. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same model, same certified optimum, so the estimator is unchanged. When there are several optimal w, HiGHS may return a different one than GLPK, but deterministically, and the mirror coin already re-randomizes labels. It adds a Suggests dependency (GPL-2|3 R wrappers, MIT core). Whether HiGHS actually moves the n=20–25 cliff on these weak-relaxation dense BQP linearizations is unmeasured: one JuMP anecdote has GLPK beating HiGHS on a specific model. Enumeration (v1.0.5-TODO-1) probably dominates for n ≤ 30 anyway, so the main value is beyond the enumeration range and for the Dinkelbach subproblems. Parallel HiGHS must be run single-threaded, or checked for determinism.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look. Run the existing in-file benchmark (mahal_dist quadratic, p=5, n = 15/20/25, plus one Dinkelbach A-ratio case) with roi_solver = 'highs' against GLPK. If HiGHS clearly moves the cliff, add it to EDI_OPTIMAL_ROI_SOLVERS and make linearization_max_n depend on the solver (as the optimal_solve_auto catalog row already suggests). Record the result in catalog §12 table (a) as a new row.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_optimal_milp_solvers.R::milp_solve_quadratic`, `R/EDI/R/helper_optimal_milp_solvers.R::milp_solve_A_dinkelbach`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=highs https://cran.r-project.org/package=ROI.plugin.highs https://highs.dev/ https://plato.asu.edu/ftp/milp.html https://discourse.julialang.org/t/is-glpk-still-maintained-relevant/116950/4

### Batch F.2: MILP branch and bound (GLPK) → Add roi_solver = "highs" via ROI.plugin.highs, built on the CRAN package highs (HiGHS 1.15.x, GPL ≥ 2 wrapper of the MIT-licensed solver). It would be an open-source MIP backend and possibly a new default, with the linearization_max_n cutoff re-benchmarked per backend. The model formulation stays the same, so the certified optimum is the same.
- **Current:** GLPK branch and bound through ompr/ROI (default roi_solver = "glpk"; the only other choices are commercial gurobi and cplex). l1 always goes through the MILP at any n, and the quadratic and Dinkelbach MILPs have a GLPK cliff between n = 20 and 25.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for EDI's models. Mittelmann's MILP benchmark (28 Oct 2026, 240 MIPLIB 2017 instances): HiGHS has a scaled shifted geometric mean of 10.7 and solves 158, against SCIP at 14.0 and 136 (HiGHS parallel: 7.69 and 179). GLPK is not in that MILP table at all. GLPK appears only in Mittelmann's LP tables (INFORMS 2022 talk), whose summary slide says "HiGHS is strong in MILP". Anecdotal reports go both ways: GLPK was faster on some small IPs. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same optimum, but when several allocations tie for the optimum a different solver may return a different one. The mirror coin re-randomizes only the label, so the allocation distribution under ties could change, and the existing brute-force exactness tests must hold. Determinism of HiGHS MIP across thread counts needs checking; pin threads = 1 if needed. MILP support through the ROI plugin: the README states MIP support, but no binary-variable example was found, so verify that ompr.roi passes types = "B" through. It adds a Suggests dependency. It does not fix the weak McCormick LP bound, so gains on the quadratic kind may be modest, and TODO-1 enumeration probably dominates for n ≤ 30 anyway. The l1 kind (MILP at any n) is where a stronger open-source backend matters most.
- **Suggested action:** Add "highs" to EDI_OPTIMAL_ROI_SOLVERS behind a requireNamespace check. Benchmark GLPK vs HiGHS on milp_solve_l1 (n = 20, 50, 100, 200; p = 5) and on milp_solve_quadratic (n = 15, 20, 25), checking that objectives match. Switch the default and re-tune linearization_max_n only if HiGHS wins at these sizes.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_optimal_milp_solvers.R::milp_solve_l1`, `R/EDI/R/helper_optimal_annealing.R::optimal_solve_auto`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=ROI.plugin.highs https://cran.r-project.org/package=highs https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ROI.plugin.highs/readme/README.html https://plato.asu.edu/ftp/milp.html https://plato.asu.edu/talks/informs2022.pdf

### Batch H.2: Pocock–Simon minimization (sequential simulation) → SIGA (stratum-imbalance Gaussian approximation; SIGA-R calibrates to the conditional randomization distribution of a fixed-score test) in place of nested re-randomization
- **Current:** Exact Monte Carlo re-randomization: B independent replays of the minimization rule, O(B·n·J)
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The abstract says only 'substantially reduced computation relative to nested rerandomization'. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an estimator change, not a speedup. It approximates the randomization reference distribution with a Gaussian and drops exactness/MC-consistency. The paper reports 'close agreement', not equivalence. It targets fixed-score statistics and power evaluation, not general EDI inference. No code is linked, so there is no license to check. EDI's per-draw cost is already optimal.
- **Suggested action:** No change to the exact draw path. At most, note it in the catalog §7/§8 options table as a rejected estimator-changing approximation (not exact). Reconsider only if EDI adds a fast power-calculation helper for minimization designs.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/generate_permutations.cpp::generate_permutations_pocock_simon_internal`, `R/EDI/src/pocock_simon_assign.cpp::pocock_simon_redraw_w_internal`, `R/EDI/src/generate_permutations.cpp::generate_permutations_pocock_simon_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05527 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05527

### Batch I.1: rerandomization (acceptance–rejection sampling) → Early abandoning (partial-distance elimination). Store M row-major and accumulate d_j² (or |d_j|) one row at a time. Reject as soon as the running sum exceeds the cutoff. Both objectives are sums of nonnegative per-component terms, so the decision cannot change except by floating-point rounding exactly at the cutoff. Because M is whitened, under complete randomization the components are approximately iid N(0,1) (Morgan & Rubin 2012). Most rejected candidates should therefore exit after a few of the p rows when p_acc is small.
- **Current:** Each candidate computes the full d = M·(2w−1) (p×n GEMV, O(np)), then squaredNorm / lpNorm<1>, then compares with the cutoff.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source for this problem. arXiv 2102.05221 reports only 'substantial speedup' from early abandoning in threshold-based search, for elastic distances. The Fisher–Yates O(n) part of each draw is not reduced, so the gain is bounded by the share of time spent in the O(np) check. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Gains appear only with a finite, tight cutoff and moderate p (≳5). With cutoff = Inf or loose cutoffs it does nothing. Row-wise accumulation changes the rounding versus Eigen's GEMV + squaredNorm, so it is tolerance-equal, not bit-identical: accept/reject can flip only at the cutoff exactly. Same as the catalog P6 '2Σ_T M_i' option, which can be combined with this. It does not change the RNG stream or determinism.
- **Suggested action:** Add as a row in catalog §P6. If it gets prototyped, benchmark at n=100–1000, p=5–20, p_acc=1e-2–1e-4 against the current kernel before adopting.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/rerandomization_helpers.cpp::rerandomization_search_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.05221

### Batch I.1: rejection sampling (rerandomization) → PSRSRR (Lu, Zhang & Zhu, arXiv 2602.07613, Feb 2026). It runs a pair-switching Metropolis–Hastings chain, checks the threshold along the way, and adds an 'inverse back' correction aimed at a uniform law on {w : M(w) ≤ a}. The idealized Algorithm 2 is exactly uniform (Thm 3.2) but far too slow (Table 7: 37,208 s at n=250, p=2). The practical Algorithm 3 is only asymptotically justified and is checked by KS tests.
- **Current:** Plain i.i.d. acceptance–rejection, capped at max(1000r, 1e5) draws. It errors at very small p_acc. The catalog P6 rejects MCMC samplers because their draws are not i.i.d. uniform on the acceptable set.
- **Claimed speedup:** From arXiv 2602.07613: 10–10,000× in simulations, and over 1,800× median (Fig. 2) for 100 assignments at p_a = 1e-3 or 1e-5. Reserpine (n=30): 0.47 s vs 22.70 s (~48×). STAR (n≈974, 5 covariates): 2.95 s vs 193.65 s (~66×, Table 1). Simulations cover n=50–3000 and p=2–25, which overlaps EDI's sizes. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a design-law change, not a speedup. Draws are only asymptotically uniform, are not shown to be independent, and the paper reports no Fisher randomization test validity. EDI's exact randomization tests rely on i.i.d. uniform draws from the acceptance region, so the catalog's MCMC rejection still holds for the default sampler. No code or license is given. It would be useful only as an opt-in sampler for tiny p_acc, where the current code errors on max_draws.
- **Suggested action:** Keep the default sampler. Add arXiv 2602.07613 to catalog P6's MCMC row, noting the asymptotic-only uniformity and the absence of FRT validity results. Also correct the v2.0.0 rerandomization_criterion_variants.md §B wording ('MR theory intact' / 'uniform law'): per 2602.07613, PSRR is not uniform, and PSRSRR is uniform only asymptotically.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/rerandomization_helpers.cpp::rerandomization_search_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_rerandomization.R::generate_rerandomized_w_batch`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_rerandomization.R::generate_one_rerandomized_w`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2602.07613v1 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2103.13051 https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.17230

### Batch J.2: i.i.d. bootstrap resampling (Lemire draws) → Batched ranged random integer generation: several unbiased bounded integers from one random word, typically one multiplication and no division per value. Reference code is MIT-licensed (github.com/lemire/batched_random), which is GPL-3 compatible.
- **Current:** One Lemire multiply-shift draw (bounded_rand) per index, one 32-bit MT19937 word each.
- **Claimed speedup:** arXiv 2408.06213v2 experiments: shuffles 1.4-2.5x with fast 64-bit generators (Lehmer/PCG), 2.6-4.7x with ChaCha, on in-cache arrays. Not benchmarked for repeated draws from one fixed range, nor with 32-bit MT19937. Not quantified at EDI sizes. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same distribution, but the realized draws change, so seed-pinned tests need refreshing. With 32-bit words only two indices fit per word for n < 65536. End-to-end gain is likely negligible, since per-replicate refits and per-call MT seeding (624 words) dominate index generation at n in the hundreds. Low priority; only worth it if TODO-149 profiling shows RNG time is material.
- **Suggested action:** No action unless RNG/index time is shown to matter in TODO-149 profiling. If it does, prototype a batched bounded_rand for sample_int_replace_internal and resample_group_rows_internal, and fold it into the same stream-change window as catalog §7 TODO-2/TODO-3.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_sample_int.cpp::sample_int_replace_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_sample_int.cpp::sample_int_replace_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/bootstrap_indices.cpp::bootstrap_indices_internal`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06213 https://arxiv.org/html/2408.06213v2 https://github.com/lemire/batched_random

### Batch J.3: nonparametric bootstrap → The cheap bootstrap (Lam 2022): ψ̂ ± t_{B,1−α/2}·S with S² = (1/B)Σ(ψ*_b − ψ̂)². It gives asymptotically exact coverage with B as small as 1 and is typically used at B ≈ 5–10. It would be offered as a new opt-in CI type for expensive refits (GLMM, survival, ordinal).
- **Current:** B = 501 (default) i.i.d. resamples, each refit, followed by percentile, basic or other quantile-based CIs.
- **Claimed speedup:** Refits drop from 501 to about 10 (~50x fewer); this is arithmetic, not a measured benchmark.
