Coverage comes from the ar5iv HTML of arXiv 2202.00090 (0.6-quantile of Exp(1), n = 100, 95% nominal, 1,000 reps): coverage 0.92 at B = 5 and B = 10 with mean widths 0.63 and 0.53, against percentile-bootstrap coverage 0.69 and 0.80 at the same B. At B = 50 the cheap interval has width 0.50 and coverage 0.94. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a different interval, not a speedup of the current one. It is wider at small B (about 6% wider than at B = 50 when B = 10, per the paper) and symmetric, so it loses percentile/BCa transformation-respecting behaviour and asymmetry. Validity rests on asymptotic normality of the estimator. It does not give a bootstrap distribution for p-values or BCa. It would have to be an opt-in CI type, never a default replacement. It would need an A/B coverage check on EDI fixtures. It is deterministic under the existing per-draw seeding.
- **Suggested action:** Consider adding an opt-in cheap-bootstrap CI type (B ≈ 10) for classes whose per-replicate refit is expensive. Document it as a distinct interval and validate its coverage against the B = 501 percentile and BCa intervals in the A/B harness.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_bootstrap_distribution_beta_hat_T`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::compute_reusable_bootstrap_worker_distribution`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.00090 https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2202.00090 https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.10974

### Batch J.3: nonparametric bootstrap (studentized) → The k-step bootstrap (Davidson & MacKinnon 1999; Andrews 2002, Econometrica 70:119–162): a fixed small number k of Newton (or line-search Newton) steps from the full-data estimate in each replicate. Andrews proves it is higher-order equivalent to the standard bootstrap for t-CIs (symmetric, equal-tailed, one-sided) and Wald tests, under the iid and block bootstraps. Batch A.1 raised this only for the Weibull kernel; this finding generalizes it to the shared bootstrap loop for smooth likelihood classes.
- **Current:** Each bootstrap replicate is refit by Newton/IRLS/L-BFGS, warm-started at the full-data MLE, to full convergence.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the abstract; Andrews calls it 'a very attractive alternative computationally'. Because EDI already warm-starts at the MLE, the gain is only the iterations beyond k plus convergence checks. It is likely modest (≈1–3x) for Newton/IRLS families and larger for L-BFGS families, but not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Each replicate's θ* differs from the fully converged MLE, so this is an estimator change unless k is iterated to tolerance. It must be opt-in and must not be applied silently (same conclusion as A.1). The theory needs smooth (concave or near-concave) criteria, so it does not suit nonconcave mixtures (row B), ranks, KM or IVWC compounds. Percentile/BCa quantiles are not explicitly covered by Andrews' results; t, Wald and J tests are. It is deterministic under the same seeds.
- **Suggested action:** Measure how many iterations warm-started bootstrap refits actually take on representative fixtures. If it is well above 2–3 for some families, consider an opt-in k-step mode (k = 2–3 Newton steps) for studentized/t-based bootstrap CIs in smooth GLM-type classes, validated in the A/B harness. Otherwise record it as no gain at EDI's warm-start setup.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_bootstrap_statistics_beta_hat_T`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::compute_reusable_bootstrap_worker_distribution`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_bootstrap_distribution_beta_hat_T`
- **Sources:** https://ideas.repec.org/a/ecm/emetrp/v70y2002i1p119-162.html

### Batch J.4: parametric bootstrap → k-step parametric bootstrap. Each bootstrap sample gets a fixed, small number k of Newton (or Gauss–Newton) steps from the original-sample estimate instead of a full refit. Offer it as an opt-in mode.
- **Current:** Each parametric-bootstrap replicate refits the full and null models to convergence, warm-started from the base MLE. That is 2B = 398 refits per δ, and about 8–10k refits per bootstrap-LR CI.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the sources. The Davidson & MacKinnon (1999) abstract says only that 'very few steps are needed', and fewer for LR tests and for Newton than quasi-Newton. The saving would be the iteration count beyond k per refit, which is modest because replicates are already warm-started. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The bootstrap statistic is a k-step approximation, so p-values and CIs differ from the full-refit bootstrap at finite n. Andrews (2002) proves higher-order equivalence only asymptotically. It needs specs that expose a Newton step with a Hessian (count families have C++ score/Hessian getters). It is risky for non-concave likelihoods, and determinism is unaffected. The gain is smaller than it looks because workers already warm-start at the MLE. Compare with the A.1 Weibull finding (2026-11-16), which applied the same idea to a different loop. Needs a coverage A/B before any use.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look: record the k-step bootstrap (Davidson–MacKinnon 1999; Andrews 2002) as an estimator-change option in the catalog §9 J options table. If it is pursued, prototype it as an opt-in for count-family LR bootstraps and measure the iteration counts the warm-started workers actually use first. If they already converge in 2–3 Newton steps, drop the idea.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_param_boot.R::run_param_bootstrap_replicates`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_param_boot.R::compute_lik_ratio_bootstrap_two_sided_pval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_param_bootstrap_estimate.R::run_param_bootstrap_estimate_replicates`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_poisson.R::simulate_under_lik_null`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_composite_likelihood.R::simulate_under_lik_null`
- **Sources:** https://ideas.repec.org/a/ier/iecrev/v40y1999i2p487-508.html https://ideas.repec.org/a/ecm/emetrp/v70y2002i1p119-162.html

### Batch J.4: parallel refit loop → Fast robust bootstrap (Salibian-Barrera & Zamar 2002). Keep the full-sample robustness weights and scale fixed, and compute each replicate as a single weighted least-squares solve on the resampled rows, with a linear correction. An R implementation exists in the CRAN package FRB (GPL ≥ 3).
- **Current:** OpenMP over B. Each replicate rebuilds X_b and does a full cold robust fit: OLS-QR start, MAD scale, IRLS for up to about 20 iterations, O(K n p²).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the abstract, which says each bootstrap sample needs only 'solving a linear system of equations'. In theory this is about K× fewer weighted solves than K-iteration IRLS. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** It approximates the bootstrap distribution of the robust estimator; it does not recompute it, so this is an estimator change. The paper shows it is asymptotically correct only with the auxiliary scale term. It is designed for i.i.d. bootstrap of S/MM estimators. For the randomization (permuted-w) part of the BRT loop, the full-sample weights are tied to the observed assignment, so validity as a randomization statistic is unclear. EDI's 'MM' is not Yohai's MM (catalog O8), so the derivation would have to be redone for fixed-MAD Huber/bisquare IRLS. Warm-starting (already Prototype TODO-8) keeps the estimator and should come first.
- **Suggested action:** Add FRB to the catalog O8 / J options table as a rejected or estimator-change option, so it is not rediscovered. Keep the warm-start Prototype (TODO-8) as the speedup that preserves the estimator. Revisit FRB only if a true MM (FAST-S) is adopted, because then cold refits would cost 50–200× more and FRB becomes the standard remedy.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_robust_regression.cpp::compute_robust_rand_bootstrap_parallel_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://projecteuclid.org/journals/annals-of-statistics/volume-30/issue-2/Bootrapping-robust-estimates-of-regression/10.1214/aos/1021379865.full https://cran.r-project.org/package=FRB

### Batch J.5: stratified bootstrap / subsampling → Rate-dependent sampling without replacement, as in dqrng: partial Fisher–Yates on a reusable scratch copy when k/n > 0.5, and rejection against a reusable bitset (std::vector<char> or bitset of size n) instead of an unordered_set at moderate rates such as PRW's b=n^0.7 (~20–40% at n=200–1000).
- **Current:** Per stratum, sample_group_units with replace=FALSE draws positions by rejection and checks duplicates in a std::unordered_set (the hash set is allocated per call). The catalog already proposes partial Fisher–Yates (TODO-2).
- **Claimed speedup:** From the CRAN dqrng vignette: bitset rejection is about 6.4x faster than R's hashset at a 1% rate (102.62 µs vs 657.81 µs, m=1e6, n=1e4), and partial Fisher–Yates is about 6.2x faster than base R at a 60% rate (10.3 ms vs 63.7 ms). These populations are far larger than EDI's, so the gain at n≈100–1000 is not quantified. End to end it is negligible, because B·fit(b) dominates. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The realized draws change but the distribution is the same, so seed-pinned tests need regenerating. Determinism holds (one RRng stream). This is a minor refinement to fold into the existing TODO-2, not a new priority.
- **Suggested action:** When implementing catalog TODO-2 (partial Fisher–Yates), also use a reusable bitset in place of the unordered_set for k/n ≤ 0.5. Bundle it with TODO-2 rather than tracking it separately.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/exchangeable_resampling_draws.cpp::exchangeable_resampling_draws_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dqrng/vignettes/sample.html

### Batch J.5: subsampling (Politis–Romano–Wolf) → Lam's cheap bootstrap, a t-type interval from very few resamples ('as low as one Monte Carlo replication'), with generalizations to 'a range of subsampling variants'.
- **Current:** Quantile-based CIs from B=501 (subsampling) or B (smoothed bootstrap) refits.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the fetched abstract. The saving comes from replacing ~500 refits with a handful of resamples. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an estimator change, not a speedup: it is a different interval (wider at very small B), and it is not the PRW or smoothed-percentile CI. It is only asymptotically valid. It could be offered as an opt-in CI type for expensive per-replicate fits, and must never silently replace an existing type.
- **Suggested action:** Note it in the catalog as an estimator-change option (opt-in 'cheap' CI type for expensive refits). No change to the existing algorithms.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_prw_subsampling.R::approximate_subsampling_distribution_beta_hat_T_impl`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_prw_subsampling.R::subsampling_centered_pivot`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::ci_smoothed_bootstrap`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.00090

### Batch K.1: GEE with local odds ratios (Fisher scoring) → Cache the estimated uniform LOR structure and pass it back as LORstr='fixed' with LORterm (built with multgee::matrixLOR from the cached fit). According to the CRAN reference manual, the structure is estimated by aggregating responses across clusters into marginalized contingency tables and fitting an RC-G(1) model; covariates do not enter. So the structure is the same whenever y and the cluster pairing (m) are unchanged, e.g. across ladder rungs, and across permutation draws at delta = 0 when the design does not redraw m.
- **Current:** Every call to multgee::ordLORgee(LORstr='uniform') re-estimates the marginalized local-odds-ratio structure, then runs Fisher scoring for beta. This includes every randomization-inference replicate (compute_treatment_estimate_during_randomization_inference) and every rung of the predictor-candidate ladder.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. It removes one gnm/IPFP association-model fit per ordLORgee call. The share of ordLORgee time this represents is unknown. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Exact only when the cached table's inputs are identical: same y, same m, same within-cluster ordering, same 'add'. It is invalid for bootstrap resamples, shifted-response CI inversion, and designs whose permutation draws supply a new m_mat column (inference_all_abstract_rand.R:1239-1245), so the cache key must include y and m. Equivalence of a 'fixed' LORterm round-trip with the internally estimated uniform structure must be verified numerically against the current fits; a rounding difference would move beta slightly. The source does not say whether the LOR step dominates runtime, so profile before adopting.
- **Suggested action:** Profile one ordLORgee call at typical KK sizes to measure the share of time spent in LOR estimation versus Fisher scoring. If it is material, prototype a (y, m)-keyed cache with LORstr='fixed'/LORterm, used in the randomization loop and across ladder rungs, and A/B it against the current fits for identical coefficients within tolerance.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_KK_combined.R::fit_ordinal_gee_mod`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/multgee/refman/multgee.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=multgee

### Batch M.1: bisection (test-inversion CI) → Chandrupatla's hybrid quadratic/bisection method (Chandrupatla 1997, Adv. Eng. Softw. 28:145–149; used by SciPy's elementwise find_root). Alternative: ITP (Oliveira & Takahashi 2021, ACM TOMS 47(1)) with n0=0 or 1, which guarantees no more than n0 evaluations beyond bisection's count. Use either in place of textbook Brent when implementing brent_ci_inversion.md TODO-2.
- **Current:** Wald-seeded bracket, then pure bisection to 1e-6 in δ. That is about 20–25 constrained refits per bound. The not-yet-implemented plan brent_ci_inversion.md would replace it with textbook Brent–Dekker, capped at max_bisect=60.
- **Claimed speedup:** From the evaluation-count table at https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html (15 test functions, tol 1e-15): bisection 574 total, Brent 260, ITP 303, Chandrupatla 178. Brent is worse than bisection on flat or near-multiple roots: x³ 40 vs 17, sin((x−7.14)³) 46 vs 18. Chandrupatla is never much worse than bisection: 17 and 18 on those two. The CRAN itp vignette has uniroot (Brent) at 65 iterations against itp's 35–36 on a repeated root, with parity elsewhere. Neither source benchmarks EDI's p(δ). The tie-in to EDI (the jump guard for isolated low-p null refits, and flat p near a ridge fallback) is my inference. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a bracketing method on the same bracket with the same tol, so the bound is within tol of bisection's whenever p(δ) has a single α-crossing in the bracket. ITP and Chandrupatla are slightly more code than Brent (~40–60 lines). ITP has tuning constants (κ1, κ2, n0). On smooth p, Brent and Chandrupatla are roughly tied, and Brent sometimes uses fewer evaluations (Warsaw example in the itp vignette). The benefit is mainly a bounded worst case: textbook Brent could exhaust the 60-evaluation cap on a flat or jumpy p(δ) and return a looser bound than bisection would. Deterministic. The algorithms are public and need no library, so licensing is not an issue (the itp R package is GPL ≥ 2 if reference code is wanted).
- **Suggested action:** Before implementing brent_ci_inversion.md TODO-2, re-look at the method choice: add Chandrupatla and ITP to the catalog's row-M options table, and consider Chandrupatla (or ITP with n0=1) as the Phase-2 solver. Keep bisection as the fallback. Add an evaluation-count test that includes a flat-near-root p(δ), to check that the new solver never needs many more evaluations than bisection.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/lrt_ci_newton.cpp::pval_invert_ci_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_ci_inversion.R::invert_test_pval_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_ci_inversion.R::invert_gradient_ci_uniroot`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_count_likelihood.R::compute_lik_ratio_confidence_interval_impl`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_hurdle.R::get_likelihood_test_spec`
- **Sources:** https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/itp/vignettes/itp-vignette.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/itp/index.html https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITP_method https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.optimize.elementwise.find_root.html

### Batch M.1: Brent root-finding test inversion → Bring this path under brent_ci_inversion.md (Brent, or Chandrupatla/ITP per the finding above). Correct the inventory row's algorithm and verdict.
- **Current:** The inventory says `uniroot` (Brent), verdict 'Keep (already Brent)'. The code actually calls private$invert_gradient_ci_uniroot (inference_incidence_log_binomial.R:392), which calls pval_invert_ci_cpp (inference_ext_ci_inversion.R:150-195): Wald seed, exponential bracket, then pure bisection to 1e-6, about 20 constrained refits per bound. Catalog I15 already notes that the name is a misnomer.
- **Claimed speedup:** Bisection 574 vs Brent 260 vs Chandrupatla 178 total function evaluations across 15 test functions (https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html). That is about 2–3x fewer evaluations on smooth functions. Not quantified for log-binomial gradient refits. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: high)
- **Trade-offs:** None beyond those of the main row-M plan. The bound is within tol (1e-6) of today's. The log-binomial gradient CI's fallback to LR Newton when one side is infeasible is unaffected.
- **Suggested action:** Fix the algorithm_inventory.csv row (algorithm → 'bisection (test-inversion CI)', verdict → Keep-tracked under brent_ci_inversion.md). Add the log-binomial gradient CI to that plan's test gate alongside score, gradient and Bartlett.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_log_binomial.R::compute_gradient_confidence_interval_impl`
- **Sources:** https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html

### Batch M.1: bisection test inversion → ITP with n0=0 (Oliveira & Takahashi 2021). It is guaranteed to need no more evaluations than bisection, and it is superlinear when the α-crossing lies inside a smooth piece. If the crossing is at a jump, it converges to the jump just as bisection does.
- **Current:** Outward doubling until p ≤ α, then up to 100 bisection steps to a width of 1e-6 (≈20+ evaluations from a bracket of width ≥ 1). The inventory and catalog justify Keep with 'p(δ) is a step function, so Brent degenerates to bisection'. That premise is inaccurate. binom_two_sided_pval and fisher_noncentral_two_sided_pval (zhang_exact_speedups.cpp:22,41) sum smooth dbinom / noncentral-hypergeometric masses over a minlike acceptance set that changes only at discrete δ, so p(δ) is piecewise smooth with jumps, not piecewise constant.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this problem. The itp vignette has the Staircase discontinuous function at parity with uniroot (31 vs 31) and smooth examples at about 8 iterations, at tol 1e-10. Expected roughly 20 → 8–10 evaluations per bound when the crossing is smooth, and never worse than bisection. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Each evaluation costs only O(d+S), so the wall-clock gain is small; the catalog notes fork overhead dominates. Minlike two-sided p(δ) need not be monotone (Fay 2010), so with several α-crossings in the bracket ITP could converge to a different crossing than bisection does. That would be a change of answer, not a speedup; check the bound against bisection on a grid of tables before adopting. Low priority.
- **Suggested action:** Correct the 'step function' rationale in the inventory and in catalog §6/§11 to 'piecewise smooth with jumps'. Optionally record ITP (n0=0) in the options table as a no-worse-than-bisection option, with the non-monotone-p caveat. Keep bisection unless a test grid shows identical bounds.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_bisect_ci_boundary`, `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_ci_exact_combined`, `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_compute_ci_bounds_parallel`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/itp/vignettes/itp-vignette.html https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITP_method

### Batch M.2: Miettinen–Nurminen score test; bisection inversion → Closed-form Farrington–Manning/Miettinen–Nurminen cubic for p̃_C in trigonometric form, clamping the acos argument to [-1,1] and p̃ to [0,1], as implemented in ratesci 1.1.1 scoreci.R (GPL >= 3). Optionally pair it with the already-cataloged Brent/secant on z(δ) − z_{1−α/2} for the outer root.
- **Current:** Constrained MLE p̃_C solved by a 60-step bisection on the score (1e15 boundary sentinels, |score| < 1e-9 absolute stop) for every δ. The CI is an outer ≤50-step bisection in δ with a p-space tolerance, so up to 2·50·60 score evaluations per CI.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. The catalog's theoretical estimate is ~30–60× fewer flops for the inner solve; absolute cost is already microseconds. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same restricted MLE, so the same estimate within tolerance. Risks: the boundary cases x=0, x=n and δ→±1 need special handling, and ratesci also rounds p̃ to 10 decimals, which EDI should not copy blindly. Gate this with a grid cross-check against the current bisection MLE. This is not new evidence overturning a rejection: it supports the existing Adopt (v1.0.5 audit TODO 3) with a maintained GPL-compatible reference implementation that handles the risks the catalog lists. The outer CI search in ratesci is still bisection (on the score statistic, not p), so it offers no faster outer-root reference.
- **Suggested action:** When implementing v1.0.5 audit TODO 3 / the brent_ci_inversion.md scope extension, use ratesci's scoreci.R RD-binomial branch (cubic coefficients a, b, c, d; clamped acos) as the reference. Cross-check against mn_constrained_mle_pc_cpp over an (x_t, n_t, x_c, n_c, δ) grid. Low priority because the absolute cost is tiny.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/miettinen_nurminen_speedups.cpp::mn_ci_internal`, `R/EDI/src/miettinen_nurminen_speedups.cpp::mn_pvalue_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/miettinen_nurminen_speedups.cpp::mn_ci_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_miettinen_nurminen_univ.R::compute_asymp_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_miettinen_nurminen_univ.R::compute_asymp_two_sided_pval`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=ratesci https://cran.r-project.org/src/contrib/ratesci_1.1.1.tar.gz

### Batch O.1: erfc-based normal CDF (clamped tails) → Use the scaled complementary error function erfcx from S. G. Johnson's Faddeeva package (MIT license). Its method is 100 Chebyshev polynomials on y=4/(4+x) for 0<=x<=50 and a continued fraction beyond 50. Then for x<0: Phi(x) = 0.5*exp(-x^2/2)*erfcx(-x/sqrt2), and log Phi(x) = -x^2/2 + log(0.5*erfcx(-x/sqrt2)). That gives near-machine-precision log Phi over the whole negative tail with no branch-boundary jump, and Phi down to underflow.
- **Current:** Phi(x) = 0.5*fast_erfc(-x/sqrt2), clamped to 6e-16 (log Phi = -35.0496) for x<=-8. The adopted fix (catalog TODO 2 / v1.0.5-TODO-22) plans a truncated asymptotic Mills-ratio series below -8, which the catalog estimates at about 1e-9 relative with 4 terms at x=-8.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The Faddeeva.cc header comment (Octave doxygen copy) says the erfcx routine 'seems to be substantially faster than either the SLATEC DERFC function' and Cody's CALERF (the basis of R's pnorm), at 'near machine precision'. Against the planned asymptotic series it is expected to cost about the same (one table lookup plus a polynomial), not to be faster. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an accuracy/implementation choice inside an already-adopted fix, not a speedup over the clamped code. It adds about 100 Chebyshev coefficient sets (a few hundred lines) to the core, but no R dependency, so it stays EDI_CORE_ONLY-portable, unlike calling R::pnorm5 in the tail. It removes the ~1e-9 accuracy at the series/erfc switch point and the boundary jump the catalog flags. MIT is GPL-3 compatible. The tail branch is rare, so overall speed impact is negligible either way. Zaghloul (2018) questions the accuracy of the MIT package's complex Faddeeva function in some regions; that concerns complex arguments, not real erfcx, but the gate grid vs R::pnorm(log.p=TRUE) on [-40,40] should still be run.
- **Suggested action:** When implementing v1.0.5-TODO-22, compare a real-argument erfcx tail (Faddeeva erfcx, MIT) against the planned 4-term asymptotic series. Use the TODO's existing gate (grid vs R::pnorm(log.p=TRUE), 1e-12 rel on [-40,40]) and pick whichever passes at equal cost. The series is unlikely to reach 1e-12 at x=-8, so erfcx (or a later switch point for the series) may be needed to meet the gate.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_erfc.h::pnorm_fast`, `R/EDI/src/fast_erfc.h::fast_log_pnorm`, `R/EDI/src/fast_math_utils.cpp::fast_log_pnorm_vec_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://docs.octave.org/doxygen/9/df/d5a/Faddeeva_8cc_source.html

### Batch O.2: series / Lentz continued fraction → Branch on integer or half-integer a = df/2. These use the exact finite sums DLMF 8.4.10, Q(n,x)=e^{-x}Σ_{k<n}x^k/k!, and DLMF 8.4.14, Q(n+½,z²)=erfc(z)+e^{-z²}/√π·Σ_{k=1}^{n} z^{2k-1}/(½)_k. For df=1 this is just fast_erfc(√(x/2)); for df=2 it is exp(−x/2). Keep the series/continued-fraction path for non-integer a, or for a≥30 as Boost does.
- **Current:** Q(df/2, x/2) uses a power series for x<a+1 (returned as 1−P) and otherwise a modified-Lentz continued fraction (up to 500 iterations, 1e-16 stop), each with an exp(−x + a·log x − lgamma(a)) prefactor. All call sites pass an integer df, and df=1 is the hot one: the LR p-value in lrt_ci_newton.cpp:53, evaluated once per constrained refit in Newton CI inversion, plus _helper_functions.h:162/194.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. Boost.Math docs say these finite sums 'are both more stable and more efficient than the continued fraction alternative' (a<30), but give no number. For df=1 it replaces a CF loop plus lgamma/log/exp with one rational erfc evaluation. The caller's cost is dominated by the model refit, so the end-to-end gain is likely negligible; the gain is mainly accuracy (no lgamma prefactor cancellation at larger df). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The identities are exact, so the p-value is unchanged to within tolerance, and the result is deterministic. Accuracy is at least as good: it removes the a·log(a)·ε prefactor loss the inventory notes, and in the far tail fast_erfc defers to std::erfc above 5.6. The finite sums are evaluated directly, so no Boost code is needed and there is no license issue. Risks: a branch-boundary test against R::pchisq is needed. The df=1 result is not bit-identical to the current CF, so stored reference p-values may shift in the last digits. The speed benefit end-to-end is small; the main argument is accuracy and simplicity.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look, low priority. Add an integer/half-integer fast path in fast_gammaq, with at least the df=1 → fast_erfc(√(x/2)) and df=2 → exp(−x/2) special cases. Gate it on a cross-check against R::pchisq(lower=FALSE) over df ∈ {1,…,60} and x ∈ [1e-8, 1e3]. It could be folded into the row-O accuracy TODO (v1.0.5 audit TODO-22).
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_pchisq_upper`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammaq`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammaq_cf`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammap_series`
- **Sources:** https://dlmf.nist.gov/8.4 https://www.boost.org/doc/libs/release/libs/math/doc/html/math_toolkit/sf_gamma/igamma.html

### Batch O.3: truncated atanh series → Minimax (Remez/fpminimax) polynomial in s² over [0,1/9], the same construction fdlibm uses for log: s=f/(2+f) with a degree-14 even polynomial (Lg1..Lg7, i.e. 7 coefficients) whose error is bounded by 2^-58.45 on s in [0,0.1716]. The same evaluation scheme as the current Horner, with fitted coefficients in place of the 1/(2k+1) Taylor ones.
- **Current:** Truncated 10-term Taylor (atanh) series in s²; s=z/(2+z) with z=exp(-|x|), so s is in (0,1/3]. Claimed absolute error below 5e-12.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. At equal accuracy it would save a few FMAs per element (fewer terms); at an equal number of terms it gives much smaller error than the Taylor truncation. The exp call dominates the cost either way, so the speed gain is marginal. This is mainly an accuracy-at-equal-cost option. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The coefficients have to be generated (Sollya/Remez) for EDI's wider s range, (0,1/3] rather than fdlibm's [0,0.1716], and validated. The result is still not bit-identical to R's log1p, though it is closer. fdlibm is Sun-permissive licensed, and only the method, not the code, would be reused. This option is already listed in catalog P11 ('minimax polynomial … [cite needed]'); this finding supplies that citation and adds no new speed evidence.
- **Suggested action:** Fill the '[cite needed]' in catalog P11 with fdlibm e_log.c. Optionally refit the 10 Horner coefficients as a minimax polynomial on s² in [0,1/9] to bring fast_log1pexp close to ulp accuracy at the same cost. No speed-driven change is warranted.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::fast_log1pexp`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::log1pexp_array_fast`
- **Sources:** https://www.netlib.org/fdlibm/e_log.c

### Batch P.1: all-pairs one-pass variance → Exact closed forms from per-group moments, computed on centered data. Rank-sum: the mean of the differences is x̄_T − ȳ_C, and the population variance of all differences is var_pop(T) + var_pop(C), so the sample variance is N/(N−1)·(var_pop(T)+var_pop(C)) with N = n_T n_C. Signed-rank: with S = Σd and Q = Σd² over m centered diffs, the sum of Walsh averages is (m+1)S/2 and their sum of squares is ((m+2)Q + S²)/4, divided by M = m(m+1)/2. Both use the shift invariance of the variance. Cost is O(n_T + n_C) or O(m), and Chan/Welford-style centering avoids the cancellation that affects the naive formula.
- **Current:** An explicit double loop over all n_T·n_C pairwise differences (or all m(m+1)/2 Walsh averages), accumulating a naive one-pass sum and sum of squares. Cost is O(n_T n_C) or O(m²), and the variance comes from (SumSq − Sum²/N)/(N−1).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by a source. Algebraically, O(n_T n_C) becomes O(n_T + n_C), and O(m²) becomes O(m). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: high)
- **Trade-offs:** The result matches the double loop up to floating-point rounding and should be more accurate, because the naive sum/sum² over O(n²) terms is the cancellation-prone formula the source warns against. The only caller is the legacy IVWC path, so the practical gain is small. This is already tracked as Adopt, low priority (TODO 5). Keep the R mirror in inference_all_KK_wilcox_ivwc.R in lockstep.
- **Suggested action:** No new decision is needed. When TODO 5 is picked up, replace both double loops with the centered O(n) closed forms. Verify against the current loop on fixtures, to 1e-12 relative, including a large-offset case where the naive formula loses precision.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_wilcox_hl.cpp::estimate_hl_ssq_rank_sum`, `R/EDI/src/fast_wilcox_hl.cpp::estimate_hl_ssq_signed_rank`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algorithms_for_calculating_variance

### Batch P.3: Newcombe paired hybrid score interval → Newcombe (1998b) method 10 as implemented by StatsDirect and statsmodels (the latter says it was verified against ratesci and PropCIs): when AD−BC > 0, replace the numerator with max(AD − BC − N/2, 0); a negative numerator is unchanged; φ = 0 if any margin is 0. The zero-margin rule already matches EDI.
- **Current:** φ = (n11·n00 − n10·n01)/sqrt(product of margins), uncorrected Pearson φ; φ = 0 when the margin product is 0 (newcombe_speedups.cpp:128-129).
- **Claimed speedup:** none: this is an estimator/accuracy correction, not a speedup (O(1) either way) (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This changes the reported CI whenever AD > BC: φ shrinks, so the interval widens slightly. It matters most at small n. It brings EDI in line with the published method 10 and reference software, so the numbers will change relative to current EDI output. I could not read the original Newcombe PDF text, so the formula comes from secondary implementations (StatsDirect code, statsmodels docs) that agree with each other.
- **Suggested action:** Resolve the Investigate verdict. Check the formula against Newcombe 1998b, Stat Med 17:2635, then apply the N/2 correction to φ's numerator when it is positive. Add a test against ratesci (moverpairci/pairbinci with Wilson base and corc=TRUE) or statsmodels method 'newcombe'.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/newcombe_speedups.cpp::newcombe_paired_ci_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://www.statsdirect.com/help/proportions/paired.htm https://www.statsmodels.org/dev/generated/statsmodels.stats.proportion.confint_proportions_paired.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ratesci/vignettes/paired_contrasts.html

### Batch P.4: separate logit + beta regression fits → Call EDI's existing fast_beta_regression_weighted_cpp(X_mid, y_mid, w_mid) (C++ L-BFGS, weights multiply each observation's log-likelihood contribution as betareg case weights do) on the interior rows, keeping betareg/lm.wfit only as fallbacks. Skip the two logits unless the estimand is marginal.
- **Current:** Per Bayesian-bootstrap replicate: betareg::betareg(y ~ ., data = df_mid, weights = w_mid) via the formula interface (model.frame, optim BFGS plus Fisher scoring in R). Two weighted binary logits for 1[y=0] and 1[y=1] are also fit every replicate, but they are only read when the estimand is marginal_mean_diff.
- **Claimed speedup:** Repo benchmark (fetched rawcdn.githack page, model-fit table, N=1,000): InferencePropBetaRegr 0.88 ms (EDI C++ beta regression) vs 24.85 ms betareg.fit, 28.38x. The formula betareg() used here adds model.frame overhead on top, so the per-replicate gain should be at least this order (not measured on this exact path). Skipping the unused logits saves two more IRLS fits per replicate under the conditional estimand (not quantified). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same weighted beta-regression MLE (interior rows), but via a different optimizer and stopping rule, so equal within tolerance rather than bit-identical. Needs an equivalence test vs betareg with non-integer BB weights. betareg's start uses phi = 10 in the current call; pass start_phi = 10 to match. Confirm the C++ kernel's handling of y exactly at sanitize_beta_response bounds matches. GPL-compatible (in-package code).
- **Suggested action:** Prototype: swap betareg::betareg for fast_beta_regression_weighted_cpp in the ZOIB BB replicate and gate the zero/one logits on the marginal estimand. Verify coefficient agreement with betareg on weighted data, then time a B=1000 BB run.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_zero_one_inflated_beta.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`
- **Sources:** https://rawcdn.githack.com/kapelner/EDI/main/R/benchmark/benchmark_model_fits_R.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/betareg/refman/betareg.html

### Batch P.4: separate logit + beta regression fits → Vectorize: idx = which(u >= p0 + p1); y_sim[idx] = rbeta(length(idx), pmax(mu[idx]*phi, 1e-4), pmax((1-mu[idx])*phi, 1e-4)). rbeta recycles shape arguments elementwise and draws in index order, so the RNG stream is consumed in the same order as the loop.
- **Current:** Parametric-bootstrap simulator draws runif(n), then loops i in 1:n in interpreted R, calling rbeta(1L, a_i, b_i) for each interior row.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source; removes an O(n) interpreted loop with n separate rbeta calls per PB replicate. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Should be bit-identical for a fixed seed, since draws happen in the same order with the same per-draw algorithm (Cheng 1978). This needs an identical() check. Small win relative to the ZOIB refit that follows each simulation.
- **Suggested action:** Adopt as a low-priority cleanup, gated on an identical() check of y_sim vs the loop under a fixed seed.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_zero_one_inflated_beta.R::simulate_under_lik_null`
- **Sources:** https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/stats/html/Beta.html

### Batch P.4: weighted Greenwood-type RMST variance → Sort the arm once by y, use rowsum (or tabulate on match(y, ut)) for per-time weighted events and totals, and get the weighted at-risk n_j as a reverse cumulative sum of the per-time totals. Then apply the same cumprod / reverse-cumsum A(t_j) / variance formula. Alternatively, reuse the weighted n.risk/n.event from the survfit fit already built for the point estimate, if its weighted counts match.
- **Current:** Per arm and per BB replicate: ut = sort(unique(y)); n_j = vapply(ut, sum(wt[y >= t])); d_j = vapply(ut, sum(wt[y == t & d == 1])). That is O(n·D) ≈ O(n²) work in R closures, followed by the O(D) Greenwood-type sum Σ A² d/(n(n−d)).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source; complexity O(n·D) → O(n log n) per arm per replicate, the same pattern and rationale as the already-adopted v1.0.5-TODO-6 (weighted KM median). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same formula. Results differ only in floating-point summation order (not bit-identical), so use an all.equal gate, and keep unit weights reproducing the C++ get_restricted_mean_se_diff. Ties form by exact equality, as now.
- **Suggested action:** Adopt: fold into v1.0.5-TODO-6's scope (same reverse-cumsum fix) and update the inventory verdict from 'Not yet audited' to Adopt.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_rmst.R::weighted_restricted_mean_se_diff`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaplan–Meier_estimator https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/refman/survival.html

### Batch P.4: weighted Kaplan–Meier (repeated tail sums) → After the existing sort, compute at-risk weights by reverse cumsum, preallocate the vectors, and stop the sweep at the first time S ≤ 0.5 plus one more event time, which the midpoint rule needs.
- **Current:** Still as audited at commit fa2fe36d: at_risk_weight = sum(row_weights[i:n]) at every distinct time, survival_probs/unique_times grown with c(), and no early exit once S ≤ 0.5. O(n·D) per BB replicate.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by a source; O(n·D) → O(n log n) (sort-bound) per replicate. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Identical up to FP summation order. The median midpoint rule (|S−0.5| < tol uses the next event time) must be preserved when exiting early.
- **Suggested action:** No new decision: v1.0.5-TODO-6 (Adopt) is still unimplemented in code. Keep it open and consider doing it together with the weighted RMST variance fix above.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_km_diff.R::weighted_km_median`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaplan–Meier_estimator

### Batch Q.1: bootstrap refits (per-draw sort + Newton) → For the p=1 two-group Cox case, collapse each draw to per-distinct-event-time counts (deaths and at-risk per arm, which the planned rank0 presort/counting pass yields in O(n)). Then run Newton at O(K) per iteration, as FastSurvival::coxph_fast does (single-pass risk sets, Newton from beta=0 with step-halving and a bracketing interval, matching survival::coxph to rounding).
- **Current:** Per draw: gather, re-sort inside CoxData, then Newton over all n records each iteration, O(B·(n log n + iter·n)) at p=1.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this kernel. FastSurvival's speed-comparison vignette reports ~25x for coxph_fast vs coxph() at n=500, but most of that is R formula/object overhead that EDI's C++ kernel does not have. The real algorithmic gain is the per-iteration cost falling from O(n) to O(K), which is small unless ties are heavy. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same Breslow MLE within tolerance, but summation order changes, so the result is tolerance-equal rather than bit-identical. Only applies to p=1 without covariates. The function is documented as having no in-package caller, so the payoff is near zero unless it gets wired in. FastSurvival is MIT-licensed (GPL-3 compatible), but only the idea would be reused.
- **Suggested action:** Add as a note to small_kernel_hoists_batch.md Item C: if compute_coxph_rand_bootstrap_cpp ever gets a caller, fold the per-arm counting pass into a K-row aggregated Newton. Otherwise no action.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_coxph_regression.cpp::compute_coxph_rand_bootstrap_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/FastSurvival/refman/FastSurvival.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/FastSurvival/vignettes/speed-comparison.html

### Batch Q.2: Monte Carlo randomization distribution → Anytime-valid sequential Monte Carlo test by betting (Fischer & Ramdas, JRSS-B 2025, doi:10.1093/jrsssb/qkaf014). It generalizes Besag–Clifford with test martingales: a valid p-value at any stopping time, early stopping under both null and alternative (classical BC saves draws only under the null), and bounded resampling risk. The anytime-valid BC variant and the binomial-mixture strategy are both closed form, O(1) per draw.
- **Current:** Fixed r (default 501) draws generated up front. p = (b+1)/(r+1). Besag–Clifford sequential stopping is tracked as an opt-in (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-156), and Gandy 2009 is listed in catalog P4.
- **Claimed speedup:** Fischer, Barry & Ramdas, arXiv:2404.15586 §6.1 / Fig. 2: about 200 permutations on average vs a fixed B = 10,000, with similar power. Fig. 3: mean 327 (anytime-valid BC) and 462 (binomial mixture) permutations per rejection. The gain relative to EDI's default r = 501 is not quantified and would be much smaller. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The reported p-value changes: it becomes an anytime-valid (generally more conservative) p, not (b+1)/(r+1), so it is an estimator change and must be opt-in like TODO-156. Deterministic only if draws are produced in draw-index order with per-index seeds (EDI already does this) and generated in batches rather than as one n×r matrix. The early-stop gain over r = 501 is modest except for clearly significant or clearly null results. Not covered by the v1.2.0-TODO-34 confidence-sequence plan, which targets the CI search, not the plain p-value.
- **Suggested action:** When TODO-156 (Besag–Clifford) is designed, evaluate the anytime-valid BC / betting p-value as the stopping rule, since it also stops early under the alternative. Add it to catalog P4.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::approximate_randomization_distribution_beta_hat_T`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::generate_permutations`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.07365 https://arxiv.org/html/2404.15586v4

### Batch Q.2: Lin–Wei robust sandwich (prefix sums) → Since sd.y is already sorted ascending within each stratum (the risk-set loop relies on it), advance a monotone pointer over unique_event_times while iterating i, giving O(n+K) with identical k_last. survival's coxscore2.c (updated 4/2023, survival 3.5-7 NEWS: score residuals from O(ndp) to O((n+d)p)) uses a single backward pass over sorted data with running cumhaz/xhaz sums.
- **Current:** Per-subject binary search over unique_event_times for k_last, O(n log K), plus O((n+K)p) prefix sums.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. It removes a log K factor from a non-dominant term, so it is negligible at EDI's sizes. (applies at EDI's sizes: no; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Bit-identical (same k_last, same arithmetic) and deterministic. The benefit is negligible because the O((n+K)p) terms and the p×p sandwich dominate, and the function runs once per clustered fit. Worth doing only alongside the I-6 change to reverse accumulation in the same function, which is the more important accuracy fix.
- **Suggested action:** Optional. When fixing I-6 (reverse accumulation of r_exp) in compute_robust_vcov, replace the binary search with a monotone pointer, or restructure as survival's single backward pass. No standalone change is warranted.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_coxph_regression.cpp::compute_robust_vcov`
- **Sources:** https://raw.githubusercontent.com/therneau/survival/master/src/coxscore2.c https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/news.html

### Batch R.1: exact binomial test (minlike) → R's stats:::.binom_test_two_sided_pval_minlike approach. It calls dbinom only on the side of the mode opposite x to count the boundary y, then takes pbinom(x) + pbinom(n−y, upper), or the mirrored form. For large n it finds the boundaries by binary search, O(log n) dbinom calls.
- **Current:** Loops x=0..n calling R::dbinom(log) for every outcome, and sums exp() of all outcomes with log p ≤ log p_obs + log1p(1e-7). That is O(n) dbinom calls plus O(n) exps.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. By construction it needs about half the dbinom calls and no exp-sum for n<1234, and O(log n) for n≥1234 (R source of binom.test). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same minlike rule and the same 1e-7 relative tolerance, so the p-value is identical within FP. pbinom tails are, if anything, more accurate than summing exp(log dbinom). EDI's d (discordant pairs) is typically hundreds or fewer, so each call already takes microseconds. The win matters only if the Zhang CI bisection that calls this per δ shows up in profiling. Edge cases to port: x == n·p returns 1, and p at 0 or 1.
- **Suggested action:** Low priority. If touching zhang_exact_speedups.cpp anyway, port R's one-sided boundary search plus the pbinom tails. Otherwise Keep.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/zhang_exact_speedups.cpp::binom_two_sided_pval`
- **Sources:** https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wch/r-source/trunk/src/library/stats/R/binom.test.R

### Batch R.1: centroid balanced clustering + Ward holdout → fastcluster::hclust (or hclust.vector with method='ward' on the raw X to avoid storing the dist object). The package says it is a drop-in replacement for stats::hclust with a much faster implementation and memory-saving routines for vector data. anticlust 0.8.18 still has no unequal-size option that would remove the holdout step.
- **Current:** When n mod B ≠ 0 the holdout runs stats::hclust(stats::dist(X), method='ward.D2'), with O(n²) memory for dist and stats::hclust's generic algorithm, then anticlust::balanced_clustering(method='centroid') on the rest. The design is re-solved per BRT replay draw.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI sizes. The CRAN page and the JSS abstract (Müllner 2013) say only 'much faster' and 'improves asymptotic time complexity in most cases'. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Adds a dependency (FreeBSD/GPL-2, which is GPL-3 compatible). Results match hclust only up to tie-breaking among equal merge heights, so the cutree partition, and with it the design, could change on tied inputs. hclust.vector's 'ward' takes Euclidean input, so ward.D2 equivalence and height scaling would need checking. At n in the hundreds, stats::hclust already takes milliseconds.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look only if profiling a DesignFixedOptimalBlocks BRT at large n shows the hclust holdout as a hotspot. If so, A/B fastcluster::hclust against stats::hclust for identical cutree output on the test fixtures.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_optimal_blocks.R::solve_kway_blocks`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastcluster https://www.jstatsoft.org/article/view/v053i09 https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/anticlust/refman/anticlust.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=anticlust

### Batch R.2: Fisher exact test (noncentral hypergeometric enumeration) → For p-value-only calls, compute just the minlike p. Option 1: call EDI's existing C++ zhang_exact_fisher_pval_cpp(n11, n10, n01, n00, delta_0), which uses the same minlike rule with relErr 1e-7 (log-space log1p(1e-7) is equivalent to R's d <= d_obs*(1+1e-7)). Option 2: once EDI's minimum R allows it, use fisher.test(..., pval.only = TRUE), new in R-devel for the 2x2 exact case. Keep the full fisher.test for the CI and estimate calls.
- **Current:** pval_exact_fisher(delta_0) calls stats::fisher.test(table, or = exp(delta_0), conf.level = ...) for the single-stratum 2x2 case. That computes the minlike p-value, and also the CMLE (uniroot on mnhyper) and both conditional CI limits (uniroot on pnhyper), each root search taking ~10-30 O(S) pmf passes. The result is cached only for delta_0 = 0, alpha = 0.05, so each p-value at another delta_0 pays for the unused CMLE and CI.
- **Claimed speedup:** R-devel NEWS says pval.only gives 'substantial speed gains in the case of large counts'; it gives no number. In theory the p-only path drops the ~3 uniroot searches (roughly 30-90 extra O(S) pmf passes), so it should be several times to tens of times faster per p-value call. Not measured. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The same minlike p to within floating-point tolerance. The C++ path uses lgamma log-weights where R uses dhyper on the log scale, so the two can differ by a few ULP and could flip a tie at exactly the 1e-7 boundary. pval.only needs R-devel, so it is not yet usable under EDI's R dependency. The absolute gain is in milliseconds and matters only if p-values at many delta_0 values are requested, e.g. user-side CI inversion or simulations. The stratified mantelhaen.test branch is unaffected.
- **Suggested action:** Low priority. Route pval_exact_fisher for the single-stratum case through zhang_exact_fisher_pval_cpp, or a p-only R helper, instead of the full fisher.test. Gate it with an equality test against fisher.test(...)$p.value over a grid of small tables and delta_0 values. Revisit pval.only when EDI's minimum R version includes it.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_indicidence_exact_fisher.R::get_exact_fisher_htest`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/doc/manuals/r-devel/NEWS.html https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wch/r-source/trunk/src/library/stats/R/fisher.test.R

### Batch R.2: Fisher / Stouffer / min-p p-value combination → Wasserstein-adjusted discrete combination (Contador & Wu 2025, SJS 52(3):1281-1300; arXiv:2508.02647). Each discrete component is replaced by an adjusted statistic, and a continuous null (e.g. an optimal gamma instead of chi^2) is fitted to the combination statistic. Lancaster's mid-p and mean-value chi^2 come out as special cases. It is implemented in DPComb 1.0.1 (CRAN, 2026-08-22, MIT), which explicitly supports binomial and noncentral hypergeometric components, i.e. EDI's exact pair.
- **Current:** Two independent exact p-values (a matched-pairs conditional binomial and a reservoir noncentral-hypergeometric Fisher test) are combined by Fisher chi^2_4, Stouffer or min-p. Each treats the discrete p's as U(0,1), which is conservative. The catalog option is exact joint convolution, O(d·S).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not a speedup. Its cost is O(d + S) like the current method, and cheaper than the O(d·S) exact convolution. This is an estimator (test) change: it reduces the conservativeness the Investigate verdict flags. The sources give no runtime numbers. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** It changes the reported p-value and so the Zhang combined CI. It is an approximation, not exact: the arXiv abstract says Type I error is accurate only when the adjusted statistic's variance matches the continuous case, so it may be slightly anti-conservative, unlike the exact convolution, which keeps EDI's exactness promise. MIT is GPL-3 compatible, but a new dependency, or reimplementing it, is needed. The JCGS paper is still under revision.
- **Suggested action:** Add as a third comparator row in catalog §11 (KK combination) when resolving the Investigate verdict. Prefer exact convolution if exactness is required, and treat DPComb/Lancaster mid-p as the cheap less-conservative non-exact option. Possibly use DPComb as a test oracle for a mid-p/Wasserstein variant.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_combine_exact_pvals`, `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_pval_exact_combined`, `R/EDI/src/zhang_exact_speedups.cpp::fisher_noncentral_two_sided_pval`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=DPComb https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02647

### Batch R.3: MILP branch and bound (McCormick linearization) → Add 'highs' to the closed roi_solver set (ROI.plugin.highs → highs, both on CRAN) and consider making it the default open-source backend in place of GLPK. The model is unchanged. This is independent of the reformulations in catalog table (c) and stacks with them.
- **Current:** ompr model with binary x_ik and binary z_ijk, three McCormick rows per (i,j,k), x_11 = 1. Default roi_solver = 'glpk'; the closed set is {glpk, gurobi, cplex}.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this problem. General MIPLIB 2017 figures from Mittelmann's benchmark, as reproduced in Table form at https://arxiv.org/html/2312.13527v4: unscaled shifted geometric mean 7317 for GLPK vs 3114 for HiGHS (about 2.3×), with 6 vs 24 instances solved. These are large general MILPs, not this partition model at EDI's tiny n. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Both solvers are exact, so the optimal objective value is the same. When several partitions tie at the optimum, HiGHS may return a different one than GLPK, which changes the blocks and therefore the design, so existing seeded results would not reproduce bit for bit. HiGHS MIP can use threads; determinism across thread counts needs checking (or fix threads = 1). It adds a CRAN dependency: highs 1.15.1-2 (published 2026-11-02) and ROI.plugin.highs 1.0-4 (2025-04-24), both GPL-2 | GPL-3. I could not read the plugin manual's text, so it is not yet confirmed that ROI.plugin.highs registers binary/integer (MILP) signatures; verify before adopting.
- **Suggested action:** As part of the TODO-5 benchmark (catalog table (c)), time GLPK vs HiGHS through ompr.roi on the current model and on the continuous-z/symmetry-broken model at the n EDI tests (6–20). If HiGHS wins and supports MILP via ROI, add 'highs' to assert_optimal_roi_solver's closed set.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_optimal_blocks.R::solve_optimal_blocks`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=ROI.plugin.highs https://cran.r-project.org/package=highs https://arxiv.org/html/2312.13527v4

### Batch R.3: MILP branch and bound (McCormick linearization) → For dist ∈ {euclidean, mahal}, the kernels return squared distances, so when n mod B = 0 the objective is (n/B) × equal-size MSSC on X (or on whitened X for mahal). Specialized exact clustering methods then apply. (a) A Brusco-style repetitive branch-and-bound (Psychometrika 2006) written as a small deterministic C++ kernel, with the size bounds ⌊n/B⌋..⌈n/B⌉ added as a pruning rule. It needs no solver dependency. (b) Piccialli & Sudoso's SDP branch-and-cut for cardinality-constrained MSSC (Math. Programming 2023), which treats equal sizes as a special case.
- **Current:** General MILP (McCormick, weak LP bound, B! label symmetry with only x_11 fixed) solved by GLPK. Practical only for very small n.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified against GLPK. The Cambridge abstract for Brusco 2006 says it solves 'poorly separated problems with no inherent cluster structure ... for up to 60 objects and six clusters' (well-separated: 240 objects, 8 clusters); unconstrained sizes, no runtimes given. Piccialli & Sudoso, Tables 2–3 of https://arxiv.org/html/2209.08901v3: cardinality-constrained instances solved exactly with n=75, k=4 in 3 s (1 node) up to n=1000, k=4 in 34396 s, with a 12 h limit. The paper does not benchmark against a plain MILP. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Both methods are exact, so the optimum value is unchanged, though tie-breaking among equal-cost partitions can differ. The MSSC equivalence holds only for squared Euclidean/Mahalanobis with n mod B = 0. With unequal sizes, the pairwise-sum objective weights each block's within-SS by its size, so it is not MSSC, and a branch-and-bound would have to bound the pairwise objective directly (Brusco 2003 targets that objective, but I could not fetch it). L1 and custom distances are not covered. Brusco's published method has no size constraints; adding them is unverified engineering. CC-SOS-SDP is not usable as is: it needs MATLAB SDPNAL+ and Gurobi, and it targets n in the hundreds, far above where EDI runs this path. It is useful only as evidence that SDP bounds close the gap in a few nodes.
- **Suggested action:** Add a row to catalog table (c): 'squared-distance kinds with n mod B = 0 reduce to equal-size MSSC; Brusco RBBA-style B&B with size pruning (C++, no solver)'. Include it as an arm of the TODO-5 benchmark alongside the continuous-z and symmetry-breaking MILP. Do not pursue CC-SOS-SDP, because of its MATLAB/Gurobi dependency.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_optimal_blocks.R::solve_optimal_blocks`
- **Sources:** https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/psychometrika/article/repetitive-branchandbound-procedure-for-minimum-withincluster-sums-of-squares-partitioning/2F76AC24E922EBFE879A16FE7429B15C https://arxiv.org/html/2209.08901v3 https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.08901

### Batch S.1: i.i.d. draws / rejection sampling without replacement → (a) Same rejection loop, but mark seen positions in a dense bitset or std::vector<uint8_t> of length n (scratch reused across b and strata). Same RNG calls in the same order, so the selected units are bit-identical and no seed-pinned test changes. (b) dqrng's rate-based dispatch: a partial Fisher–Yates on a scratch copy of ids above a 50% sampling rate (= v1.0.5-TODO-2; changes the stream), a bitset below.
- **Current:** replace = FALSE: draw positions with bounded_rand and reject duplicates through a std::unordered_set<int> reserved at 2k. Expected n(H_n − H_{n−k}) draws, each with a hash insert.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for unordered_set vs bitset at EDI's sizes. The dqrng vignette (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dqrng/vignettes/sample.html) reports, against base R sample.int: bitset rejection 102.6 µs vs 1.94 ms, and vs 657.8 µs for base R's hash variant, at m=1e6, n=1e4. Partial Fisher–Yates: 10.3 ms vs 63.7 ms at m=1e6, n=6e5. Those sizes are far above EDI's, so the ratio is indicative only. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Option (a): O(n) bits of scratch (trivial) and zero change in output, so it is a pure drop-in. Option (b): fewer RNG words when k is near n, but the realized draws change. End-to-end gain matters only if m-out-of-n subsampling dominates a run, which the catalog says it usually does not.
- **Suggested action:** When v1.0.5-TODO-2 is picked up, first swap the unordered_set for a reusable dense marker, which keeps the output bit-identical, and microbenchmark it at k/n ∈ {0.25, 0.5, 0.9}, n ≤ 1e4. Adopt partial Fisher–Yates only if it is still materially faster at k > n/2. Add the bitset option to the catalog's P2 options table.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/exchangeable_resampling_draws.cpp::sample_group_units`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dqrng/vignettes/sample.html

### Batch S.1: Fisher–Yates shuffle → Batched ranged random integer generation (Brackett-Rozinsky & Lemire, Software: Practice and Experience 55(1), 2024). Several unbiased bounded indices come from one random word, with one multiply and no division in the common case. With 32-bit words, two indices per word work while i·(i−1) < 2^32 (i ≲ 65k), which covers EDI's n.
- **Current:** Durstenfeld Fisher–Yates with one Lemire bounded_rand, so one or more 32-bit MT19937 words, per swap.
- **Claimed speedup:** The paper reports shuffle_6 vs the unbatched shuffle across a range of array lengths: 1.4–1.8× (Lehmer) and 1.8–2.5× (PCG64) on Xeon, 1.5–2.3× / 1.9–2.6× on Apple M2. Only 64-bit generators were tested; it is not measured with MT19937 or 32-bit words, and the sizes in the 100–10,000 range are not reported separately. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same distribution and unbiased (Theorem 4.1), deterministic given the seed. But the realized permutations change, so every seed-pinned draw is refreshed a second time after v1.0.5-TODO-3. Python/R parity must be re-verified, and the code is more complex. The shuffle is usually not the dominant cost: rerandomization evaluates an O(np) objective per candidate, so the end-to-end win is small. Reference code at github.com/lemire/batched_random is MIT (GPL-3 compatible).
- **Suggested action:** Record it as an option in the catalog P2 table. Consider it only if a profile shows shuffle or RNG words are a material share of rerandomization or null-draw time; bundle it with any other planned stream change (for example TODO-149(c) RNG throughput) so seeds are refreshed only once.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/RNG.h::fisher_yates_shuffle`, `R/EDI/src/rerandomization_helpers.cpp::complete_randomization_forced_balanced_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/rerandomization_helpers.cpp::complete_randomization_imbalanced_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/generate_permutations.cpp::generate_permutations_ibcrd_internal`, `R/EDI/src/optimal_design_search.cpp::random_balanced_init`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06213 https://arxiv.org/html/2408.06213v2 https://github.com/lemire/batched_random

### Batch S.1: batched complete randomization within blocks → A small C++ kernel: per draw column, a partial Fisher–Yates of k = min(n_T, N−n_T) positions per block with the existing edi_rng::bounded_rand, keeping the block_ra law (random rounding of N·prob_T, singleton Bernoulli). Seed per draw index for thread-count determinism.
- **Current:** Vectorized R, per block: runif(N·r), then order(col_id, U), which by default is a linear-time radix sort, then ranks and a threshold at n_T. O(N·r) time, but with several N·r double/int temporaries and a two-key radix pass.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI's sizes. The R docs confirm the current order() is already linear (radix), so the gain is a constant factor only. For context, the dqrng vignette shows a partial Fisher–Yates at 10.3 ms vs base R sample's 63.7 ms (m=1e6, n=6e5). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same per-block law but different realized draws (RRng instead of R's runif stream), so the seed-pinned blocking tests and the bootstrap golden test would need refreshing. Adds a C++ kernel shortly after the old generate_permutations_blocking_cpp was deleted. One call per batch, so absolute savings are likely milliseconds.
- **Suggested action:** Update the inventory complexity to note the current path is linear (radix order). Profile block_ra_batch at typical sizes (N·r ≈ 1e5–1e6) and close v1.0.5-TODO-11 as Keep unless it is a measurable share of a design's draw time.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_blocking.R::block_ra_batch`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_blocking.R::draw_ws_raw`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_blocked_cluster.R::draw_ws_raw`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_cluster.R::draw_ws_raw`, `R/EDI/R/design_fixed_optimal_blocks.R::draw_ws_raw`
- **Sources:** https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/base/html/order.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dqrng/vignettes/sample.html

### Batch S.2: Lemire nearly-divisionless bounded integer → Batched ranged random integer generation (Brackett-Rozinsky & Lemire 2024). It draws k bounded integers from one L-bit word whenever the product of the ranges is ≤ 2^L, still unbiased, with one multiply per value in the common case. The paper gives L=32 batch thresholds, so 32-bit MT words work: for example 2 draws per word when the remaining length is ≤ 2^16.
- **Current:** One Lemire multiply-shift-reject per bounded draw, so one 32-bit MT word per Fisher-Yates step in fisher_yates_shuffle (generate_permutations.cpp:191,406, rerandomization_helpers.cpp:232, design_fixed_greedy.cpp:160, spbr/random_block speedups, fast_shuffle.cpp).
- **Claimed speedup:** The arXiv 2408.06213 HTML reports that shuffle_6 beats unbatched Fisher-Yates by 1.4-1.8x (Lehmer), 1.8-2.5x (PCG) and 2.9-4.7x (ChaCha) on Xeon, and says slower generators gain more. Arrays were cache-resident with 64-bit generators. MT19937 and 32-bit words were not benchmarked, so the gain for EDI is not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Exact uniformity and the estimator are unchanged, but the seed→permutation map changes, so every seed-pinned test and the documented R/Python cross-language draw identity need refreshing. With 32-bit words the batches are small (2-3 per word at n in the hundreds), so the realistic gain is below the paper's 64-bit numbers. It only matters if TODO-149(c) profiling shows RNG time is material next to the per-draw statistic. Licensing is fine: the algorithm is published and can be reimplemented, or the reference code reviewed.
- **Suggested action:** Add as an option row under catalog P2 (bounded integers/shuffles) and bundle it into any future stream-changing RNG work under performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-149(c), so the seed-pinned tests are refreshed only once. Do not adopt on its own.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/RNG.h::bounded_rand`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06213 https://arxiv.org/html/2408.06213

### Batch S.3: SplitMix64 seed derivation → A counter-based generator such as Philox4x32-10 or Threefry (Random123, header-only, BSD-3-Clause), keyed by (master_seed, draw index) with the within-draw position as the counter. A small-state generator (xoshiro/PCG) seeded through SplitMix64 is a second option. Either way the per-index setup drops from about 1,300 state words of work to a few words, and the derivation stays a pure function of the index, so results are still thread-count invariant.
- **Current:** Each logical draw index gets seed_for_index(master, index), truncated to 32 bits. That seed constructs a fresh edi_rng::RRng (an R Mersenne Twister port): 50+625 LCG steps to fill the 624-word, 2.5 KB state, then a full 624-word regeneration on the first next_word(). This happens per draw in hot acceptance-rejection and search loops (rerandomization_helpers.cpp:230 candidate_is_accepted, design_fixed_greedy.cpp:97/147, optimal_design_search.cpp:167/214, binary_match_search.cpp:39).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source for this workload. Random123 docs (fetched): CBRNGs are 'as fast as, or faster than conventional RNGs', need 'a few cycles per byte', and have 'little or no overhead to creating billions of unique' generators. Vigna's PRNG page (fetched): MT19937-64 has a 20,000-bit footprint and costs 1.14–1.39 ns per 64 bits, against 0.58 ns for xoshiro256++. From reading the code (my count, not a measurement): at n=100, p=5 one rerandomization candidate does about 100 bounded draws plus a 5x100 mat-vec, while the RRng construction plus first twist is several thousand integer ops. Setup could therefore be the largest part of each candidate at EDI's sizes. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same distributions and same estimators, but every seed-pinned draw and fixture on these paths changes once (as with the planned Fisher–Yates switch). Per-index streams would no longer be R's MT algorithm. These streams already cannot be reproduced from R's own stream, because the seed passes through SplitMix64, so parity with R is not lost in practice, but the RNG.h design rationale ('portable re-implementation of R's actual algorithm') would need updating. License: Random123 is BSD-3-Clause, compatible with GPL-3. Side benefit: today's 32-bit truncated seeds collide by the birthday bound (about 1 expected duplicate candidate pair per ~1e5 draws, which rerandomization with low acceptance rates reaches). A 64- or 128-bit key removes that. Caveat: arXiv 2605.18227 (May 2026, fetched) reports BigCrush failures in some streams for every generator family tested, Philox included, so pick the generator with that in mind. Benchmark before adopting: the speedup is an inference, not a measured number.
- **Suggested action:** Micro-benchmark RRng(seed_for_index(...)) construction plus first draw against the per-candidate work in rerandomization_helpers.cpp at n in {50, 200, 500} and p in {2, 10}. If setup is more than about 30% of a candidate, prototype a Philox-keyed (master, index) stream behind the existing seed_for_index call sites, regenerate the seed-pinned fixtures, and widen the key past 32 bits.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/RNG.h::seed_for_index`
- **Sources:** https://www.thesalmons.org/john/random123/releases/latest/docs/index.html https://raw.githubusercontent.com/DEShawResearch/random123/main/LICENSE https://prng.di.unimi.it/ https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18227

### Batch T.1: forward stepwise via full Wald refits → One reduced fit per step, then a Rao score z for every candidate, the same scheme kk21_stepwise_logistic_weights_cpp already uses. statmod::glm.scoretest is a vectorized reference implementation: candidates are columns of x2, it needs only the null fit, and NB dispersion is held fixed.
- **Current:** Full beta-quasi or NB2 refit of [1, x_j, selected, w] for every remaining candidate at every step, Wald |t|: O(p²/2) fits, about O(iter·n·p⁴).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the sources. The catalog's theoretical estimate is about p/2× fewer fits (5× at p=10). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Weight values change from Wald t to score z, so KK21 distances and matches change. Ordering can differ in small samples. NB needs θ fixed at the reduced fit, which is justified by β⊥θ orthogonality. Beta's φ is currently grid-estimated (catalog P4), which affects the information used. This is the option already tracked as v1.2.0-TODO-23; the new material here is only implementation references, not new evidence against the plan.
- **Suggested action:** No re-decision needed. When implementing v1.2.0-TODO-23, use statmod::glm.scoretest (Dunn & Smyth 2018 §7.2.6) as the A/B reference for the NB score z, and run the existing KK21 weight-comparison A/B.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_beta_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_negbin_weights_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/statmod/refman/statmod.html https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/stats/html/add1.html https://arxiv.org/html/2609.29335

### Batch T.1: cumulative-link fit via R callback (stepwise Wald) → Score-chi-square entry from the current reduced cumulative-link fit, with thresholds as nuisance parameters in the efficient information. This is how SAS PROC LOGISTIC does forward selection for cumulative models (1-df score chi-square at the reduced-model MLEs). The R package ordinal (2026.7-26) has only LR-based add1/drop1, which refits each candidate.
- **Current:** multivariate_ordinal_tstat now calls the C++ fast_ordinal_regression_internal directly, not an R Function() callback, so the inventory details are stale. The stepwise path still refits [x_j, S, w] for every candidate at every step (O(p²/2) cumulative-link fits).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the sources. The catalog's theoretical estimate is about p/2× fewer fits. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Score z replaces Wald |t|, so weights and matches change. The efficient information must include the threshold block (a Schur complement over the J−1 cutpoints plus selected β). This is already tracked as v1.2.0-TODO-23. Separately, the inventory's 'R callback' description and the unprotected-wrap crash note look out of date at fa2fe36d and need re-checking.
- **Suggested action:** Update the inventory rows for kk21_ordinal_weights_cpp, kk21_stepwise_ordinal_weights_cpp and multivariate_ordinal_tstat: the per-fit R callback is gone (it is now a direct C++ fast_ordinal_regression_internal call). Re-check whether the unprotected-wrap caveat still applies. Keep v1.2.0-TODO-23 as the speedup path and use PROC LOGISTIC's cumulative-model score entry as the reference design.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_ordinal_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::multivariate_ordinal_tstat`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_ordinal_weights_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://go.documentation.sas.com/api/docsets/statug/v_023/content/statug_logistic_details21.htm https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/ordinal.pdf

### Batch U.1: brute-force pairwise distance matrix (R callback) → Also accept a compiled user distance (RcppXPtrUtils::cppXPtr external pointer, routed through EDI's existing normalize_user_cpp_fn plumbing) and call it directly from C++. This is the pattern parallelDist uses for method='custom'. Keep the R-function path as is. Independently, reuse the two xi/xj buffers across pairs instead of allocating per pair.
- **Current:** A user R distance function is called once per pair, n(n-1)/2 R calls, and each call also allocates two fresh NumericVectors.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this exact comparison. parallelDist's README (fetched) calls its compiled custom-distance path 'fast' but gives no number against R-level functions. The gain is the removal of per-pair R interpreter call overhead. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Opt-in API addition; the user must write C++. Same distances if the user's C++ function matches their R function. Small user-visible win: the matrix is computed once and cached per design, and the ompr MILP dominates end-to-end at the n it can solve (low hundreds). parallelDist is GPL ≥2, but only the pattern is borrowed and no dependency is needed.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look, low priority: consider adding XPtr support for custom optimal-blocks distances, consistent with the other user-compiled-function entry points, and hoist the per-pair NumericVector allocation. Otherwise keep the verdict 'Keep (inherent to a black-box R fn)'.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/optimal_blocks_distance.cpp::distance_matrix_custom_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/optimal_blocks_distance.cpp::distance_matrix_custom_cpp_impl`, `R/EDI/src/optimal_blocks_distance.cpp::optimal_blocks_distance_matrix_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/parallelDist/readme/README.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=parallelDist

### Batch U.2: iterated pairwise-correlation filter → Compute cor once, drop unique(pairs[,2]) and return; delete the repeat loop. Pearson r_xy depends only on columns x and y, so removing columns leaves every remaining entry unchanged. Every flagged pair already lost its second member in round 1, so round 2 always finds zero pairs and breaks. NA correlations are dropped by which() in both rounds alike.
- **Current:** A repeat loop recomputes stats::cor(M) after removing unique(pairs[,2]), and stops when no pair exceeds the threshold. Each round costs O(np²).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. By the argument above, it removes exactly one of the two cor() calls, about 2× on this function. It matters because cox_design_candidates calls it 12× per call, per replicate (catalog §10 incidental note). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: high)
- **Trade-offs:** None for the speed change: same keep-set, bitwise identical. Separately, the known over-drop (incidental #8) would be fixed by dropping one column at a time. That fix is an estimator change (a different keep-set) and should not be bundled with this speedup.
- **Suggested action:** Remove the repeat loop, leaving a single cor pass, and add a test asserting the keep-set is unchanged on the existing corpus. Treat the incidental #8 over-drop fix as a separate estimator-change item.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_model_matrix.R::drop_highly_correlated_cols`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearson_correlation_coefficient

### Batch U.2: greedy column selection via repeated QR → Use a single column-pivoted QR with the required columns forced to the front. LAPACK DGEQP3 does this natively: JPVT(j) ≠ 0 marks column j as a 'leading' column, which is factored first, and the free columns are then norm-pivoted. Alternatively, append columns to an existing QR by updating it (Hammarling & Lucas, MIMS EPrint 2008.111) instead of refactoring.
- **Current:** A fresh ColPivHouseholderQR of the growing trial matrix for every required column and every candidate column, plus a QR on the residualized remainder: O(n p³).
- **Claimed speedup:** Hammarling–Lucas say updating is 'much faster' than refactoring but give no number on the abstract page. The O(n p³) → O(n p²) reduction is theoretical. Not quantified at EDI's sizes. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The keep-set can differ on near-collinear designs, because rank decisions depend on the factorization order and threshold. Eigen's ColPivHouseholderQR has no fixed-column option, so the DGEQP3 route means calling LAPACK through R's BLAS/LAPACK, which is GPL-compatible, or emulating it: an unpivoted Householder pass on the required block, then ColPivQR on the residual. The current code already partly does that emulation and then redundantly re-verifies each candidate. The function runs once per fit with the keep-set cached, so the absolute gain is small.
- **Suggested action:** When prototyping TODO-6 (Prototype-lite), use the DGEQP3 leading-column semantics as the reference. Drop the per-candidate re-QR after the residual QR and gate on keep-set equality across the test corpus, as the catalog already says.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/qr_reduce_design_matrix.cpp::qr_reduce_preserve_cols_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://www.netlib.org/lapack/explore-html/d0/dea/group__geqp3_gae96659507d789266660af28c617ef87f.html https://eprints.maths.manchester.ac.uk/1192/

### Batch U.2: Golub–Welsch eigenvalue method → Keep the adopted caching (TODO-1).
