If any uncached path remains, call SelfAdjointEigenSolver::computeFromTridiagonal(diag = 0, subdiag = sqrt((i+1)/2)), which skips the dense tridiagonal reduction that compute() performs. Asymptotic O(n) methods (Townsend–Trogdon–Olver) are not worth it at n_gh ≤ 60: SciPy uses Golub–Welsch for n ≤ 150.
- **Current:** A dense n_gh×n_gh Jacobi matrix passed to SelfAdjointEigenSolver::compute(), about 9n³ flops with eigenvectors per Eigen's docs. Rebuilt per fit and per score/Hessian export call.
- **Claimed speedup:** Eigen documents about 9n³ flops for compute() with eigenvectors and gives no figure for computeFromTridiagonal. The saving is not quantified, and caching makes it moot. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** computeFromTridiagonal agrees only within tolerance (different floating-point path), not bitwise, whereas caching is bitwise identical. Only worth it as a micro-option where caching is awkward.
- **Suggested action:** No change to the plan: finish TODO-1 caching and dedupe, including fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm's copy. Optionally build the single shared cached rule with computeFromTridiagonal.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::gauss_hermite_rule`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_log`, `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_poisson`, `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_hp`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/classEigen_1_1SelfAdjointEigenSolver.html https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.special.roots_hermite.html https://arxiv.org/abs/1410.5286

### Batch U.3: per-pair Mahalanobis quadratic form → LLT of (S + relative ridge), whiten once with Z = X·L^-T, then a direct per-pair squared-Euclidean loop on Z (not the Gram expansion): O(np^2 + n^2 p). This is the same option the audit already adopted. The sources fetched this run confirm it is the standard route: skfolio computes squared Mahalanobis distance via Cholesky with a relative ridge escalated on failure, and Eigen ranks LLT fastest among dense decompositions.
- **Current:** Naive covariance loops, absolute 1e-8 ridge, hand-rolled Gauss–Jordan inverse, then a per-pair S^-1·diff matvec: O(np^2 + p^3 + n^2 p^2).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this kernel by any source. The audit's theoretical estimate is about p× on the pair loop. Eigen's benchmark table at 8x8 gives LLT 0.05 ms against FullPivLU 0.1 ms, which affects only the O(p^3) part. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The result is equal within tolerance, not bit-equal, so MILP tie-breaking between near-equal distances could choose different blocks. The end-to-end gain is small because the ompr MILP dominates at the n it can solve. Keep the per-pair difference form rather than the Gram trick, to avoid cancellation on near-duplicate rows.
- **Suggested action:** No new action: this confirms the existing Adopt verdict (v1.0.5 TODO-29, catalog §8 TODO-1). When it is implemented, make the ridge relative (scaled by mean(diag S)) as planned.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/optimal_blocks_distance.cpp::distance_matrix_mahal_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/optimal_blocks_distance.cpp::compute_mahal_inverse_covariance`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html https://skfolio.org/generated/skfolio.utils.stats.squared_mahalanobis_dist.html

### Batch V.1: delete-one jackknife → For least-squares estimators, compute the exact delete-one-unit estimates in closed form: β^(g) = (X'X − X_gᵀX_g)⁻¹(X'y − X_gᵀy_g). This needs the per-unit cross-products X_gᵀX_g and X_gᵀy_g, formed once in O(n·k²), plus G k×k solves (MacKinnon, Nielsen & Webb 2023, Eq. 15–16). For a plain mean difference it reduces to O(n) arm-sum updates. Keep the refit loop for nonlinear fits.
- **Current:** n_units full refits, one per deleted unit (observation, cluster, block, pair or matched set), through reusable R6 workers or par_lapply, for every inference class, including ones whose estimate is an OLS, WLS or mean-difference fit.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified against naive re-estimation. MacKinnon, Nielsen & Webb (arXiv 2301.04527, Section 4) benchmark only against other CV3 methods: the jackknife formula is about 12× (k = 20) and 26× (k = 40) faster than the score method for small clusters at N = 2²⁰. Against EDI's n_units refits with R6 overhead, the theoretical saving is the whole refit loop: n_units fits become O(n·k² + G·k³). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** An algebraic identity, so the result matches the refit jackknife to rounding. It needs a per-class hook that exposes the design X and response y, plus any weights, and the identity must match exactly how each class builds its estimate: covariate handling, collinearity column drops after deletion, KK compound or IVWC weights. Classes whose deletion changes the column set, or whose estimate is not a single least-squares solve, must fall back to refits. If X'X − X_gᵀX_g is singular (deleting a unit removes an arm or level), mark the replicate NA the same way the refit path does. Deterministic, with no RNG involved. Downstream BCa â and the jackknife SE are unchanged.
- **Suggested action:** Prototype an exact closed-form jackknife fast path for the least-squares classes (mean difference, OLS and Lin-type, WLS), behind a bit-tolerance test against the refit jackknife. Add the option to the catalog's NEW-jackknife-influence table, which today lists only approximate deletion methods.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_jackknife_distribution_beta_hat_T_private`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::build_jackknife_deletion_draws`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2301.04527v2 https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.04527

### Batch V.2: sequential Monte Carlo stopping (Clopper–Pearson band) → Anytime-valid sequential Monte Carlo tests by betting (Fischer & Ramdas, JRSS-B 2025): the binomial strategy, or the binomial mixture with c<α, plus a futility stop. They generalise Besag–Clifford, give an anytime-valid p-value or decision at any stopping time, and bound resampling risk. A related option the catalog also misses is p-value buckets with bounded resampling risk (Gandy, Hahn & Ding, Scand. J. Stat. 2020).
- **Current:** Batches of max(25, ⌈2√r⌉) draws. After at least mc_min_draws, stop once a per-look Clopper–Pearson (99%) band on the running two-sided MC p-value excludes the threshold. The looks have no multiplicity control, so resampling risk is not bounded across them.
- **Claimed speedup:** From the Fischer & Ramdas arXiv v5 HTML, Section 7.4 (Fisher sharp null, α=0.05, 1000 runs, at most 5000 permutations): binomial strategy mean 85 / median 53 permutations, versus Besag–Clifford mean 200 (h=10) or 160 (h=8); the binomial mixture averaged 147. Section 5.3 (analytic): rejection possible after 39 permutations at c=0.04. Neither paper compares against EDI's Clopper–Pearson band, so the speedup over the current rule is not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The main gain is validity: resampling risk stays bounded under continuous monitoring, which the current rule lacks. A speed gain over EDI's rule is unproven. The stopping rule and the reported early-stop p-value differ from the current ones (the decision should agree outside MC error, but the run would need re-checking against the existing confirm pass). The binomial variants need α fixed in advance, which suits the CI-search use (threshold α or α/2). Deterministic given a seeded draw order, because stopping depends only on the draw sequence, so the per-draw-index seeding is unchanged. No external code dependency; the betting rules take a few lines.
- **Suggested action:** Add Fischer & Ramdas 2025 and Gandy–Hahn–Ding 2020 to the NEW-seq-mc-stopping options table and to performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-156. Before changing anything, A/B the binomial betting stop against the current Clopper–Pearson band on the existing CI-search workloads: compare mean draws and decision agreement with the fixed-r answer.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::compute_two_sided_pval_with_sequential_mc`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R::compute_two_sided_randomization_pval_band`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap.R::compute_two_sided_brt_pval_with_sequential_mc`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap.R::get_brt_distribution_prefix`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_sequential_mc_pval.R::sequential_mc_band_excludes_threshold`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.07365 https://arxiv.org/html/2401.07365v5 https://arxiv.org/abs/1703.09305

### Batch W.1: sign-flip / permutation test with simplex refits → Use the closed form. Minimizing Σρ_τ(y_i − q) over a constant is the sample τ-quantile, so the intercept is an order statistic of signs·yd. Compute it with one std::nth_element (O(m)) in C++, or batch all draws in a single C++ loop with per-draw-index seeds.
- **Current:** With no covariates (p == 0) or when underdetermined (m ≤ p+1), each of the nsim_rand sign-flip draws, and every bisection step of the randomization CI, calls quantreg::rq(yd ~ 1, tau) through the formula interface. That pays for a model.frame plus a Barrodale–Roberts simplex fit for what is a 1-parameter problem.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. It removes the formula/model.frame overhead (the catalog's own 3–10× estimate for rq.fit) and the simplex, leaving an O(m) selection per draw. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same estimator only if the closed form reproduces br's choice when the solution is non-unique (τ·m an integer, e.g. the median of an even number of pairs, where any point in [y_(k), y_(k+1)] minimizes the loss). The rule br uses must be confirmed empirically against rq(yd~1) on tied, even-m and τ·m-integer inputs before switching, otherwise T_obs and the null draws shift. Deterministic if the signs are drawn per draw index. It covers only the intercept-only path; covariate-adjusted draws still need the LP.
- **Suggested action:** Fold into the existing Adopt (v1.0.5 TODO-19 / catalog TODO-6b). Special-case the p == 0 / m ≤ p+1 path of qr_intercept_pairs to an nth_element order statistic after a bit-identity test against quantreg::rq(yd ~ 1, tau) on tied and even-m data. If the tie rule cannot be matched, keep rq.fit.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::qr_intercept_pairs`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::compute_rand_pval_matched_pairs`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::quantile_for_matched_pairs`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantile_regression

### Batch W.1: Frisch–Newton interior point / Barrodale–Roberts simplex → Preprocessing bootstrap (Chernozhukov, Fernández-Val & Melly 2022, Algorithm 4), extending Portnoy–Koenker 1997. Use the full-sample β̂(τ) to predict residual signs in each replicate, solve the reduced LP on the M = m·(kn)^{1/2} uncertain observations plus two glob observations, verify the signs and repair any that are wrong. The result is the exact LP solution.
- **Current:** Each bootstrap or permutation replicate is a cold-start quantreg rq fit (br, or fn for the proportion point fit).
- **Claimed speedup:** ar5iv 1909.05782, bootstrap panel (median regression, k=20, 50 replications): Stata bsqreg simplex 2.63 s vs preprocessing 0.42 s at n=500 (~6×), 3.14 vs 0.63 s at n=1,000 (~5×), 8.19 vs 1.89 s at n=5,000 (~4×). For a single fit with k=8, preprocessing gives no gain at n=500 (fn 0.02 s vs pfn 0.04 s) and pays off only from about n=5,000. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The paper reports results numerically identical to simplex or interior point when no wrong signs are tolerated, but it may select a different vertex when the solution is non-unique. The benchmark is against Stata's simplex with k=20, not quantreg br with EDI's small p (≈2–10), where br is already fast. Permutation and sign-flip draws under a shifted null are less well predicted by the observed β̂ than bootstrap draws are, so more sign repairs are needed. It needs custom LP code (quantreg's pfn does not take a user-supplied initial estimate), and the gain only appears for large reservoirs.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look only if profiling shows replicate QR fits dominate at n ≳ 1,000 after the rq.fit Adopt lands. Otherwise record it in the catalog's options table as considered: exact, but no evidence of a gain at EDI's small p and n.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_proportion_quantile_regr.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_continuous_quantile_regr.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::qr_trt_coef_reservoir`
- **Sources:** https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1909.05782 https://boris.unibe.ch/150869

### Batch X.1: IRLS M-estimator (Huber / bisquare) → Finite-termination Newton for Huber's M-estimator (Madsen & Nielsen 1990; Chen & Pınar 1998, BIT). It exploits the piecewise-quadratic Huber objective: once the active set (|r_i|≤c·σ) is right, one Newton step lands exactly on the minimizer. Each iteration factorizes XᵀDX over the inlier set, at the same O(np²) as IRLS.
- **Current:** IRLS with Huber weights at fixed MAD scale, LDLT WLS each iteration. It converges linearly; KK randomization draws cap maxit at 10–25 with acc 1e-3/1e-4, so those fits are truncated.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The Chen & Pınar abstract says the enhancements 'improve its efficiency' and prove finite termination with no assumptions, but gives no numbers and no IRLS comparison. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Huber only; bisquare is non-convex, so Newton's issues stand there. Same unique minimizer at fixed scale, so it is deterministic and the answer matches within tolerance. For truncated KK draws (maxit 10–25) the answer could differ, by becoming exact. Needs step control for rank-deficient inlier sets. Catalog O8 rejected 'Newton instead of IRLS' citing only the need for safeguards; this work removes that concern for Huber.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look at catalog O8's 'Newton instead of IRLS' row for the Huber ('M') path only. Prototype a Madsen–Nielsen/Chen–Pınar active-set Newton and A/B its iteration counts against IRLS at EDI's sizes (n≈50–1000, p≲10), especially for the KK randomization draws.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_robust_regression.cpp::fast_robust_regression_internal`, `R/EDI/R/inference_continuous_KK_robust_regr_one_lik.R::fit_rlm`, `R/EDI/R/inference_continuous_KK_robust_regr_ivwc.R::fit_rlm_with_treatment`
- **Sources:** https://repository.bilkent.edu.tr/items/01e2b27c-57e2-48a5-ac3f-aa024da2221d

### Batch X.1: IRLS M-estimator (Huber / bisquare) → Deterministic Peña–Yohai (1999, JASA) initial estimator for the S-step: candidates from principal-sensitivity-component cleaning, then refinement steps (refine.PY=10 by default), then the bisquare/mopt M-step at the S-scale. It is implemented in RobStatTM::lmrobdetMM (v1.0.11, GPL ≥3) and pyinit (v1.1.5, 2026-01, GPL-2|3).
- **Current:** 'MM' is bisquare IRLS from an OLS start with the scale fixed once (no S-step). The planned fix (v1.0.5 algorithm_choice_audit TODO-31) is a true MM with a FAST-S start, which catalog O8 marks as RNG-dependent and ~50–200× slower. Its 'deterministic S/MM start' option still reads 'regression variant [cite needed]'.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI's sizes. The 1999 paper targets large p where subsampling is infeasible, and none of the pages I fetched gives timings against FAST-S. It is almost certainly slower than today's mislabeled bisquare-M, since it adds an S-step. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** An estimator change relative to the current output: it gives a true MM with 50% breakdown, which is what v1.0.5 algorithm_choice_audit TODO-31 intends anyway. Compared with the FAST-S prototype it removes the RNG/seed contract (fully deterministic, so thread-count-invariant). The estimate can differ from robustbase::lmrob (FAST-S), so parity tests need RobStatTM as the reference. Breakdown guarantees for the PY start are heuristic rather than proven.
- **Suggested action:** Fill the catalog O8 '[cite needed]' with Peña & Yohai 1999 / RobStatTM lmrobdetMM. In the v1.0.5 algorithm_choice_audit TODO-31 MM prototype, A/B the deterministic PY start against FAST-S on per-fit time at n≈50–1000, p≲10, and on breakdown simulations. If comparable, prefer it, since it needs no seed contract.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_robust_regression.cpp::fast_robust_regression_internal`, `R/EDI/R/inference_continuous_robust_regr.R::fit_rlm_model`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/RobStatTM/refman/RobStatTM.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=RobStatTM https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/pyinit/refman/pyinit.html

### Batch Y.1: per-draw NPMLE re-solve in randomization loop → (a) Interim step using only the existing interval package: compute the pooled icfit once and pass it as icFIT= to every per-draw ictest call. On the per-draw estimate-only calls, also pass method='pclt' (or exact=FALSE) so no exact p-value is computed per draw. (b) The full v1.0.5 TODO-11 Adopt: get the scores c_i once with interval::wlr_trafo(L, R, scores=..., icFIT=pooled_fit), then compute each draw's statistic as a linear form in w (one matrix product over the draw matrix).
- **Current:** Each randomization draw calls interval::ictest(L, R, w, scores=...) from scratch. That re-solves the identical pooled Turnbull NPMLE by EM, and when n ≤ 20 the default methodRuleIC1 also picks method='exact.network', so every draw runs an exact network-algorithm permutation p-value that EDI then discards.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. The interval manual only says icFIT is 'for increased computation speed'. The audit's own estimate for (b) is about r× fewer NPMLE solves (≈500× at r=501); that is a theoretical figure, not a measurement. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The manual warns that ictest does no checks on icFIT, so a wrong fit gives wrong answers silently. It is valid only for randomization draws at δ=0, where the pooled (L,R) do not depend on the labels; it does not apply to bootstrap draws, which resample rows. Before dropping the exact method on per-draw calls, check that fit$estimate does not depend on method (the manual says $statistic is NULL for exact methods). The observed-data p-value call must keep the default method so the reported p-value does not change. Draws stay deterministic, since no RNG is involved.
- **Suggested action:** When implementing v1.0.5 TODO-11, use wlr_trafo(icFIT=) for the scores and check them against ictest's estimate on fixtures. If TODO-11 slips, ship the interim icFIT= plus method='pclt' change on per-draw estimate-only calls first.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_log_rank.R::compute_shared_icen`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_gehan_wilcox.R::compute_shared_icen`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/interval/interval.pdf

### Batch Y.1: Turnbull NPMLE (EM) → Go straight to icenReg::ic_np: EMICM in C++ (RcppEigen) with O(n) work per iteration, a weights= argument since 2.0.8, and icenReg is already an EDI dependency. Skip the initEMICM intermediate step, because the Icens EMICM it relies on is the slow implementation in this benchmark.
- **Current:** interval::icfit: plain self-consistency EM from a simple start, eps 1e-6, maxit 10,000, dense A-matrix products in R. Two fits per call inside the B-replicate resampling loops. v1.0.5 TODO-28 plans icfit(initfit='initEMICM') first and icenReg::ic_np second.
- **Claimed speedup:** icenReg JSS paper, Table 1 (simIC_weib data, 100 reps): ic_np mean 0.001 s at n=100 and 0.003 s at n=1,000; Icens::EMICM 0.106 s and 8.319 s on the same data (~100× and ~2,700×). interval::icfit's own EM is not in that table, so the gain over EDI's current path is not quantified directly. For context, Wang & Taylor 2013 (Table 3, R implementations, one larger dataset) report EM at 9,018 iterations / 657 s against hybrid ICM-EM at 29 s. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same NPMLE likelihood, but ic_np converges to a tighter tolerance and the NPMLE masses can be non-unique, so the median and RMST functionals need an A/B check (the catalog already notes this). Results are deterministic. GPL-compatible. The benchmark compares ic_np with Icens, not with interval::icfit, so the size of the gain over EDI's current EM is an inference. Also checked and not proposed: CNM/HCNM (npsurv, GPL, has w=), which is pure R; its published times (0.044–0.056 s at n=400, Wang & Taylor Table 4) are not better than ic_np's.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look at the order of v1.0.5 TODO-28 (release_v1_1_0.md TODO-50): prototype icenReg::ic_np first, with an A/B harness on median/RMST against icfit, and drop or deprioritize the initEMICM step.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_inference_survival_turnbull.R::turnbull_npmle_group_stat`, `R/EDI/R/helper_inference_survival_turnbull.R::turnbull_npmle_stat_diff`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_log_rank.R::compute_shared_icen`
- **Sources:** https://www.jstatsoft.org/article/view/v081i12/1168 https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/icenReg/NEWS https://openrepository.aut.ac.nz/bitstreams/64852c7a-27ea-48c2-8a28-44eb5dcac4fa/download https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/npsurv/npsurv.pdf

### Batch Z.1: Fisher scoring (IRLS) with step-halving → Write the log-binomial (and identity-binomial) MLE as an exponential-cone program: the log-likelihood is a sum of log(μ) and log(1−μ) terms with linear constraints on Xβ. Solve it with a primal-dual interior-point conic solver; ECOS (GPL-3), Clarabel (Apache-2.0) and SCS (MIT) are all GPL-3 compatible. Such a solver needs no feasible start, handles boundary MLEs natively and returns KKT multipliers as duals.
- **Current:** Fisher-scoring IRLS with step-halving against the −∞ barrier, starting from a possibly infeasible point. At the bounds it adds an active-set projection, a KKT gate and a cycle guard. This certifies boundary MLEs but is not a true constrained solver (Prototype, v1.0.5 TODO-32).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in what I could fetch. The 2021 abstract (Schwendinger, Grün & Hornik, Comput Stat 36(3):1721–1754) names conic optimizers the preferred choice for reliability, no tuning and speed, but the runtime tables are behind a paywall. A 2024 talk on the same group's follow-up says conic solvers were more accurate and that the ROI interface added runtime overhead. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** For interior MLEs, warm-started IRLS (a few LDLT solves) is very likely faster than an interior-point solve, so the conic route probably wins only on boundary or infeasible-start fits. A hybrid would run IRLS first and switch to the conic solver when the KKT gate fails or the start is infeasible. It adds a C/C++ solver dependency. The interior-point tolerance must be tight enough to match the IRLS MLE within numerical tolerance. Results are deterministic, since the solver is single-threaded and does no random draws.
- **Suggested action:** Add 'exponential-cone interior point (ECOS/Clarabel)' to the catalog §1 O4 options table. In the v1.0.5 TODO-32 log-binomial constrained-solver Prototype, benchmark it as a fallback for the boundary or infeasible-start fits that IRLS fails to certify, at EDI's n and p. Get the paper's runtime tables before deciding.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_log_binomial_regression.cpp::fit_constrained_binomial_cpp_impl`, `R/EDI/src/fast_log_binomial_regression.cpp::fit_constrained_binomial_weighted_cpp_impl`, `R/EDI/src/fast_log_binomial_regression.cpp::project_increment_off_active_bounds`, `R/EDI/src/fast_log_binomial_regression.cpp::kkt_verdict_at_bounds`
- **Sources:** https://ideas.repec.org/a/spr/compst/v36y2021i3d10.1007_s00180-021-01084-5.html https://rbstats.netlify.app/project_files/agll_files/_iwsm24/iwsm24_pres https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/BSW/refman/BSW.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=logbin

### Batch AA.1: deterministic multistart → For the cumulative cloglog model, F(z) = 1 − exp(−exp(z)) has a log-concave density, so the log-likelihood is concave in the raw (α, β) parametrisation EDI uses (Pratt 1981, as cited in Kosmidis arXiv 1204.0105; that paper covers the G = 1 − exp(−exp(η)) case explicitly). When the MLE exists it is unique, and a restart that finds a strictly lower NLL means the primary solve stopped early, not that it found a different local optimum. Two cheaper checks fit this: (a) a convergence certificate on the primary fit, either the Newton decrement already in ordinal_scale_guard.h or a maximum-absolute-gradient plus positive-definite-Hessian test like ordinal::clm's maxGradient/cond.H, running the restarts only when it fails; or (b) a damped Newton polish from the primary solution, which is what clm does with step halving and gradTol 1e-6.
- **Current:** After the primary cumulative-cloglog fit, generate_mod always runs 4 more full fits: the treatment coefficient set to 0, reflected, and shifted by -1 and +1. It does this on both the variance path and the estimate_only path, so every randomization or bootstrap draw costs about 5× a single fit, plus a with_var refit when a restart wins.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. Based on the code structure, it avoids up to 4 extra fits per call when the primary fit passes the certificate, so up to about 5× fewer fits per estimate_only resampling draw. This is a theoretical figure, not a measurement. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This holds only where the MLE exists. Under quasi-separation the NLL decreases along a ray, the certificate fails, and the restarts would still run, so behaviour is unchanged there. The v1.0.5 bug-fix plan blamed the problem on an 'inferior local optimum', which for a concave objective must be early stopping or non-convergence, so the fix should be validated against the cases that motivated the multistart. Results could differ at the 1e-6 tolerance level from the current lowest-NLL choice. This is the same issue the catalog already marks 'Investigate' for the cloglog null refits (catalog §… line 1817), but the full-data multistart added on 2026-11-16 has not been audited yet.
- **Suggested action:** Re-examine the cloglog multistart. Gate the 4 restarts on a failed convergence certificate (gradient norm or Newton decrement with a PD Hessian), or replace them with a Newton polish. Before changing the default, A/B the result against the current multistart on the cases in ordinal_cumulative_link_null_refit_multistart.md. Apply the same reasoning to the cloglog fit_null 2-start, which is already marked Investigate.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_cloglog.R::.cloglog_full_data_multistart`
- **Sources:** https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1204.0105 https://arxiv.org/abs/1204.0105 https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/refman/ordinal.html

### Batch AB.1: CR2 (Bell–McCaffrey) sandwich + Satterthwaite df → The exact p×p formulation. Take a QR (or (XᵀX)^{-1/2}) once. Per cluster, take the p×p spectral decomposition Q_gᵀQ_g = Σ λ_i r_i r_iᵀ and set D_g = Σ_{λ_i≠1} (1−λ_i)^{-1/2} r_i r_iᵀ. Then A_gᵀQ_g = Q_g D_g (dfadjust vignette Lemma 2, a generalized inverse, so clusters with I−H_gg singular are handled). The full CR2 meat follows as s_g = Rᵀ D_g Q_gᵀ e_g, the same idea as the 'score' method of Niccodemi et al. 2020 as revised in MacKinnon–Nielsen–Webb eq. 12–13. For the df, a_g = Q_g D_g ℓ̃ and B (G×p) with B_gk = a_gᵀQ_{g,k}. G'G = diag(a_gᵀa_g) − BBᵀ, so f_BM = tr(G'G)²/tr((G'G)²) needs only G×p and p×p matrices. Total O(np² + Gp³), with no n_g×n_g or n×G objects.
- **Current:** For each cluster, an R loop forms M = I − X_g(XᵀX)⁻¹X_gᵀ (n_g×n_g) and runs eigen() on it, keeping a pseudo-inverse square root of eigenvalues above 1e-10·max. It then fills a dense n×G matrix Umat, with one X %*% (·) per cluster, and takes crossprod(Umat) for the Bell–McCaffrey df. Cost is O(Σ n_g³ + nGp + nG²) plus R per-cluster overhead.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI's sizes. MacKinnon, Nielsen & Webb (arXiv 2301.04527, §4/Fig. 1, N=2^20, k=20/40, Fortran) find the n_g×n_g residual method fastest only for cluster size M ≤ 4. It became 'prohibitively costly' above M = 4096, while the k×k score method is 'much faster … unless all the clusters are very small'. The dfadjust vignette says the p×p route is what makes large clusters feasible, but gives no timing. A 2025 blog benchmark (160 clusters, HSB data) shows the k×k NCR2 agreeing with clubSandwich to 7 decimals, but its median time is not faster than clubSandwich's. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an algebraic identity, so it is the same estimator and the same df (Lemma 2 also covers the generalized-inverse case). The eigenvalue cutoff moves from 'eigenvalue of I−H_gg ≤ 1e-10·max' to '1−λ_i ≤ tol' on the p×p side, which can differ only in near-singular clusters. Check those with an A/B test against the current code and clubSandwich. Deterministic, with no license issue: it is a re-derivation from papers, not a code copy (dfadjust is MIT). The win shows up only when clusters are larger than a handful of subjects (typical of cluster-randomized designs) and n_g > p. With n in the hundreds to low thousands and G in the tens, the current cost is already small (milliseconds). It matters only if this function runs per resampling draw (e.g. a studentized bootstrap), which I did not confirm. The R-level loop of eigen() calls also adds per-cluster overhead that the p×p version shares.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look. First check whether cluster_robust_cr2_variance runs inside any resampling loop and what cluster sizes the cluster-capable designs use. If clusters typically have >4 subjects or the function is called per draw, prototype the p×p Lemma-2 meat and the B-matrix df. A/B test against the current implementation and clubSandwich::coef_test(vcov='CR2', test='Satterthwaite'), including a cluster-fixed-effect case where I−H_gg is singular. Otherwise leave as is; this is a speed tidy-up, not a correctness issue.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_robust_sandwich.R::cluster_robust_cr2_variance`
- **Sources:** https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2301.04527 https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dfadjust/vignettes/dfadjust.pdf https://pure.rug.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/112981601/2020002_EEF_def.pdf https://francish.netlify.app/post/cr2_2025

### Batch AC.1: full re-imputation per arrival → Call missRanger(..., keep_forests = TRUE) once, or every k arrivals. Impute each new arrival with predict(imp, newdata = x_new) from the stored forests, and refit the forests only every k arrivals or when columns or levels change. This needs no new dependency, since missRanger ≥ 2.6.0 is already imported. Per the 2.6.0 NEWS, out-of-sample imputation is cheapest for rows with one missing value; rows with several missing values may need iter > 1.
- **Current:** A full missRanger (missForest fallback) chained random-forest imputation over all t rows, including y, plus a model.matrix rebuild on every arrival. That is n forest imputations in total.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. Structurally, about k times fewer forest fits per arrival, with only a predict for the new row in between. This matches the catalog §12(e) row, which had no reference. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Estimator change: imputations of past subjects no longer drift as responses arrive (catalog incidental finding 13 argues this is arguably more correct), and the new row's imputation comes from a model fit on earlier rows. Keeping the forests costs a lot of memory (the vignette advises smaller num.trees, max.depth or larger min.node.size, which change the forests too). The missForest fallback has no predict path. Changes design output, so it needs the A/B harness under v1.0.5-TODO-9.
- **Suggested action:** Add missRanger keep_forests/predict as the concrete, dependency-free implementation of the 'stored forest, refit every k' option in v1.0.5-TODO-9, and cite the missRanger 2.6.0 NEWS in catalog §12(e).
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_seq_one_by_one_abstract.R::add_one_subject`, `R/EDI/R/design_abstract.R::covariate_impute_if_necessary_and_then_create_model_matrix`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/missRanger/news/news.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/missRanger/vignettes/missRanger.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=missRanger

### Batch AC.1: interleaved A/B timing, unpaired median rule → Analyze the per-round paired differences a_i − b_i, as zenbench does: a Wilcoxon signed-rank p-value plus a 10k-resample bootstrap percentile CI on the differences. Accept only when the CI excludes zero and lies entirely outside a ±noise band (zenbench default 1%; EDI could keep 5%). Stop adaptively once the CI is narrow and stable, with a minimum number of rounds. perfgate-paired uses the same alternate-and-difference design. Use a fixed seed for the bootstrap so results stay deterministic.
- **Current:** Interleaved ABAB timing analyzed unpaired: accept if Δmedian ≥ 5% and > 2·IQR(candidate), with a fixed number of repetitions and no multiplicity control.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The zenbench methodology only says paired tests on per-round differences have 'far more power' than unpaired tests. The only number it quotes is Google Benchmark's figure of about 40% variance reduction from random interleaving, which EDI already does. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Changes which candidates the tuner accepts (intended: fewer false accepts), but does not change any package statistical result. A CI-width stop gives a variable number of repetitions. Repeated looks need a sequential correction (group-sequential or anytime-valid) to keep the error rate, and multiplicity across hundreds of cells still needs handling separately. The zenbench docs note that Tukey outlier trimming can hide bimodal timings.
- **Suggested action:** No verdict change. Use this as the design reference for the paired-difference half of v1.0.5-TODO-12: paired Wilcoxon or bootstrap CI on the differences, a noise-band acceptance rule, and adaptive stopping with a sequential correction.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/local_machine_tuning_harness.R::edi_tuning_accept_candidate`, `R/EDI/R/local_machine_tuning_harness.R::edi_tuning_blocked_ab`, `R/EDI/R/local_machine_tuning_harness.R::edi_tuning_interleaved_ab`
- **Sources:** https://docs.rs/crate/zenbench/0.1.1/source/METHODOLOGY.md

## 2. Inventory currency

### Applied in this pull request (`algorithm_inventory.csv`, `algorithm_references.csv`)
- Added `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_stereotype_logit.R` `.stereotype_null_refit_multistart`: deterministic multistart (row AA)
- Added `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_dep_cens_transform.R` `dep_cens_multistart_fit`: deterministic multistart (row AA)
- Added `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_KK_cond_logit_glmm_abstract.R` `fit_clogit_reservoir_logit`: clogit-as-logistic reduction + Newton-Raphson (IRLS) (row A)
- Added `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand.R` `compute_additive_delta_shift_slopes`: closed-form affine-shift slopes (per-permutation) (row D)
- Added `R/EDI/src/fast_zinb.cpp` `identified_pseudo_inverse_free_information`: eigendecomposition pseudo-inverse (row U)

### Needs a human (not applied)
_Nothing._

<details><summary>Algorithms reviewed with no acceptable faster option (376)</summary>

- Batch A.1: alternating IRLS / Newton (glm.nb): no_faster_acceptable_found. MASS::glm.nb is used only on the KK21 stepwise fallback path (design_seq_one_by_one_KK21_stepwise.R:352). fastglm 0.1.4 (CRAN, 2026-11-11, GPL (>= 2)) fits the same NB2 MLE (IRLS for β alternating with a θ update, plus step-halving) and converges more often (200/200 vs 165/200 at n=200, p=9). Its vignettes report no timings, so the gain is reliability, not proven speed. Recorded as a low-confidence re-look below.
- Batch A.1: analytic NLL + gradient: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form O(n·p) objective and gradient (ZIP/hurdle-Poisson, truncated NB, ZOIB, ZINB), already using distinct-y special-function tables. No asymptotically or practically faster way to evaluate an exact likelihood exists. The dead `mu` pass in ZOIB is already recorded as incidental.
- Batch A.1: analytic observed information: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form Hessians costing O(n·p²) (LMM trace identities, hurdle NB, ZIP/hurdle, ZOIB block-diagonal). The cost equals forming XᵀWX, which is optimal for an exact Hessian. Finite differences or BFGS approximations would be less accurate.
- Batch A.1: BFGS (MASS::polr): no_faster_acceptable_found. The faster alternatives are a Newton-type solver (ordinal::clm's modified Newton supports weights and logit/probit/cloglog/cauchit; checked in the CRAN ordinal manual) or EDI's own C++ weighted ordinal kernel. Both are already in the catalog (§2 line 393) and in this entry's Investigate note. I found no new benchmark at EDI's sizes; the one public comparison (StackOverflow, p≈100) had polr faster than clm.
- Batch A.1: BFGS (optim): no_faster_acceptable_found. KK cond-Poisson weighted replicate (still optim BFGS at inference_count_KK_cond_poisson.R:2139): the C++ Newton with a weights argument is already Adopt (catalog §10, TODO-2), still unimplemented. PPO polr rung: last rung of a robustness ladder, not hot. Nothing new beyond the tracked item.
- Batch A.1: bootstrap refits (L-BFGS, zero start): no_faster_acceptable_found. The code is unchanged (params0.setZero(), "lbfgs", fast_weibull_regression.cpp:706-713). The known improvement (Newton with the in-file analytic Hessian, plus a warm start at the observed MLE) is already Prototype, catalog §5 TODO-3. The literature's only further speedup is the Davidson–MacKinnon k-step bootstrap, which is an approximation (an estimator change), so it is not acceptable. LBFGSpp releases (through v0.4.0) list no performance changes.
- Batch A.1: bordered normal equations + column-pivoted QR: no_faster_acceptable_found. Both kernels are dead code. The permutation kernel's FWL rewrite, O(B(n_T p + p²)), is already tracked (ols_distr_kernel_fwl.md, v1.2.0-TODO-9). The bootstrap kernel's O(np²) per draw is inherent to resampling rows (catalog P5).
- Batch A.2: boundary convergence certificate: no_faster_acceptable_found. Costs 2 extra objective evaluations after a fit, which is negligible next to the L-BFGS fit. Reparametrizing to α=1/θ, which would remove the need for it, is already recorded in the catalog (ZINB TODO 4) as a robustness change, not a speedup. Nothing cheaper certifies the θ→∞ limit.
- Batch A.2: Breslow partial likelihood (reverse cumulative sum): no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_cox_neg_ll_only is O(np) per evaluation. That is the lower bound, since every subject's η has to be read. The survival NEWS (cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/news.html) shows only a score-residual speedup in 3.5-7, from O(ndp) to O((n+d)p), and EDI's compute_robust_vcov already matches it with prefix sums. Efron is an accuracy option, not a speed one (row L).
- Batch A.2: clogit-as-logistic reduction: no_faster_acceptable_found. This is the exact identity: 1:1 conditional logit equals no-intercept logistic regression on within-pair differences of discordant pairs. Building the design is O(n_d p), which is optimal. A search found no newer fast 1:1 clogit method; recent work (e.g. clogitLasso) targets penalized or large-p cases.
- Batch A.2: clogit-as-logistic reduction + Newton–Raphson (IRLS): no_faster_acceptable_found. After the exact reduction the problem is a strictly concave logistic MLE with p in the tens. Newton/IRLS converges quadratically at O(np²) per iteration, which is the row-A Keep rationale. GLR recursion and enumeration both collapse to the same 2-term sum when m=2.
- Batch A.2: closed form: no_faster_acceptable_found. These are direct O(1), O(n) or O(np²) formulas: link cdf/pdf, analytic scores and Hessians, Wald/RD/BRS variances, ridit DeLong SE, lgamma sums. Each is at the lower bound of reading its inputs. Some are dead exports (beta_*_cpp, neg_loglik_nb_cpp). The micro-optimizations already noted (distinct-y lgamma caching in NegBinNoZiLikelihood, SYRK/weighted_crossprod reuse) are constant-factor and trivial.
- Batch A.2: closed form (log-sum-exp): no_faster_acceptable_found. The adjacent-category and stereotype log-likelihoods use a max-shifted log-sum-exp in O(n(K+p)). It is overflow-safe and cannot be done in less than linear time. Nothing faster that is also as accurate.
- Batch A.2: closed form (Pearson dispersion): no_faster_acceptable_found. Pearson χ²/(n−p) times the diagonal of the inverse is O(n + p³), the standard quasi-Poisson estimator (as in glm quasipoisson). Nothing faster.
- Batch A.3: closed-form expected information: no_faster_acceptable_found. Both inventoried entries (ZAP and ZOIB expected_hessian) are closed forms at O(n k²), the minimum work for an information matrix. Both are dead code with no caller, so speeding them up gains nothing.
- Batch A.3: closed-form score / LR / gradient statistics: no_faster_acceptable_found. These are closed forms computed from one null fit. The only cost is the p×p efficient-information step in score_test_from_score_information (_helper_functions.h:153), which forms covariance_from_information(I_nn) and then multiplies. A Cholesky solve would use about 3x fewer flops at p³, but p is in the tens and this runs once per test, not per replicate. That is a micro-choice already covered by performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155. No faster algorithm exists for the statistic itself.
- Batch A.3: cluster-robust sandwich + delta method: no_faster_acceptable_found. The cost is O(np² + p³): one pass of cluster score sums plus a p×p bread, which every sandwich needs. Grouping with unordered_map<int,VectorXd> allocates one vector per cluster; dense integer-indexed grouping would be a constant-factor micro-optimization, not an algorithm change. The search turned up no faster method.
- Batch A.3: conditional logit (discordant-pair logistic): no_faster_acceptable_found. For 1:1 strata the exact conditional likelihood reduces to a no-intercept logistic fit on within-pair differences, at O(n_d p²) per Newton step. That is already minimal and exact. A 2026 search found only estimator changes (the bclogit empirical-Bayes prior, arXiv 2602.08212) and penalized coordinate descent (clogitL1). Neither is a same-estimator speedup.
- Batch A.3: constrained Newton refits (score / LR tests): no_faster_acceptable_found. The constrained refits already use C++ Newton with fixed_idx and are warm-started (get_backend_warm_start_args / start=), and Newton is optimal for a concave Poisson likelihood. The alternatives found are a bootstrap-calibrated Bartlett correction (spaMM/pbkrtest) and the modified approximate quasi-LR of Noma et al. 2025. Both change the test, so they are estimator changes, not speedups.
- Batch A.3: Cox fit + cluster-robust sandwich: no_faster_acceptable_found. The sandwich is computed with prefix sums and a binary search at O(n log K + np), on top of an O(np²) Newton fit, so nothing is asymptotically better. survival 3.8-x NEWS shows no algorithm change. The faster score residuals in survival date from 3.5-7, and EDI already uses the equivalent cumulative-sum form. mets::phreg (1.3.12, 2026-07-30) makes no speed claim. Recomputing the ≤13-rung candidate ladder on every call is a caching incidental already recorded in the inventory, not an algorithm choice.
- Batch A.3: Cox partial-likelihood Newton / L-BFGS (Breslow ties): no_faster_acceptable_found. EDI already sorts once and sweeps S0/S1/S2 in reverse in one pass, O(np² + Kp²) per Newton iteration. coxstream 0.1.0 (CRAN, 2026-06-20) uses the same single-descending-pass Newton, aims at memory rather than speed, makes no speed claim and uses Efron ties, which would be an estimator change. The only open idea is BLAS-3 Hessian assembly, already in catalog §5 options (tolerance-equal, about 1.5–3x on assembly at p≥5, theoretical).
- Batch A.4: Cox partial-likelihood Newton–Raphson (Efron ties): no_faster_acceptable_found. Newton–Raphson with step-halving is still the standard fitter for an unpenalized Cox model at small p. The survival NEWS (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/news.html) shows no coxph algorithm change from 3.6 to the latest release; the only ties entry is a 3.7-2 test-suite fix. FastSurvival (arXiv 2410.19081) targets high-dimensional and sparse Cox models, and its abstract gives no small-p benchmark. Swapping this call for the in-house C++ cox_newton_raphson would be faster but moves from Efron to Breslow, an estimator change that is already tracked as the 'Investigate' / row L item.
- Batch A.4: damped Newton–Raphson (LDLT, step-halving): no_faster_acceptable_found. cox_newton_raphson: Newton with LDLT on a concave partial likelihood at p ≤ ~10 converges in about 4–8 iterations. That is the optimal local rate, and the survival package uses the same method. Neither the survival NEWS nor the FastSurvival abstract (arXiv 2410.19081, aimed at high-dimensional and sparse Cox) gives a small-p alternative that beats it.
- Batch A.4: damped Newton–Raphson with L-BFGS fallback: no_faster_acceptable_found. The generic MLE driver. The only open change is the linear-algebra micro-choice FullPivLU → LDLT/LLT (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155), plus the indefinite-Hessian safeguard covered under 'damped Newton with step-halving'. Gratton et al.'s small-problem profiles (119 OPM/CUTEst problems with 2–49 variables) show regularized Newton, AR2 and trust region within about 0.01 of each other in performance-profile area. No newer method changes the Keep verdict.
- Batch A.4: deterministic start grid: no_faster_acceptable_found. At most 12 closed-form starts (OLS on log/log1p × moment/Pearson θ), O(S·np). Building them is negligible next to the fits, and nothing cheaper gives the same robustness. Multistart policy belongs to multistart_nonconcave_likelihoods.md / row B.
- Batch A.4: escalating ridge regularization: no_faster_acceptable_found. Up to 8 solves of a (p−1)×(p−1) nuisance block in R, called once per score test; the cost is trivial next to the model fits. One eigendecomposition reused across all ridge levels would save at most 7 small solves, a win that is not material at EDI's p. I found no source for a faster method with the same answer. Its calibration is the open question, and that is not a speed issue.
- Batch A.4: exact Gaussian LMM likelihood + GLS fast path: no_faster_acceptable_found. The closed-form compound-symmetry marginal likelihood is O(n·p) per evaluation, and the frozen-variance-component GLS replicate path is O(np²+p³). Nothing is cheaper for the same estimator. lme4 NEWS 1.1-36 to 2.1-0 (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/lme4/news.html) and glmmTMB NEWS (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/glmmTMB/news.html) add no new LMM fitting algorithm. Profiled 1-D Brent (catalog §4 TODO-3) remains the tracked robustness prototype.
- Batch A.4: fallback ladder of PPO fitters: no_faster_acceptable_found. ordinal NEWS since 2023 (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/NEWS) has no clm algorithm or nominal-effect changes. VGAM 1.1-14 (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/VGAM/NEWS) adds an optional 'bhhh' outer-product Hessian to vglm.control, an alternative information approximation with no claimed speedup. The ladder is a robustness device against improper PPO likelihoods, so Keep stands.
- Batch A.5: FD Hessian + LU inversion: no_faster_acceptable_found. Only used for the ZINB vcov once per non-estimate-only fit (fast_zinb.cpp skips the Hessian when estimate_only=TRUE), so it is not in a resampling hot path. An analytic observed-information Hessian exists elsewhere (fastglm 0.1.0 NEWS: fastglm_zi has an 'analytical observed-information vcov'). It would save about 2k gradient evaluations of digamma-heavy work, but nobody has quantified that gain, and both cost O(n k²). The O(k³) LU is negligible at k in the tens. The ZINB optimizer and Hessian question stays tracked under row B.
- Batch A.5: Fisher scoring (IRLS): no_faster_acceptable_found. Probit (non-canonical): Fisher scoring needs about 5–10 iterations and has a PSD information matrix; observed-info Newton differs only in the local rate, and no source quantifies an iteration gain for binary probit. fastglm 0.1.4 (2026-11-11) also uses bounded Fisher-scoring updates for binary probit. LDLT on p×p is already the cheapest solve (catalog O2). Step-halving is already an Adopt item (TODO-2), so nothing new.
- Batch A.5: g-computation + delta method: no_faster_acceptable_found. Ordinal PO g-comp: plug-in standardization is O(n(p+K)) and the central-FD gradient costs 2(p+K−1) cheap post-fit calls. The analytic gradient is already recorded (catalog §2 TODO-5) as a deterministic, low-priority drop-in. I found no faster method for the same estimator.
- Batch A.5: g-computation + delta-method sandwich: no_faster_acceptable_found. One logistic or fractional-logit fit plus an O(np) standardization and an O(p²) analytic delta-method gradient is the minimal work for this estimator. CRAN 'risks' (0.4.3) and stdReg use the same standardization + delta method. The FD fallback fires only on failure. The covariate-sampling variance term (Incidental 16) is an estimand question, not a speed one.
- Batch A.5: HC0 sandwich + delta method: no_faster_acceptable_found. LDLT of XᵀWX plus an O(np²) meat is the standard cost of HC0 (sandwich vcovHC and fastglm vcovHC use the same formulas). At p in the tens the O(p³) inverse is negligible, and nothing beats O(np²) for building the meat.
- Batch A.5: Hessian-scaled gradient-norm stopping test: no_faster_acceptable_found. This check runs only on the line-search-failure exit of the generic Newton (not per iteration). Its cost is dominated by one Hessian evaluation, and a values-only symmetric eigensolve at p in the tens costs microseconds. An affine-invariant Newton-decrement test (gᵀH⁻¹g via one LDLT, as row AA kernels already do) would be slightly cheaper (p³/3 vs ~4p³/3 flops). But it changes which stalled fits are flagged as converged, so it is a policy change, not a speedup. I fetched no readable source for it this run, so I am not reporting it as a finding.
- Batch A.5: Householder QR (lm.fit) + HC2: no_faster_acceptable_found. A single Lin fit is O(np²) in compiled LINPACK QR, followed by the C++ HC2 post-fit. The fastglm benchmark vignette shows LLT-Cholesky about 2× faster than pivoted QR at n=1e3, p=30 (logistic: 0.67 vs 1.26 ms), but per fit that is sub-millisecond and costs accuracy on ill-conditioned X. The real cost is the per-draw R loop, which is already a Prototype item (C++ batch kernel, catalog §10).
- Batch A.6: independent univariate GLM fits: no_faster_acceptable_found. KK21 non-stepwise weights: p independent 2-parameter fits at tiny m, which is already the minimal structure (O(p·fit)). No closed form exists for univariate beta/NB/Weibull MLEs. The stepwise refit cost is a separate Prototype (catalog P3) that changes the weight values, so it is not a drop-in.
- Batch A.6: IRLS: no_faster_acceptable_found. Only the GEE independence starting β (logistic/Poisson IRLS, about 5–8 iterations of O(np²)). For canonical-link GLMs at p in the tens, IRLS is Newton with quadratic convergence. The step-halving and LDLT/LLT micro-choices are already catalogued (O1/O2). This is a one-off start, not a hot path.
- Batch A.6: joint Newton–Raphson (β, log θ): no_faster_acceptable_found. NB2 joint fit. The alternatives (Fisher scoring/alternation, glm.nb, profile-θ Brent, moment θ) are already in catalog O6 / v1.0.5 TODO-5. The fastglm_nb 2026 vignettes use the same IRLS/θ alternation and give convergence rates but no timings (already reported by batch A.1). Yang et al. arXiv:2404.07457 (APMA) covers iid NB only, with no covariates, so it does not apply.
- Batch A.6: L-BFGS: no_faster_acceptable_found. The generic LBFGSpp driver uses backtracking strong-Wolfe. The Newton alternative for the concave ordinal/CR/AC/beta/logistic fits is already catalogued (catalog §2 TODO-1/2, §1 TODO-7). LBFGSpp releases add a More–Thuente / Nocedal–Wright line search but no performance claim. The literature found (arXiv 1911.04584, 2310.07296) suggests Wolfe/More–Thuente searches use more evaluations than Armijo backtracking, so switching the line search is not a demonstrated win.
- Batch A.6: L-BFGS (closed-form Clayton copula likelihood): no_faster_acceptable_found. The closed-form marginal already avoids quadrature. The open options (L-BFGS-B for the dependence bound, multistart, analytic Hessian) are already catalogued under NEW-nonconcave-dependence-mle and NEW-fd-hessian. The fit is a single small-p optimization, and no newer faster method for Clayton-copula AFT pairs was found.
- Batch A.6: L-BFGS (joint β, log θ): no_faster_acceptable_found. Same problem as the NB2 joint fit. Fisher-scoring alternation (TODO-5) and the α=1/θ L-BFGS-B parametrization (catalog P3) are already Prototype. No new source shows an NB-regression algorithm that beats them at EDI's n≈50–500, p≤10; the fastglm_nb vignettes report no timings.
- Batch A.6: L-BFGS (joint, block-separable): no_faster_acceptable_found. Hurdle-Poisson. Block-separate Newton/IRLS, which gives the same unique MLE, is already Prototype (catalog §3 TODO-3, pscl::hurdle precedent). Nothing newer or faster was found for a strictly concave two-block objective.
- Batch A.7: L-BFGS (Newton optional): no_faster_acceptable_found. Weibull AFT. Making Newton (newton_then_lbfgs with the analytic Hessian) the default is already the Prototype item (TODO-3, catalog §5), and the warm-started BRT draws were reported in batch A.1 today. survival's NEWS since 3.7-3 changes nothing in survreg's Newton–Raphson except a step-halving fix for NaN trial steps (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/survival/news.html). No newer evidence.
- Batch A.7: L-BFGS / damped Newton: no_faster_acceptable_found. The Poisson GLM C++ default is already IRLS (fast_poisson_regression.cpp:406, optimization_alg="irls"). On a strictly concave likelihood at small p it converges quadratically, and the LDLT step solve is the cost floor (catalog O2). Nothing faster at EDI's sizes.
- Batch A.7: L-BFGS / IRLS: no_faster_acceptable_found. Beta regression. Catalog O12 already covers the betareg recipe: moment φ start, then quasi-Newton, then a Fisher-scoring polish. betareg's newest NEWS entry (3.2-6) only adds analytic score/hessian methods to the BetaR distribution class (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/betareg/news/news.html). No new fitting algorithm.
- Batch A.7: L-BFGS / Newton–Raphson: no_faster_acceptable_found. Weibull AFT through fast_weibull_regression / fast_weibull_regression_cpp. Same as the 'L-BFGS (Newton optional)' rows: Newton by default is already Prototype TODO-3, and survreg's Newton–Raphson fallback has not changed (survival NEWS). The R wrapper also defaults to "lbfgs" (helper_glm_fit.R:1023), so TODO-3 should switch the wrapper default too.
- Batch A.7: L-BFGS then Newton fallback chain: no_faster_acceptable_found. Truncated NB count part. The first converged run returns, usually after one L-BFGS fit, and the chain only runs on failure. Catalog P3 already weighs the α=1/θ L-BFGS-B reparametrization. The search found nothing newer that is faster: Newton-type ztnb fits exist, but their speed is not quantified. An analytic Hessian exists (fast_hurdle_negbin.cpp:399), so Newton by default is possible but unbenchmarked; same pattern as TODO-3.
- Batch A.7: LDLT inverse: no_faster_acceptable_found. Ordinal vcov at d≈3–15. Eigen rates LLT and LDLT both 'very fast' and LLT's blocking only helps large matrices (https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__TopicLinearAlgebraDecompositions.html), so there is no faster decomposition at this d. The only saving is implementation-level and already recorded as incidental: the Hessian is built twice, and one LDLT could serve both the diagonal entry and the full inverse.
- Batch A.8: LDLT solve (QR fallback): no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_diagonal_inverse_entry factors the information matrix once with LDLT and does one solve. Eigen's benchmark (libeigen.gitlab.io DenseDecompositionBenchmark) puts LDLT at 1.3x LLT at 8x8, so the only cheaper option is LLT, which is less robust on near-singular information. That is a micro-choice already tracked as v1.1.0-TODO-155, and the O(p^3) cost is dwarfed by the O(np^2) build of X'WX.
- Batch A.8: LDLT with QR fallback: no_faster_acceptable_found. gee_solve_system: LDLT on the symmetrised p x p system is near the fastest dense SPD solve. Only LLT is faster, and only slightly (1.3x at 8x8 per Eigen's benchmark). Not worth the robustness loss; it sits in v1.1.0-TODO-155 territory.
- Batch A.8: likelihood-ratio test (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. The LR statistic is a closed form 2*(nll0 - nll1) plus one chi-square tail, O(1); nothing is faster. The related Rao score test forms a full covariance_from_information(I_nn) and then a matrix-vector product, where one LDLT solve would be enough. That is a constant factor at p in the tens and is already noted as v1.1.0-TODO-155.
- Batch A.8: LU log-determinant: no_faster_acceptable_found. R determinant() (LU) on an information matrix of size p in the tens. A Cholesky log-determinant would save about 1.5x on an O(p^3) term, but the evaluator has no caller yet and the row is already marked Investigate for a sign issue, so a speed change does not matter here.
- Batch A.8: marginal cumulative proportions → link quantiles: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form start, O(nK), run once per fit. Nothing faster that matters: the O(nK) count loop could be O(n) with a level index, but it is negligible next to one Hessian build.
- Batch A.8: MLE + cluster-robust sandwich: no_faster_acceptable_found. Weibull AFT: Newton MLE plus a pair-cluster meat built from per-cluster score sums (rowsum + crossprod), O(np^2). This is the standard and minimal computation. Changing the variance estimator would be an estimator change, not a speedup.
- Batch A.8: modified Poisson IRLS + cluster sandwich: no_faster_acceptable_found. Poisson IRLS (Newton, quadratic convergence) plus a hash-grouped cluster meat, O(np^2 + Gp^2). A search found no newer fast algorithm for this problem. Fast-GLM packages (capybara, FastHDFE) speed up high-dimensional fixed-effects demeaning, which does not apply to EDI's marginal model with pair clusters.
- Batch A.9: multistart L-BFGS + damped Newton polish: no_faster_acceptable_found. The only real speedup is to replace the central-FD Hessian in the Newton polish (fast_ordinal_glmm.cpp:220-223, still present at fa2fe36d) with an analytic Louis-identity Hessian. That is already catalog §4 TODO-2 (Adopt). The ordinal NEWS at cran.r-project.org shows no optimizer changes in the 2025–2026 releases; clmm still uses nlminb/ucminf, not Newton. No new evidence against multistart (Keep-tracked).
- Batch A.9: Newton (block-separable hurdle MLE): no_faster_acceptable_found. Newton on separable binary and truncated-NB blocks, with count-block-only null refits, already exploits the structure. Comparators (pscl-style optim/BFGS) are slower by construction. No newer method found.
- Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson: no_faster_acceptable_found. cpoisson-combined and continuation-ratio are concave canonical-link Newton fits at small p with quadratic convergence. The open items are robustness (halving, LDLT info checks: catalog §1 TODO-2), not speed. No faster method in recent literature.
- Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson (Cox partial likelihood, Breslow): no_faster_acceptable_found. survival 3.8-10..3.8-12 NEWS shows no change to coxph fitting. coxstream 0.1.0 (CRAN, 2026-06) is a memory-saving streaming Newton with no speed claim. FastSurvival (Liu, Zhang & Rudin, NeurIPS 2024) targets high-dimensional and sparse Cox via surrogate minimization and gives no win for small-p unpenalized Newton from a good start. EDI's C++ cached risk-set Newton is the hot path; coxph.fit is a fallback only.
- Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson (IRLS): no_faster_acceptable_found. fastglm 0.1.4 (CRAN, 2026-11-11) runs the same IRLS with Marschner step-halving. Its Cholesky path is the same per-iteration cost as EDI's C++ LDLT IRLS, so it is no faster than what EDI already has; its value is the step-halving pattern already in TODO-2. KK21 univariate logistic (2×2 IRLS, design-time only, O(p·iter·n)) is not a hot path. A logit(ȳ) start or a closed-form log-OR for two-valued columns would save only an iteration or two.
- Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson (IRLS) + inverse-variance weighting: no_faster_acceptable_found. Two small C++ logistic fits plus a closed-form IVW combination. Nothing faster exists; the legacy path only.
- Batch A.9: Newton–Raphson (IRLS) + sandwich / dispersion variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. Parametric-bootstrap refits already warm-start β and Fisher info (get_fit_warm_start_*). The sandwich is O(np²) once per fit. One-step/k-step bootstrap would change the estimator (an approximation), so it is not acceptable as a drop-in.
- Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson (IRLS) with step-halving: no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_poisson_internal: canonical-link Newton = IRLS with LDLT and cached-Δη O(n) halving probes. This is the same scheme the newest comparator uses: fastglm 0.1.4 (CRAN, 2026-11-11) is IRLS with Marschner (2011) step-halving. Quadratic local convergence at p in the tens; nothing faster at EDI sizes.
- Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson (Weibull AFT MLE): no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_weibull_regression / KK21 reduced fit / univariate_weibull_tstat use damped Newton on the analytic Hessian with an L-BFGS fallback, at p ≤ ~10. Newton is already the fastest-converging option. The BRT warm-start and Newton-default item was already reported by batch A.1 (2026-11-16). The Burridge reparametrization is already in the catalog (§ Weibull options) and changes robustness, not speed. survival 3.7-3 fixed survreg's step-halving NaN check, which is irrelevant to EDI's own kernel.
- Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson / Fisher scoring: no_faster_acceptable_found. Cumulative-link and adjacent-category C++ Newton/Fisher kernels. ordinal (the reference CLM package, NEWS through 2026-07-26) still uses modified Newton–Raphson with no optimizer change since 2013. No newer algorithm found that beats Newton at n in the low thousands and K·p in the tens.
- Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson with step-halving: no_faster_acceptable_found. PPO ladder rung ordinal::clm(nominal=): the modified Newton with step-halving is the reference algorithm (ordinal NEWS: unchanged since the 2013 clm.fit.NR redesign). The ladder runs only when nonparallel terms exist. Rungs after the first run only on failure, so speed depends on the first rung's success rate, not on the algorithm.
- Batch A.10: Newton–Raphson with step-halving (survival::coxph): no_faster_acceptable_found. weighted_cox_bootstrap_surrogate_fit already has the coxph.fit direct-call fast path (bayesian_bootstrap_performance.md TODO-6.1), and the native weighted Efron kernel is planned there. survival NEWS 3.7-3 / 3.8-0 has no algorithmic speedup, only a step-halving NaN fix and a survreg robust-variance fix. FastSurvival targets regimes EDI does not run.
- Batch A.11: normal equations (LDLT): no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_ols.cpp. Eigen's DenseDecompositionBenchmark (https://eigen.googlesource.com/mirror/+/03f646b7e352a68f26fff252615bedb4a2b359aa/doc/DenseDecompositionBenchmark.dox) puts LLT/LDLT on normal equations, with AᵀA formation counted, about 5x faster than HouseholderQR at 10000x8 and 4.3x at 10000x100. It is the fastest dense option at EDI's tall, small-p shapes. LLT is about the same speed. The known weaknesses (κ² conditioning, LDLT not flagging rank deficiency, SSE cancellation) are accuracy issues already recorded in catalog §1 O10 and Incidentals 6–7. They are not a speed gap.
- Batch A.11: normal equations with LDLT: no_faster_acceptable_found. rand_bootstrap_ols_parallel.cpp BRT loop. Rows are resampled and w is redrawn every replicate, so the Gram matrix must be rebuilt each time. MacKinnon's fast pairs cluster bootstrap (https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:qed:wpaper:1465) precomputes per-cluster cross-products. That only helps when the number of clusters G is much smaller than n. With singleton rows (G = n) it reduces to the count-weighted Gram option that catalog §7 P5 already rejected (same O(np²)). Per-replicate LDLT is about 5x cheaper than QR per the Eigen benchmark. Nothing faster that keeps the estimator.
- Batch A.11: OLS (lm.fit / lm.wfit) + HC2: no_faster_acceptable_found. KK_ols_one_lik: R lm.fit/lm.wfit (Householder QR) followed by a C++ HC2 post-fit, which costs O(np²), the same order as the fit. Leverages need X(XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀ diagonals, as the fixest vignette notes for HC2/HC3. No cheaper exact method was found. The real cost here is R overhead per draw, which is already tracked as catalog §10 TODO candidate 9 (C++ batch randomization kernel). That is not an algorithm-choice issue.
- Batch A.11: OLS + HC0 sandwich: no_faster_acceptable_found. Linear-probability-model OLS delegates to fast_ols (normal equations + LDLT). HC0 needs only residuals and the bread, so it is O(np²) and needs no leverages. Nothing faster exists for the same estimator.
- Batch A.11: OLS + HC2, inverse-variance-weighted combination: no_faster_acceptable_found. KK_ols_ivwc: two small OLS+HC2 fits and a closed-form inverse-variance-weighted combination with Satterthwaite df. The IVWC step is O(1). The fits are covered by the HC2 entries above. The R-loop overhead in randomization draws is already tracked in catalog §10 TODO candidate 9.
- Batch A.11: OLS on log-time (Gumbel moment shift): no_faster_acceptable_found. A one-off Weibull AFT start in _helper_functions_core.h, O(np²) once per fit. The result is a start value, not an estimate, so any faster OLS would not change answers. Closed form; nothing to gain.
- Batch A.11: OLS via normal equations (LDLT): no_faster_acceptable_found. Start values for fast_gaussian_lmm (make_start). It runs once per fit, and normal equations + LDLT is the fastest dense least-squares solve per the Eigen benchmark. Its cost is negligible next to the LMM optimization.
- Batch A.11: OLS via QR: no_faster_acceptable_found. inference_continuous_ols.R. The R side delegates to fast_ols_cpp (normal equations + LDLT), and QR is only the hardened column-drop fallback. The fast path already uses the fastest method (Eigen benchmark: QR is about 4–5x slower at tall, small-p shapes). The QR fallback is a rank-handling choice and should stay.
- Batch A.12: reverse cumulative-sum risk sets (Breslow): no_faster_acceptable_found. O(np² + Kp²) per iteration with one sort is already the standard optimal sweep (coxph's own algorithm). Newer work targets other problems: coxdev 0.4.0 (CRAN, 2026-11-07, GPL≥3) uses the same reverse-cumsum structure to give η-space derivatives and matrix-free information for penalized/large-p fits. FastSurvival (arXiv 2410.19081) uses surrogate minimization for high-dimensional, correlated designs and gives no win over Newton at p in the tens. survival NEWS 3.8-x has no change to the risk-set algorithm. The only open item is the constant-factor SYRK Hessian assembly, already catalogued as TODO-5.
- Batch A.12: pivoted QR rank reduction: no_faster_acceptable_found. Eigen ColPivHouseholderQR is effectively tied with CompleteOrthogonalDecomposition at small sizes (Eigen dense-solver benchmark: 0.23 ms each at 8x8), and SVD is slower. The known O(n p³)→O(n p²) single-factorization rewrite of qr_reduce_preserve_cols_cpp is already catalogued (TODO-6), and the keep-set is cached, so this is not hot. No new evidence.
- Batch A.12: pivoted QR rank detection + column dropping: no_faster_acceptable_found. R qr() uses LINPACK dqrdc2. LAPACK dgeqp3 is faster but does not report rank (R qr docs and R-devel threads), so it would break rank detection. The greedy refit-per-dropped-column loop only runs on rank-deficient designs (rare). Nothing faster that keeps the same keep-set.
- Batch A.12: QR rank check + inverse diagonal entry: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(p³) at p in the tens, post-fit only. Factorizing twice (QR rank check, then LDLT single-RHS solve) is a constant-factor issue already tracked as TODO-155. No algorithmic alternative is faster; one rank-revealing factorization reused for the solve is the known fix.
- Batch A.12: Rao score test via Schur complement: no_faster_acceptable_found. Efficient information I_tt − I_tn I_nn⁻¹ I_nt equals 1/[I⁻¹]_tt, so it can be one LDLT solve instead of covariance_from_information(I_nn) followed by a matvec. That is the constant-factor change already noted on the inventory row (TODO-155). It is O(p³) at small p and the same statistic, with no new algorithm.
- Batch A.12: per-stratum informative-row filter: no_faster_acceptable_found. An R loop with which(strata_id==s) is O(n·S). A split()/tapply single pass is O(n) and gives the identical row set. It is already recorded as Incidental on the inventory row, the result is cached (strat_cox_informative_rows_cache), and it runs outside resampling loops. It is not an algorithm research item.
- Batch A.12: pilot-fit warm start: no_faster_acceptable_found. Starts for nonconcave joint models (dependent-censoring transform, Weibull frailty, Weibull AFT) come from marginal fast_weibull / survreg fits. A cheaper closed-form start, such as OLS on log y, would save one pilot fit but likely cost extra outer iterations on the nonconcave surface. I found no source that benchmarks this. It is a start, not an estimator, and it is Keep in catalog §12.
- Batch A.12: Pearson moment estimator: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(np), a start value only, nothing faster. Searches found no standard truncation-corrected moment start for zero-truncated NB. The truncated-NB variance literature notes that the commonly cited Gurmu–Trivedi second moments are incorrect, so a 'corrected' moment start is not a clear win. Keep.
- Batch A.13: symmetric pseudo-inverse: no_faster_acceptable_found. Called once per fit post-fit on a d×d free information (d small); rank-awareness on possibly indefinite matrices needs an eigen/SVD-type decomposition. Eigen's benchmark (8x8: LDLT 1.3x LLT, COD 4.3x, SVD ~19x) shows the cheaper rank-revealing options give negligible absolute savings here and LDLT would lose rank-awareness.
- Batch A.13: Terrell gradient statistic (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. Code (_helper_functions.h gradient_test_from_restricted_score) is O(1): score[idx]*(estimate-null) plus pchisq; no Schur complement or inverse. The inventory 'details'/'complexity O(p^3)' text was copied from the score-test row and should be corrected; nothing faster exists.
- Batch A.13: Weibull AFT likelihood (analytic derivatives): no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form O(np) vectorized NLL/gradient and O(np^2) analytic Hessian (same as survreg's analytic derivatives); nothing faster per evaluation. The real lever (Newton default / warm-started BRT draws, optimizer choice) is already Prototype under TODO-3 and is not this kernel.
- Batch A.13: weighted Gram matrix accumulation: no_faster_acceptable_found. Upper-triangle column-pair dots at p small; the sqrt(w)*X + DSYRK / Eigen rankUpdate option is already Keep-tracked under TODO-155 (performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md), and no new benchmark for tall-skinny X'WX at EDI sizes was found this run (Eigen bench_syr only covers rank-1). Continuation-ratio structured Hessian already Adopt (TODO-3).
- Batch B.1: closed form + FD Hessian: no_faster_acceptable_found. ZipLimitLikelihood is a fallback-only boundary refit. Its objective and gradient are closed-form O(nk). The FD Hessian costs 2k gradient evaluations, which is O(nk²): the same order as an analytic ZIP Hessian, which would only buy accuracy (~1e-7 FD error). Nothing faster exists; the catalog's Keep stands.
- Batch B.1: Newton / L-BFGS (mixture MLE): no_faster_acceptable_found. This is the R-level dispatch to the same C++ kernels (fast_zinb_cpp / fast_zero_augmented_poisson_cpp). The hurdle-Poisson path already exploits exact block separability, which matches fastglm's own hurdle design ('likelihood separates into a binary component and a positive-count component'). Nothing beyond the C++ kernel findings and the existing catalog §3 TODO 1/3/4.
- Batch C.1: adaptive quadrature oracle (QUADPACK): no_faster_acceptable_found. Runs once per session per ordinal version: 2 clmm fits plus 80 one-dimensional integrate() calls. Its job is to be an oracle independent of GH, so swapping it for a GH rule would defeat the purpose, and the cost is negligible.
- Batch C.1: BFGS (optim) with analytic gradient: no_faster_acceptable_found. Nothing beyond what is already tracked. The row is Prototype: damped Newton or trust-region with the analytic Hessian EDI already has. trust 0.1-9 (cran.r-project.org/package=trust, MIT, 2026-02-11) is an off-the-shelf R trust-region solver for that route, but it is not new evidence. The per-call re-sort and Golub–Welsch overhead of the getters is already logged as incidental. The no-op 'BFGS' else 'BFGS' ternary is still at inference_count_KK_cond_poisson.R:1243.
- Batch C.1: closed-form cumulative-link interval probability: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(K) per observation per node; nothing faster exists. The upper-tail cancellation fix (survival-function form) and the probit closed-form marginal (catalog §4, TODO-4) are already recorded.
- Batch C.1: fixed-effects cumulative-link surrogate: unclear. The inventory row is stale. At fa2fe36d, compute_weighted_clmm_estimate (inference_ordinal_KK_clmm_abstract.R:256) calls weighted_ordinal_random_intercept_fit, i.e. ordinal::clmm with a random intercept at nAGQ=20, not a fixed-effects surrogate. The KKGLMM weighted path (inference_ordinal_KK_combined.R:485) does the same. The estimand-mismatch Investigate looks resolved; the speed question now lives in the 'adaptive Gauss–Hermite quadrature (ordinal::clmm)' finding. Recommend updating or retiring this inventory row.
- Batch C.2: Golub–Welsch algorithm: no_faster_acceptable_found. K=20 rule, O(K³) once per functor build. Caching it (catalog T2, already Adopt) is the only real saving. Townsend–Trogdon–Olver Newton/asymptotic methods only pay off at large n and use Golub–Welsch for starting values at small n. SciPy also uses eigenvalues plus one Newton polish up to n=150. Accuracy note only: lme4 2.0-6 (2026-07-16) now symmetrizes its GH nodes/weights, so EDI's glmer(nAGQ=20) equivalence references can move in the last digits. Symmetrizing EDI's rule when the shared cached rule is built would keep them aligned.
- Batch C.2: Laplace approximation (glmmTMB): no_faster_acceptable_found. glmmTMB NEWS 1.1.10–1.1.15 (latest 1.1.15.2, 2026-09-29) adds TMBad autopar and an RTMB back-end, but no AGQ and no Laplace speedup. Stringer 2024 confirms Laplace performs very poorly for small binary clusters. The faster replacement for these bootstrap paths is EDI's own C++ GH kernels (already the Prototype/Investigate items), not a new algorithm.
- Batch C.2: Laplace approximation + nlminb (glmmTMB): no_faster_acceptable_found. Gaussian LMM: Laplace is exact and it is only the fallback; the closed-form C++ LMM plus the frozen-VC GLS replicate shortcut is already the fast path. Hurdle Bayesian bootstrap: the known faster option is the existing weighted hurdle-GLMM C++ (Prototype already recorded). No new glmmTMB/TMB optimizer evidence found.
- Batch C.2: max-shifted log-sum-exp: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n_gh) per group, fused with the weight normalisation. One-pass online variants only save a pass over 7–60 values. Nothing faster that matters.
- Batch C.2: piecewise log1p/expm1 evaluation: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(1) closed form; nothing faster. The current code (fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp:76-80) still uses log1p(-e^{-λ}) for all λ ≥ 1e-10, which loses relative precision for small λ. The Mächler log1mexp switch, log(-expm1(-λ)) for λ<ln2, costs the same and is already recorded as an incidental accuracy fix. Not a speed issue.
- Batch D.1: closed-form breakpoint inversion (affine statistics): no_faster_acceptable_found. Xu 2025 (Algorithm 2) uses the same breakpoint computation, sorted by an O(M log M) sweep. This is the sweep the inventory already calls irrelevant at B ≤ low thousands. Nothing faster for the same exact answer.
- Batch D.1: confirm pass + bisection refinement: no_faster_acceptable_found. It only corrects SMC early-stopping noise at the final bound: 2 full-r evaluations, more only on disagreement. The sequential-stopping alternatives (Besag–Clifford, Gandy 2009) are already in the catalog. No newer method found that is cheaper and keeps the decision.
- Batch D.1: exponential bracketing (doubling): no_faster_acceptable_found. Glazer–Stark 2025 use the same Wald-seeded doubling bracket. It costs a few evaluations (≤7 doublings) and is not the bottleneck. Nothing faster found.
- Batch D.1: per-draw QR least squares: no_faster_acceptable_found. The rows differ in every draw, so one factorization per draw is needed. Normal equations or Cholesky are faster per fit but square the condition number (sources fetched: SAS blog 2021, Julia discourse), which is an accuracy loss. Porting the loop to C++ is implementation-level, as the inventory already says.
- Batch D.1: randomization (permutation) distribution: no_faster_acceptable_found. An arbitrary user statistic must be evaluated once per draw: r × cost(stat) is the minimum and the affine shortcut does not apply. Nothing faster.
- Batch E.1: Monte Carlo two-sided p-value: no_faster_acceptable_found. This is the O(r) tail-count reduction of an already-computed t0s vector (rand_tail_counts + 2·min(ge,le)/n), and O(r) is the floor. The real lever is how many draws r are made, which belongs to the 'Monte Carlo permutation p-value' finding. A partial sort or selection does not beat one linear counting pass.
- Batch E.1: Monte Carlo randomization distribution (C++ kernel): no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_bai_distr_parallel_cpp is O(n) per draw with the halves reused across draws; the Bai et al. statistic needs every pair difference, so O(rn) is the floor. Saddlepoint or other approximations would change the test (catalog P4 / audit row E, already Prototype). Nothing newer found.
- Batch E.1: Monte Carlo randomization test: no_faster_acceptable_found. randomization_loop_cpp is dead (no R caller). The live R-callback refit path has O(r·refit) cost, so its lever is r, which is covered by the sequential-stopping finding. No algorithmic change to a dead driver is worth proposing.
