- Batch E.1: Monte Carlo randomization test (masked sums): no_faster_acceptable_found. Branch-free SIMD masked sums are O(n) per draw and memory-bound. At EDI's sizes (n ~ 50–1000, r = 501) that is about 5e5 flops, microseconds, so draw generation dominates. Saddlepoint (Robinson 1982) and the fastPerm/Segal et al. small-p approximation (arXiv 1605.03992, found in search, not fetched) are estimator changes aimed at p ~ 1e-6, which a 501-draw test never resolves. Exact DP/FFT and exact enumeration are already listed (catalog P4, TODO-6). The lever is r: see the sequential-stopping finding.
- Batch E.1: bootstrap-randomization Monte Carlo (masked sums): no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n) per replicate (gather + masked sums) is the floor for a fresh bootstrap resample per draw. The only known saving is hoisting the per-unit sharp-null transcendental out of the loop (catalog §7 Incidental #9, bit-identical), which is an implementation fix, not a new algorithm. No published faster BRT algorithm found.
- Batch E.1: Bernoulli trials (uniform threshold): no_faster_acceptable_found. One unif_rand per subject from the R-parity MT19937 stream. Faster generators (xoshiro, SFMT, bit-packed coins) all break the R/Python seed stream; the catalog already rejected them (P1, inventory verdict). At prob_T ~ 0.5, geometric-skip sampling gives no gain. Nothing new found.
- Batch E.1: cluster-level Bernoulli draws: no_faster_acceptable_found. One uniform per cluster plus an O(n) scatter: about G + n operations per draw, which is the floor for writing an n-length assignment column. Nothing faster exists.
- Batch E.2: within-pair sign-flip randomization draws: unclear. Drawing is O(n) per draw with one uniform per pair, the floor for R-parity MT draws (catalog P1 already rejected bit-packed or non-MT generators for parity). The only new item is Koning & Hemerik (Biometrika 2024): a deterministic sign-flip subgroup gives an exact test that reaches the same power with about half the transformations. That changes the reference set and the p-value, so it is an estimator/reformulation change, not a speedup. Reported as such, low confidence.
- Batch E.2: ridit analysis: no_faster_acceptable_found. The mean-ridit contrast is a single O(n) pass after O(n log K) scoring, which is the cost of reading the data. The search found no newer method. The exact 2×K null (Wilcoxon with ties, network/shift algorithms) is already tracked in exact_rank_tests_shift_algorithm.md.
- Batch E.2: resampling kernels (C++): no_faster_acceptable_found. The per-replicate O(n log n) rebuild becomes O(n+K) with count-by-level, and that is already tracked (ridit_kernel_level_slots.md TODO-1..3, catalog Incidental 9). The δ=0 pooled path is already hoisted to a linear statistic. The exact-null alternative is tracked in exact_rank_tests_shift_algorithm.md. The bootstrap-index R loop with arm rejection is an implementation detail, not an algorithm choice, and moving it to C++ would change the R RNG stream. No new external evidence.
- Batch F.1: best-improvement pairwise-swap local search: no_faster_acceptable_found. The best-improvement rule defines the design's allocation distribution, so any different search (first-improvement, KL, tabu) is a design change, not a speedup. The only speedups that keep the same local optimum (Gram G = M'M for O(1) per pair, plus routing through the pruned D-opt engine) are already tracked in catalog §8 P2 and small_kernel_hoists_batch.md Item A. I found no newer paper or software (the 2026-04-30 GreedyExperimentalDesign 1.6.1 on CRAN mentions only optional GPU acceleration) that offers a faster exact best-improvement scan at EDI's sizes.
- Batch F.1: best-improvement swap search with bound pruning: no_faster_acceptable_found. The sorted-scan early break is already the standard exact pruning for an additive delta a_i + b_j − 2P_ij. Exact maximum-inner-product search on the low-rank P would only pay off at n far above EDI's sizes. A code-reading observation, not from the literature: the D prune threshold adds the global 2·max_P, and a per-row max_j P_ij would be a tighter bound that is still provably safe. That is an implementation tweak worth a look, not a new algorithm.
- Batch F.1: Cholesky whitening: no_faster_acceptable_found. One-off closed form, O(np² + p³), and already the recommended form (catalog §8). The dense rank-p n×n Q is a downstream representation issue that v1.0.5-TODO-1/-2 (low-rank formulation, enumeration) already track. Nothing faster.
- Batch F.1: Dinkelbach parametric iteration: no_faster_acceptable_found. Dinkelbach is already Newton's method on the parametric function (superlinear outer convergence). Searching for 0-1 quadratic-fractional alternatives (a single Li-1994-style MILP linearization, SOCP/SDP plus branch and bound) turned up no computational study showing a win at n ≤ 20. At these sizes the gains come from cheaper subproblems (enumeration, v1.0.5-TODO-1; seeding t_0, v1.0.5-TODO-13; a faster MILP backend, see the Fortet finding), not from replacing the outer iteration.
- Batch F.1: greedy best-improvement pair flips: no_faster_acceptable_found. Pair-constrained mode costs O((n/2)·p) per round, already linear in the input with an O(p) incremental delta, so nothing faster is possible. Changing the flip rule would change the design.
- Batch F.1: greedy best-improvement pair switching: no_faster_acceptable_found. The search is the design (Krieger, Azriel & Kapelner 2019). The follow-up literature I found (the 2023 CJS matching + greedy hybrid; GreedyExperimentalDesign 1.6.1 on CRAN, 2026-04-30) gives no faster algorithm that reaches the same local optimum. The O(1)-per-pair Gram trick and the pruned-engine route are already tracked (catalog §8 P2, TODO 2).
- Batch F.1: greedy pruned best-swap search: no_faster_acceptable_found. This is the same shared engine as the bound-pruning entry: exact and already pruned, with the D bound adaptive and the A bound fixed per round as proven. The tighter per-row max-P bound noted above is the only cheap improvement I saw. No literature source offers a faster exact best-swap scan at n in the hundreds.
- Batch F.2: simulated annealing (swap moves): no_faster_acceptable_found. Beasley's UBQP study (tabu beats SA on most instances, SA wins on the largest) and SA with a 1-opt local-search polish are both already tracked: quantum_upgrade.md TODO-5 (PT/tabu) and catalog TODO-4 (polish plus an n-scaled budget). Nothing newer overturns them.
- Batch F.2: simulated annealing (single-exchange Metropolis): no_faster_acceptable_found. Same kernel as the swap-moves entry. Δ is already O(1) or O(p) per move and O(n) on accept. Better heuristics (PT, tabu, KL) are already tracked and would change the solution quality, not just the speed.
- Batch F.2: pruned best-improvement swap search: no_faster_acceptable_found. The search rule defines the design's allocation distribution, so a different search would change the estimator. It already uses O(1) deltas with a sorted-scan prune. Nothing faster that keeps the same best-swap trajectory was found.
- Batch F.2: incremental swap-delta objective: no_faster_acceptable_found. The O(1)-per-pair Gram trick (small_kernel_hoists_batch.md Item A) and routing through the pruned D-opt engine (P = 4G) are already tracked. Nothing faster found.
- Batch F.2: mean-|Δ| initial temperature heuristic: no_faster_acceptable_found. A one-off O(16·n²) calibration, negligible next to the 20k-move anneal. Standard practice; nothing to gain.
- Batch F.2: size-threshold solver dispatch: no_faster_acceptable_found. Glue. The cutoff should move with the solver (TODO-1 enumeration, or a faster MILP backend: see the HiGHS finding). There is no algorithmic alternative.
- Batch F.2: solver dispatch + mirror coin flip: no_faster_acceptable_found. Glue: the O(n²) objective re-check plus a fair coin. Nothing faster that matters. The BRT weaker-mechanism concern (incidental #1) is a validity issue, already recorded.
- Batch G.1: Derigs weighted non-bipartite matching: no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_binary_match_structure runs once per design build at n in the tens to low hundreds, where nbpMatching::nonbimatch (O(n^3) Derigs/Edmonds, GPL>=2, v1.5.6 2024-09-25 per https://cran.r-project.org/package=nbpMatching) takes under a second. Candidates checked: (1) Blossom VI (Arkhipov & Kolmogorov, arXiv 2604.20351 v2 Jul 2026, GPL-3, C++20, integer weights). Its benchmarks start at about 14k nodes and 10M edges, and on its smallest reported instance (TSP, 105k nodes) it is slower than Blossom V (0.23 s vs 0.20 s, https://arxiv.org/html/2604.20351v2). Nothing shows a gain at EDI's sizes, and it would add a C++20 and Boost dependency. (2) Blossom V is licensed for research only, so it is not GPL-compatible (https://pub.ista.ac.at/~vnk/software.html). (3) LEMON MaxWeightedPerfectMatching runs in O(nm log n), which on EDI's complete graph (m ~ n^2/2) is O(n^3 log n), no better than O(n^3) (https://lemon.cs.elte.hu/pub/doc/1.2.3/a00221.html). (4) The geometric/Euclidean O(n log n) algorithms (Hougardy & Tammemaa, https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.07749) are approximate, so they would change the pairs: an estimator/design change, not a speedup. The exact geometric route (Vaidya) was already noted and deferred by the audit. Also, an equally optimal solver can break ties differently, which changes the design's pairs; that is a further reason not to swap at these sizes. The p=1 sort-and-pair path is O(n log n) and optimal, so nothing is faster there.
- Batch G.1: minimum-weight perfect matching (blossom): no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_halves matches pairs of pairs on m = n/2 pair averages with nbpMatching::nonbimatch, once, and caches the result. It is not in the resampling loop: compute_bai_distr_parallel_cpp reuses the cached halves. With m in the tens, O(m^3) is negligible. The candidates are the same as for the Derigs entry: Blossom VI benchmarks only at 14k+ nodes (https://arxiv.org/html/2604.20351v2), Blossom V is research-only (https://pub.ista.ac.at/~vnk/software.html), LEMON is O(nm log n) (https://lemon.cs.elte.hu/pub/doc/1.2.3/a00221.html), and the Euclidean approximation algorithms change the matching, which would change the Bai–Romano–Shaikh variance estimate (https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.07749). Keep.
- Batch H.1: Atkinson D_A biased coin via full LU inverse: no_faster_acceptable_found. The two known speedups are already recorded. The Schur/block-inverse null-draw rewrite is Adopt (catalog P6 / TODO-5). For the per-arrival kernel, LDLT plus one solve is listed in catalog P5 with a ~3-5x estimate on the O(p^3) part, which the per-arrival O(tp^2) rebuild dominates. The Eigen dense-decomposition benchmark I fetched (https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html) puts FullPivLU at 1.9x LLT at 8x8, so it adds no new evidence. A search for faster D_A computation found only the carat/covadap implementations, which use the same formula. Sherman–Morrison was already rejected for good reasons (drift, column-set changes).
- Batch H.1: Atkinson D_A-optimal biased coin (LU inverse): no_faster_acceptable_found. Same kernel as above. draw_ws_raw / generate_permutations_atkinson_internal are already Adopt via the Schur complement (O(r·n·p²)), which keeps the coin probabilities equal to roundoff and the RNG stream aligned. The per-arrival assign_wt costs O(tp²+p³) once per arrival and is not a hot path. No algorithm newer or faster than the tracked plan was found.
- Batch H.1: batched Mahalanobis quadratic forms (GEMM): no_faster_acceptable_found. One GEMM rowsum((D·S⁻¹)∘D) is O(|R|p²), the same order as Cholesky whitening. Swapping solve() for Cholesky is already tracked (kk14_incremental_covariance.md TODO-3). Nothing beats exact quadratic forms at |R| in the tens to hundreds; the only sub-linear methods found are approximate (see the next row).
- Batch H.1: brute-force Mahalanobis nearest-neighbour + Hotelling T² threshold: no_faster_acceptable_found. The metric changes every arrival. The only dynamic-metric structure found, Qin/Reddy/Song 2023 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.01030), is randomized and approximate, so it breaks exactness and determinism. The scikit-learn guide (https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/neighbors.html) says brute force is competitive for N below ~30 and when there are few queries per structure; here there is one query per metric, which rules out amortizing a tree. The audit's Keep verdict holds. The covariance/QR rebuild is already tracked in kk14_incremental_covariance.md.
- Batch H.1: brute-force weighted Euclidean distances: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(|R|d) SIMD scan, one query per arrival. The weights change with each arrival's KK21 refit, so a static tree is invalid for the same moving-metric reason. Per the scikit-learn guide, brute force wins at these N and with few queries. This is already the minimal exact cost.
- Batch H.1: forward stepwise (FWL residualization / score test): no_faster_acceptable_found. FWL/MGS forward selection is O(np²), the optimal order, and the logistic path already uses one reduced fit plus Rao scores. Bernardi/Busatto/Cattelan, 'Fast QR updating methods for statistical applications' (https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.05905, v3 March 2026), updates only R. The abstract claims a 'substantial reduction' but gives no numbers, so the speedup is not quantified, and the method has the same asymptotic order. CRAN fastQR 1.1.5 (https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastQR, GPL≥2) assumes full column rank and has no pivoting in its update routines. Its Gram-based R-update also squares the condition number, the same objection as the rejected sweep-operator option in catalog P9. The stepwise beta/NB/ordinal full-refit paths (Prototype TODO-3) are a different algorithm and are not in this batch.
- Batch H.1: full rebuild with rank-revealing QR: no_faster_acceptable_found. Row-append QR updating (fastQR qrupdate/rupdate, https://cran.r-project.org/package=fastQR; the Chan RRQR and Stewart URV updating literature) does not fit for two reasons. First, the matrices are re-centred and re-scaled every t, which changes every row. Second, column-pivot (independent-column) decisions must match ColPivHouseholderQR, and fastQR's updates assume full rank and do not pivot. The tracked plan (kk14_incremental_covariance.md: recompute only when the varying set changes; drop the redundant second QR, catalog P8) is already the practical win. Pivoted Cholesky on a running Gram was already rejected because it changes collinearity decisions.
- Batch H.1: per-covariate univariate fits + Monte Carlo quantile threshold: no_faster_acceptable_found. The univariate fits are closed-form or a few Newton steps each, O(p·t·iters). The 500-pair MC threshold costs O(500d), already less than an exact all-pairs quantile. Any exact or deterministic pairwise-quantile method, the catalog's own TODO-7 included, changes λ and so the design rule (an estimator change). No search result gives an exact sub-quadratic pairwise-distance quantile in d>1 dimensions. Warm-starting the fits stays the low-priority Prototype already recorded.
- Batch H.2: Pocock–Simon minimization: no_faster_acceptable_found. pocock_simon_assign_internal computes G_k for K=2 arms over only the subject's J levels, so each arrival costs O(J). That is the minimum, since the rule has to read every one of the subject's factor levels. Fetched sources describe nothing faster: Wikipedia lists the same per-patient procedure, and carat 2.3.0 (2026-09-08) and Minirand 0.1.3 on CRAN claim no algorithmic speedup. Minor implementation detail (catalog incidental #10): a std::vector is allocated per (arm, covariate) inside the loop. It is not an algorithm choice.
- Batch H.2: Pocock–Simon minimization (incremental marginal counts): no_faster_acceptable_found. Updating the counts in place costs O(sum of the subject's levels) per arrival. That is optimal and keeps the same rule and the same RNG stream. A possible inventory issue: at fa2fe36d, ensure_factor_metadata in design_seq_one_by_one_pocock_simon.R:161 rescans all rows only on the first call (full_scan). After that it registers only row t through match() on the per-column map, so the 'rescans all t rows ... O(n²k)' wording in the inventory details and complexity columns may be out of date (see bug_fix_plans/v1_0_5/pocock_simon_per_arrival_rebuild.md). draw_ws_raw still calls get_subject_levels_idx once per row, an R-side cost (incidental #9), not an algorithm choice.
- Batch H.2: Pocock–Simon minimization (sequential simulation): no_faster_acceptable_found. Exact Monte Carlo null draws, i.e. replaying the sequential rule with flat int counts, cost O(B·n·J). Each draw depends on its whole path, so a draw cannot cost less than one O(nJ) replay, and exact enumeration would mean up to 2^n paths. The only newer speedup found is Kojima, arXiv:2608.05527 (Aug 2026), a Gaussian approximation (SIGA). It replaces the randomization distribution instead of drawing from it, so it changes the test (see the finding). Fast rerandomization methods such as fastrerandomize and Metropolis–Hastings samplers target threshold-based rerandomization, not path-dependent minimization.
- Batch I.1: Mahalanobis balance criterion (direct loops): no_faster_acceptable_found. The faster form (one GEMM indicTs·X_w plus a whitened squaredNorm) is already planned in v1.4.0 rerandomization_objective_vals_gemm.md. fastrerandomize (arXiv 2501.07642v3) also uses batched kernels, which supports that plan, but its 24× is GPU vs base R, so it gives no new evidence against EDI's C++.
- Batch I.1: full sort, best-fraction selection: no_faster_acceptable_found. A full order() on N = r/prop scores is O(N log N), small next to the O(N·np) scoring. Partial selection is O(N) but was already judged a trivial gain, and it can pick different members among boundary ties. No new evidence.
- Batch J.1: Bayesian bootstrap (Dirichlet weights): no_faster_acceptable_found. Weight generation is one vectorized rgamma(B*n, 1) call, O(Bn), and is negligible next to the B weighted refits. Switching to rexp (R's rgamma at shape 1 takes the GD branch per rgamma.c, while rexp uses Ahrens–Dieter 1972) could be marginally cheaper but would change every realized draw, and the saving does not matter. QMC and antithetic Dirichlet weights only cut Monte Carlo variance; they are already the audit row J antithetic note and are not a speedup. No newer source found. The I13 per-draw context rebuild remains an implementation item.
- Batch J.1: Bayesian bootstrap / parametric bootstrap refits: no_faster_acceptable_found. Conditional-logit BB and PB refits are already warm-started at the full-data MLE, with Newton/IRLS per replicate. The only faster options found (linear or quadratic one-step bootstrap, Claeskens et al.; perturbation bootstrap, arXiv:2007.01615) approximate the refit and so change the estimator. They are not acceptable as speedups.
- Batch J.1: Bayesian bootstrap refits: no_faster_acceptable_found. The weighted logistic g-computation refit per replicate is warm-started. Fully iterated weighted MLE is required to keep the same estimand, and the one-step or linearized alternatives are estimator changes. Nothing newer than the 2026-11-16 review was found.
- Batch J.1: bootstrap (per-draw HL selection): no_faster_acceptable_found. hl_from_groups materializes and runs nth_element up to 4096 pairs, otherwise sorts and does value bisection. The catalog already covers the alternatives: Monahan rank-space selection, Johnson–Mizoguchi/Frederickson–Johnson, the tie-safe early stop (`wilcox_hl_kernel_hoisting.md` TODO-3 and TODO-4) and the presort hoist (blocked by noise), and balanced indices are audit J. No new evidence overturns those decisions. The plain HL bootstrap kernel is dead code.
- Batch J.1: bootstrap (per-draw ridit rebuild): no_faster_acceptable_found. Each replicate gathers and rebuilds the ridit map in O(n log n). The O(n+L) counting/level-slot rebuild is already tracked as `ridit_kernel_level_slots.md` TODO-3 (v1.4.0 plan). It gives the same answer and is the only faster option; nothing beyond it was found.
- Batch J.1: bootstrap confidence intervals (quantile): no_faster_acceptable_found. Type-8 quantiles over B ≈ 501 values: R's quantile uses a partial sort, so this is effectively O(B) and microseconds against B refits. Balanced or importance resampling cut Monte Carlo variance, not run time, and are already tracked under audit J.
- Batch J.1: bootstrap percentile / studentized intervals: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form quantile and pivot work on a precomputed distribution, O(B log B) at most. Nothing faster exists, and the cost is dominated by the refits that produce θ* and se*.
- Batch J.1: bootstrap tail-count p-value: no_faster_acceptable_found. Tail counts are O(B). The only way to save refits is to stop early. Davidson & MacKinnon's pretest (howmany.pdf, Table 1, B_min=99, B_max=12,799) averages B* ≈ 320–490 under the null and ≈ 570–2,400 under alternatives. At EDI's default B=501 that is no saving except under the null, and it gives a decision rather than a fixed-B p-value. The bootstrap distribution is also cached and shared with the CI types. Besag–Clifford/SMC stopping is already tracked (`performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md` TODO-156, catalog TODO-8).
- Batch J.2: bootstrap-randomization draw materialization: no_faster_acceptable_found. Each draw re-runs the design on resampled covariates with its own per-draw w_seed. Running draws in parallel keeps results identical, and that is already Adopt under audit TODO-4. No algorithmic shortcut exists: the design must be replayed for each draw to get the BRT null.
- Batch J.2: bootstrap-randomization test: no_faster_acceptable_found. B design-aware resamples plus fresh assignments plus B refits is what the test is defined as. The cost is dominated by the refits, which are covered by warm-start and batch-kernel items in rows A and D. Nothing found that keeps the same test and is faster.
- Batch J.2: bootstrap-randomization test (C++ batch kernel): no_faster_acceptable_found. OpenMP over B with per-draw seeds is already in place. Per-replicate algebra cannot be hoisted because X[i_b] changes every replicate (catalog P5). Weibull and robust warm starts are already Prototype items in row A and catalog §1/§5. Fast-and-robust bootstrap (Salibian-Barrera) linearizes the refit, so it changes the estimator and was not proposed.
- Batch J.2: cluster bootstrap (bucketed group resampling): no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n + G + output) with one counting pass is optimal. The only possible gain is batched Lemire draws for the G group indices, which is negligible at EDI's G (reported under the i.i.d. Lemire finding).
- Batch J.2: conditional inversion sampling (Clayton copula): no_faster_acceptable_found. For bivariate Clayton, conditional inversion is closed form and uses d=2 uniforms. Marshall-Olkin needs d+1=3 uniforms plus a gamma frailty draw, so it is not cheaper (copula qrng vignette). The per-pair R loop could be vectorized, but that is an implementation fix, not an algorithm change, and it would change RNG consumption order.
- Batch J.2: exchangeable-unit resampling + quantile pivot: no_faster_acceptable_found. Largest-remainder allocation is O(strata) and the draws are O(B·n); B fits dominate. The partial Fisher-Yates improvement for the without-replacement subsample is already catalog §7 TODO-2. No new evidence.
- Batch J.2: i.i.d. multinomial bootstrap resampling: no_faster_acceptable_found. Index draws are O(n) per replicate. Poisson-weight bootstrap gives random replicate sizes, so it is an estimator change. Count-matrix vectorization (Chaibub Neto 2015) only helps moment statistics, not the refits EDI runs. Balanced bootstrap is already tracked (audit TODO-4 / catalog §7 TODO-1), and no new primary evidence was found.
- Batch J.3: KK compound bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. The C++ kernel does one O(n) pass per replicate (IVW combination of the pair and reservoir means), which is optimal. The O(B·m·n) R index build is an implementation fix (I9: precompute the split once), not an algorithm choice. Balanced resampling is already tracked under TODO-4.
- Batch J.3: inverse-variance weighting, one-pass moments: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, one O(Bn) pass, nothing faster. The known issue is accuracy: textbook Σx²−n·x̄² cancels catastrophically. The shifted one-pass fix is already recorded in catalog P7 (Chan, Golub & LeVeque 1983) at the same cost, so no new option.
- Batch J.3: m-out-of-n bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. Cost is B fits of size m < n, and nothing replaces them for the same √m-scaled pivot. Bag of Little Bootstraps (Kleiner et al., arXiv 1112.5016) targets massive n, not EDI's n ≤ low thousands, and the search found no 2025–2026 speedup work for m-out-of-n. Choosing m (minimum volatility) is a separate inventory row.
- Batch J.3: largest-remainder (Hamilton) allocation: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form: floor, then sort the S strata remainders, O(S log S) once per call. It is negligible next to the draws and refits, so nothing faster matters.
- Batch J.3: inverse-CDF categorical sampling: no_faster_acceptable_found. Each observation has its own probability vector (eta_i differs), so the alias method's O(K) table setup has to be rebuilt per draw and gives no gain over an O(K) inverse-CDF scan when K is small. Sources on alias vs inverse transform agree: alias wins only for static distributions. The per-observation R loop is an implementation issue (vectorise with a cumulative-probability matrix compared to runif(n)), and that cost is minor next to the ordinal refit that follows.
- Batch J.3: nonparametric bootstrap (rejection resampling): no_faster_acceptable_found. O(B·n) R loop, and effectively unreachable: the only caller returns NULL for KK (I10). Not worth optimizing; nothing faster is needed.
- Batch J.4: nonparametric bootstrap with rejection: no_faster_acceptable_found. The cost is B × attempts × a C++ refit. Rejecting unusable resamples is a statistical choice: any scheme that avoided rejection (conditional or smoothed resampling) changes the bootstrap distribution, so it is an estimator change. Balanced resampling is already tracked under audit J. Nothing new found.
- Batch J.4: pair (cluster) bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. Resampling whole pairs is O(n_R + m) per replicate, which is already optimal. The cost is dominated by per-replicate clone + refit. Balanced bootstrap is already tracked (Keep-tracked J). Faster cluster methods such as the wild cluster bootstrap change the estimator and only apply to OLS-type statistics.
- Batch J.4: pair-preserving (block) bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. A precomputed pair table plus Lemire draws gives O(n + B(n_R + m)), which is optimal for generating indices. In the production path, the R sample() callbacks cost little next to the refit. The only open item is the balanced variant, already tracked.
- Batch J.4: parametric null simulation: no_faster_acceptable_found. Vectorized r* draws at O(nK) cost, negligible next to the refits. Vectorizing the per-observation ordinal sample.int (one runif against cumulative probabilities) is already listed as incidental I16. Nothing new.
- Batch J.4: per-group linear scan aggregation: no_faster_acceptable_found. rowsum/tapply would make this O(n) instead of O(n·#pairs). That is already recorded in the verdict as trivial Keep, and it runs once per call, outside the replicate loop. Nothing new beyond the recorded option.
- Batch J.4: percentile bootstrap with Wald fallback: no_faster_acceptable_found. Type-8 quantiles plus an O(1) selection rule. The cost is the B refits, which are inherited from the generic bootstrap driver (row J). The Wald-fallback rule is a statistical choice, recorded as incidental. Nothing faster that gives the same interval.
- Batch J.5: rank-statistic resampling kernels: no_faster_acceptable_found. The KK Wilcoxon kernels are O(n log n) per replicate C++ rank statistics. The known wins are already tracked: the I9 index-build fix (split() once instead of which() inside the m-loop) and the hoisting of pair |diff| ranks at δ=0 (kk_signed_rank_hoisting.md TODO-1). Exact shift-algorithm nulls swap Monte Carlo for enumeration, so they are not a speedup at EDI's B and n. Nothing new found.
- Batch J.5: smoothed bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. Adds N(0, sd(y)/√n) noise per replicate, so cost is B refits. No source gives a refit-free way to apply the kernel noise to a general statistic: kernelboot still evaluates the statistic on every resample, and bag-of-little-bootstraps style schemes are subsampling approximations, so they change the estimator. The cheap bootstrap (Lam) is a different CI, reported under the subsampling finding as an estimator change. The I7 serial/unseeded loop is an implementation issue that is already recorded.
- Batch J.5: stratified bootstrap: no_faster_acceptable_found. stratified_bootstrap_indices_cpp is O(n log S): std::map bucketing, Lemire draws, then a portable edi_rng Fisher–Yates (the libstdc++/libc++ std::shuffle issue in incidental #17 has since been replaced in this function). It is negligible next to the refits. Balanced resampling remains the only option, and it is already the audit J prototype (Davison–Hinkley–Schechtman; a secondary source repeats that stratified data need their own balanced variant, as the catalog already notes).
- Batch J.5: studentized bootstrap-randomization test: no_faster_acceptable_found. Costs B fits with an SE, using the C++ mean-diff kernel or a reused worker, with the SE fixed at δ=0. The studentized-permutation literature (Janssen; Chung & Romano 2013; Ritzwoller & Romano review) is about validity, not speed. I found no algorithm that keeps the studentized statistic and needs fewer fits.
- Batch J.5: warm-started refit loop (sequential null anchoring): no_faster_acceptable_found. Chaining each permutation's null fit from the previous converged fit (concave likelihoods only, Fisher information dropped) is already the standard continuation / warm-start answer. The alternatives are one-step or k-step estimators and score-statistic substitution, and both change the statistic. I found no source with an exact-refit scheme that needs fewer iterations at EDI's sizes.
- Batch K.1: Fisher scoring + sandwich variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. Clusters have at most 2 members, the 2x2 V_g^-1 is closed form, and each iteration costs O(n p^2), the minimum for forming the p x p information. The one-step GEE of Lipsitz et al. (JCGS 2017) gets its 10x+ speedup (R-bloggers post: 0.087 s vs 1.946 s for geese at about 100 per cluster) by avoiding O(m^2) pair sums inside large clusters. That saves nothing at m <= 2, and it only approximates the iterated estimate (close but not identical), so it would be an estimator change. geepack NEWS through 1.3.13 (2025-10-14) has no solver changes.
- Batch K.1: GEE (Liang–Zeger): no_faster_acceptable_found. The C++ pairs-plus-singletons kernel is already the standard estimator at optimal per-iteration cost. The catalog T9 alternatives (GEE2, ALR, QIF, independence) are slower or change the estimator, and no newer evidence overturns that. One-step GEE is not acceptable for the same reason as above. The eager 12-threshold candidate ladder (inference_mixin_kk_gee_shared.R:298, still eager) is already recorded as an implementation note in catalog §11 and is not an algorithm-choice issue.
- Batch K.1: moment estimator (Liang–Zeger): no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form O(n) pass over pair Pearson products. Nothing can be faster. The phi-scaling bug recorded in the audit (F20) has since been fixed in fast_gee.cpp (alpha = num / ((N_pairs - p) * phi)).
- Batch M.1: Brent's method (uniroot): no_faster_acceptable_found. invert_ci_to_find_two_sided_pval_for_treatment_effect runs uniroot over α on a closed-form z/t CI (log-rank). The inventory already records the closed form 2Φ(−|z|) / 2·pt(−|t|,df), which needs zero root-finding. The cost (~10–15 O(1) CI evaluations) is negligible. Chandrupatla's gain over Brent on smooth functions is about 0–2 evaluations, so it is not worth it.
- Batch M.1: candidate scan + golden-section/Brent maximisation of p(delta): no_faster_acceptable_found. Three candidate evaluations cover the usual case. stats::optimize (golden section + parabolic) runs only when no candidate has p > α, and costs ~20–30 O(d+S) evaluations. No derivative-free 1-D maximiser is materially faster on a piecewise-smooth p(δ) with jumps. The real risk is a local rather than global maximiser on a non-unimodal minlike p, which is a correctness issue, not speed.
- Batch M.1: information-matrix standard error: no_faster_acceptable_found. SE = sqrt([I⁻¹]_jj) from the already-computed information matrix, O(p³) with small p. It is cached through shared(). Nothing faster preserves the same estimator.
- Batch M.1: inverse-variance weighted Wald: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(n) (paired and reservoir Wald variances, then IVW pooling). Nothing faster. Its correctness issues (not Newcombe despite the class name) are already recorded.
- Batch M.2: Newcombe hybrid score interval: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form from two Wilson intervals, O(1). Nothing is faster. ratesci's MOVER-Wilson is the same closed form.
- Batch M.2: Newcombe hybrid score interval; Brent inversion: no_faster_acceptable_found. The CI is O(1). The p-value inverts it over α with Brent (uniroot): about 10–15 closed-form evaluations, microseconds. I know of no closed-form inverse because each Wilson limit is nonlinear in z, and ratesci provides no Newcombe p-value to compare against. The remaining issues (F9 returning p = 1, uniroot's default tol) are accuracy bugs already logged, not algorithm choices.
- Batch M.2: Newton–Raphson with bisection safeguard: no_faster_acceptable_found. Newer endpoint methods do not beat it at EDI's sizes. Fischer & Lewis 2021 (RVM, a trust-region Venzon–Moolgavkar) needed about 3× the likelihood evaluations of the fastest method on an 11-parameter logistic benchmark, and its authors say it may not win on well-behaved likelihoods; its gain is robustness. CICO (LikelihoodProfiler) uses hundreds of likelihood calls per bound. Newton on the signed root (v1.0.5 audit TODO 4) remains the cataloged improvement.
- Batch M.2: safeguarded Newton test inversion: no_faster_acceptable_found. Same kernel as lrt_ci_nr_cpp / eval_lrt. RVM and CICO are not faster for smooth, small-p GLM-type likelihoods. The cataloged signed-root Newton prototype (v1.0.5 audit TODO 4) and Venzon–Moolgavkar remain the options, and there is no new evidence against their recorded trade-offs.
- Batch N.1: central finite differences: no_faster_acceptable_found. Where these are costly (log/identity-binomial score and Hessian, using a 4-point ℓ stencil at O(2np³)), the closed-form X'diag(∂²ℓ)X is already Adopt (v1.0.5 row N, catalog O5), and nothing beats it. The generic numerical_gradient/numerical_hessian take central differences of the analytic gradient, so a Hessian costs only 2p gradient evaluations at p≤~25. AD and complex-step would not be cheaper (the catalog already rejected both). The R Cox FD helpers are dead code, and ordinal g-comp compute_md_gradient calls a cheap O(nK) functional; its cost is negligible.
- Batch N.1: central finite differences + delta method: no_faster_acceptable_found. marginal_estimand_delta_se / numerical_gradient_central: the analytic gradient mean(μ_ai x_ai) is one O(np) pass and is already Adopt (v1.0.5-TODO-8). Complex-step was considered and rejected in the catalog. I found no new source that changes this.
- Batch N.1: central finite differences of gradient: no_faster_acceptable_found. For the ordinal GLMM / _glmm_engine observed information, the analytic Louis identity is already Adopt (v1.0.5-TODO-2), at about 1–2 gradient-equivalents versus 2d. Forward-mode AD costs about d gradient-equivalents and is no cheaper (catalog T5). For ZINB, an analytic Hessian costs about the same at k≈10 (Keep). A 2026 astrophysics paper (arXiv 2604.06318) reports AD Hessians 5–10× faster than FD, but that is against value-only FD with O(N²) evaluations, not FD of an analytic gradient, so it does not carry over.
- Batch N.1: central finite-difference Hessian: no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_weibull_frailty runs 2P gradient evaluations (P=p+2) once per fit, which is never a bottleneck. An analytic Hessian, complex-step or forward-over-reverse AD would save at most a constant factor on a one-off step, at high derivation or dependency cost (catalog NEW-fd-hessian). Keep.
- Batch N.1: delta method (finite-difference gradient): no_faster_acceptable_found. ordinal_gcomp_post_fit_cpp computes the md gradient by central FD over K−1+p parameters. Each evaluation is O(nK) and reuses the precomputed η, which is negligible next to the O(nd²+d³) Hessian and LU in the same call. The analytic gradient is already Adopt (low priority, v1.0.5-TODO-5). Nothing faster exists.
- Batch N.1: forward finite-difference Hessian: no_faster_acceptable_found. Clayton and dep-cens forward FD of the analytic gradient takes P+1 gradient evaluations, once per fit, which is already the minimum-evaluation FD scheme. An analytic Hessian saves only a constant factor on a one-off step. The dep-cens analytic Hessian is unreachable dead code, deliberately disabled (I-3). Complex-step second derivatives are reported as not clearly more accurate than FD (Lai & Crassidis, via search). Keep.
- Batch N.1: Louis identity (analytic observed information): no_faster_acceptable_found. Already analytic and exact for the quadrature-approximated likelihood, at O(n·n_gh·p²): about 1–2 gradient-equivalents. FD, AD and complex-step all cost more. Expected information has no closed form for GLMMs, and L-BFGS curvature pairs give wrong SEs. Cutting the node count (Laplace/AGQ) is a row C estimator question, not a derivative speedup.
- Batch O.1: Cephes rational approximation: no_faster_acceptable_found. Scalar Cephes erfc is about 1e-15 abs at O(1) cost. SLEEF (Boost license) has vectorized erfc at 1.5 ULP (Sleef_erfcd4_u15, sleef.org/x86.xhtml), but the SLEEF paper benchmarks only trig, exp, log and pow, not erfc. EDI's call sites are scalar per-observation likelihood loops, so a win at EDI's sizes is not shown. Keep.
- Batch O.1: Acklam rational approximation: no_faster_acceptable_found. Used only for optimizer warm starts and one Wilson z per CI. Exact AS241 for the Wilson CI is already adopted (TODO 7). Newer fast quantiles are either GPU-oriented and not benchmarked against AS241 (Shaw & Luu, arXiv 0901.0638) or low-precision (Voutier, ~1e-4). Nothing beats Acklam+AS241 for this job.
- Batch O.1: Lanczos + Stirling: no_faster_acceptable_found. Lanczos g=7/9 terms below x=8 and a 7-term Stirling series above is already a minimal-cost O(1) lgamma at ~1e-15 abs. SLEEF lgamma (1.0 ULP, vectorized) has no published timing, and the vec_cpp wrappers are dominated by R-call overhead. Side note, accuracy rather than speed: fast_lbeta computes lgamma differences, which cancel at large a,b; R's lbeta uses the lgammacor form there.
- Batch O.1: Lanczos approximation: no_faster_acceptable_found. The x<8 branch: one log and nine divisions. Higher-order Lanczos sets (e.g. Boost lanczos13m53) cost more and give no accuracy that matters inside lgamma differences. Keep.
- Batch O.1: closed form (lgamma differences): no_faster_acceptable_found. Three fast_lgamma calls per observation is already cheap. The known gap is the near-Poisson cancellation at large size, an accuracy item already adopted as TODO 8 (R-style log1p / Poisson-limit branch, same speed). A log-rising-factorial sum for small integer x could avoid both cost and cancellation, but I found no source benchmarking it, so it is not reported as a finding.
- Batch O.1: branch-split stable evaluation: no_faster_acceptable_found. plogis and softplus in branch-split form cost one exp plus a divide or log1p per element: closed form, O(1) per element, nothing faster that is exact. The approximate atanh-series variant (fast_log1pexp) is already catalogued (P11).
- Batch O.1: Goldberg log1p correction: no_faster_acceptable_found. Eigen 3.4.0's generic_plog1p is the same Kahan/Goldberg formula, and in 3.4.0 it is specialized only for Packet8f (float), so EDI's double-precision fast_log1p_arr is still needed. Eigen master adds EIGEN_DOUBLE_PACKET_FUNCTION(log1p, Packet4d), but that is the same algorithm (no speedup), and CRAN RcppEigen (0.3.4.0.2, 2024-08-24) has not moved to it. A later option is to replace the helper with .log1p() once RcppEigen bundles Eigen 5; that would simplify the code, not speed it up.
- Batch O.2: Miettinen–Nurminen score statistic: no_faster_acceptable_found. The z statistic is closed form plus one pnorm_fast call. Its only real cost is the 60-step bisection in mn_constrained_mle_pc_cpp. That bisection already has a catalogued and scheduled replacement (closed-form cubic, catalog §6 TODO candidate 3; inventory row for mn_constrained_mle_pc_cpp says 'Adopt, low priority (TODO 3)'). No newer source changes that, and the absolute cost is microseconds. The p floor at |z|>8 is the pnorm_fast tail clamp, already covered by the row-O accuracy TODO (v1.0.5 audit TODO-22).
- Batch O.2: rational minimax approximation: no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_atan is Cephes range reduction plus a 5/5 rational minimax at about 1 ulp, O(1). It is the standard libm-quality construction. No faster method at the same accuracy turned up, and it sits only in the cauchit CDF.
- Batch O.2: recurrence + asymptotic series: no_faster_acceptable_found. Boost.Math digamma (fetched) shifts into [1,2], uses a rational approximation there, and switches to the asymptotic series for x≥10. That is the same O(shifts) structure with about 1 ε error against EDI's ~1e-12 relative: more accurate, but not faster, and the page gives no timings. The FasterMath benchmark in search results showed SIMD digamma slower than scalar. The catalog already records EDI's version at 7–19× faster than R. No faster acceptable option.
- Batch O.2: Stirling series: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form O(1) polynomial in 1/x² used for x≥8 and already accurate to about 1e-15. Fewer terms would help only marginally and at the cost of accuracy near x=8. Nothing faster.
- Batch O.2: textbook one-pass sum-of-squares variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(n), bounded by reading the data. The accuracy fix (shifted one-pass, Chan–Golub–LeVeque) is already Adopt (catalog §7 P7 / TODO-4), and Welford was already rejected as slower in SIMD loops. The kk_bootstrap_reservoir_stats.cpp entries are dead code. Nothing new.
- Batch O.3: truncated atanh series: no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_log1pexp / log1pexp_array_fast cost one exp, one division and a 10-term Horner on s=z/(2+z), with s in (0,1/3]. The exp dominates. The alternatives are exact SIMD exp+log1p (e.g. Highway, Arm optimized-routines, 2-2.5 ULP), which adds a second transcendental call, or the exact Goldberg path, which the catalog already lists as ~1.5-2x slower. Neither is faster. A minimax polynomial in s² (the fdlibm e_log.c construction) could reach the same 5e-12 error with fewer terms, or near-ulp with the same 10 terms. That gains accuracy at the same cost, with at most a few FMAs saved. It is already listed in catalog P11 as 'cite needed' and is reported below only to supply that citation.
- Batch O.3: truncated series summation: no_faster_acceptable_found. expected_trigamma_y_plus_theta already does what the newest method in the literature does: one trigamma at θ, then the recurrence ψ₁(x+1)=ψ₁(x)−1/x² and the recursive NB pmf, at O(1) per term. That newest method is the trigamma-free tail-sum series E[ψ₁(ν+Y)]=ψ₁(ν)−Σ P(Y>y)/(ν+y)² from Yu, Dousti Mousavi & Yang (Statistical Papers 2024, arXiv:2306.04798). It is an equivalent reordering of the same sum with the same O(M) term count. Its Table 2 shows only ±25% timing differences against GFWL, and it never compares against a pmf sum that uses a recurrence. Its Theorem 2 bound, P(Y>M+1)/(ν+M), could serve as a stopping rule in place of the ad hoc μ+10·sd rule, but that is not a speedup. No O(1) exact method exists, and asymptotic expansions would change the estimator. The code is also unreachable from R: get_negbin_regression_expected_hessian_cpp has no R caller.
- Batch O.3: Wilson score interval (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(1) per CI, one quantile call. Nothing faster exists. The open item is accuracy, not speed: the code still calls fast_qnorm (Acklam, about 1e-9 relative error) at newcombe_speedups.cpp:23, so v1.0.5 TODO-7 (switch to the exact R::qnorm5 / AS241) is still open as of fa2fe36d.
- Batch P.1: adjacent-category binary expansion: no_faster_acceptable_found. The expansion writes at most 2 rows per subject, so its O(nK) cost is set by the size of the output and nothing is faster. A different likelihood (exact multinomial conditional) would change the estimator. The open issue is still SE validity, not speed: the stacked conditional fits are dependent, and Mukherjee et al. (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3085191, cumulative-logit analog) say a subject-clustered sandwich is needed. That supports the existing Investigate verdict, and it is not a speed finding.
- Batch P.1: Bai–Romano–Shaikh pairs-of-pairs variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. The λ̂² term is one sum over ⌊n/2⌋ blocks of 4 units (arXiv 2307.13094), and τ̂², Γ̂² are also linear, so each replicate costs O(n), which is the cost of reading the data. The pairs-of-pairs matching is computed once and cached (row G).
- Batch P.1: closed form (hypergeometric variance): no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form Σ s(n_b−s)/(n_b−1) in one O(n) accumulation pass. Nothing is faster.
- Batch P.1: closed form (pair-difference logistic): no_faster_acceptable_found. For 1:1 discordant pairs, the exact conditional likelihood reduces to no-intercept logistic regression on within-pair differences. Each evaluation is O(n_d p), the minimum. Gail–Lubin–Rubinstein and enumeration both reduce to this same 2-term sum.
- Batch P.1: closed form (Sherman–Morrison per group): no_faster_acceptable_found. The random-intercept ML NLL and gradient come from per-group sufficient sums via Sherman–Morrison on the compound-symmetry V_g. That is O(n p) per evaluation with no quadrature, the same O(n) Woodbury form general LMM codes reduce to for this model. Nothing is faster.
- Batch P.1: closed form (single pass): no_faster_acceptable_found. Splitting matched pairs from the reservoir is a single O(n p) pass, which is the cost of reading the data.
- Batch P.1: closed-form 2×2 inverse: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(1) stack-allocated 2×2 inverse. Nothing is faster.
- Batch P.2: continuation-ratio binary expansion: no_faster_acceptable_found. The exact sequential-logit reduction is the standard method. The only known speedup is to fit without the dense X_aug (structured Hessian, O(np²+nKp)). That is already recorded as v1.0.5 TODO-13 / catalog §2 TODO-3 (~5–8× theoretical). The search found implementations that avoid expansion (brms cratio, Stata crreg, both direct ML), which back that direction, but no new benchmark at EDI sizes and no different algorithm. expand_continuation_ratio_data_cpp is O(nK) row emission; nothing faster.
- Batch P.2: covariate centering and scaling reparameterization: no_faster_acceptable_found. One O(np) pass for column means and SDs plus a linear parameter map M applied once. This is already below the cost of the ordinal fit it conditions, and nothing asymptotically or practically faster exists.
- Batch P.2: DeLong structural-components variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. The naive DeLong placement values are O(n_T·n_C). Sun & Xu (2014, IEEE SPL 21(11):1389) get O(n log n) by expressing them as midranks. EDI already uses that equivalence: V10/V01 are cross-arm CDF midranks taken from a K-bin tabulation, so the R and C++ versions are O(n + K) after tabulation, at or below the Sun–Xu cost. Nothing faster is possible (every observation must be read).
- Batch P.2: factorized two-part fit (logistic + truncated NB): no_faster_acceptable_found. The CRAN pscl manual confirms the reference implementation does the same thing: hurdle.control(separate=TRUE) is the default, with 'the two components of the model ... estimated separately using two optim calls' (BFGS, numerical Hessian). EDI's factorization plus analytic-Hessian L-BFGS/Newton is at least as fast. The known sub-item (fit the logistic part by IRLS instead of L-BFGS with maxit 100) is already in the catalog §3 incidentals. No new evidence.
- Batch P.2: g-computation standardization (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. A closed-form plug-in, O(np) (or O(n(p+K)) for PO): one or K−1 plogis evaluations per subject per arm. Nothing faster; the cost is dominated by the upstream model fit.
- Batch P.2: Gehan / Peto–Prentice Wilcoxon (fused sweep): no_faster_acceptable_found. One sort plus a linear sweep over tie groups, O(n log n), the same as survival::survdiff(rho=1). A comparison sort is the lower bound for unsorted event times. Within resampling loops, presorting is already tracked under row Q (TODO-18).
- Batch P.2: generalized least squares (LDLT): no_faster_acceptable_found. The compound-symmetry Sherman–Morrison normal equations Σ_g w_g[X_gᵀX_g − c_g s_x s_xᵀ] are algebraically the quasi-demeaning (random-effects GLS = OLS on quasi-demeaned data) form, at the same O(np² + p³). With p in the single digits to tens, LDLT on a p×p system is optimal. lme4-style sparse PLS only pays off with many or crossed random effects, which KK pair/singleton groups (m_g ≤ 2) never have.
- Batch P.2: generic test inversion on Gaussian refits: no_faster_acceptable_found. Nothing beyond what the audit already adopted. With σ² fixed at the full-fit value, LR = score = gradient = (β̂−δ)²/(σ̂²[(X'X)⁻¹]_jj), so the bounds are closed form and the LR null law is exactly F(1, n−p) (v1.0.5 TODO-16 → v1.1.0-TODO-50). At fa2fe36d the code still does generic per-δ lm.fit refits (inference_continuous_lin.R:460, KK_ols_one_lik.R:302) plus the param-bootstrap re-simulation, so the adopted fix has not landed. No new candidate.
- Batch P.3: Kaplan–Meier median + Brookmeyer–Crowley CI: no_faster_acceptable_found. One grouped survfit plus quantile() costs O(n log n) per alpha and is memoised. The sort is the lower bound, so a faster algorithm is not possible. A C++ sweep that reused get_survival_stat_diff's sort would save only R/formula overhead (implementation, not algorithm). Alternatives such as the Su–Wei two-sample interval are different estimators, not speedups. survival NEWS up to 3.8-12 shows no change to quantile.survfit or BC limits that affects this.
- Batch P.3: Kaplan–Meier median + Brookmeyer–Crowley CIs combined by MOVER: no_faster_acceptable_found. Cost is dominated by the same O(n log n) survfit, and the MOVER combine is O(1). The 2025 arXiv work on Wald SEs from BC intervals (2503.03065) concerns how the SE is estimated, not speed. Nothing faster that keeps the estimator.
- Batch P.3: L-BFGS (joint): no_faster_acceptable_found. The ZOIB internal fit is already scheduled: Adopt for the fixed-γ path (skip the constant softmax loop) and Prototype for the split multinomial-logit Newton plus beta-regression Newton. No new evidence beyond what the catalog records, which already covers this likelihood factorization (Ospina & Ferrari).
- Batch P.3: L-BFGS (joint, separable likelihood): no_faster_acceptable_found. Already verdict Adopt: split the observed-category softmax from the beta regression, which gives the identical MLE (v1.0.5 TODO-1 / catalog §11). The search only reconfirmed the known separability, so there is nothing new to report. The R wrapper .fit_zero_one_inflated_beta remains apparently dead code.
- Batch P.3: L-BFGS (joint, unprofiled ML): no_faster_acceptable_found. Already Prototype: profiled 1-D Brent on λ=σ_b²/σ_e² with GLS β̂(λ), optional REML. lme4 itself profiles out β and σ. I found no newer one-parameter random-intercept algorithm that beats a bracketed 1-D search at EDI sizes (n up to thousands, p small). arXiv 2602.09247 (EM view of REML) is not faster.
- Batch P.3: martingale residuals (null Cox fit): no_faster_acceptable_found. A null Breslow coxph martingale residual is a weighted Nelson–Aalen sweep, O(n log n) for the sort and O(n) for the sweep, which is already optimal. Replacing coxph with a C++ sweep like fast_logrank_internal gives the same answer and is already recorded as implementation-only (Keep). No new algorithm found.
- Batch P.3: MOVER (method of variance estimates recovery): no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(1); nothing faster.
- Batch P.3: Newcombe paired hybrid score interval: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, O(1); nothing faster. However, the Investigate item resolves against the code: Newcombe's method 10 uses a continuity-corrected φ, and EDI uses the uncorrected Pearson φ. This is reported as an estimator/accuracy finding, not a speedup.
- Batch P.4: restricted mean survival time (KM area): no_faster_acceptable_found. Point RMST is survival::survfit(weights=) (C code, O(n log n) sort) + O(D) area loop; nothing asymptotically faster exists since a sort is needed. The only overhead is survfit's formula/model.frame setup per BB replicate, which is unquantified. A hand-rolled weighted KM sweep would remove it, but that is an implementation tweak, not a better algorithm. Interval-censored SE stays tracked under v1.0.5-TODO-7 (row Y).
- Batch P.4: ridit scoring (binary search): no_faster_acceptable_found. fast_ridit_scores_from_ref_indices is O(n_ref log K + n log K) with K = number of ordinal levels (small). A level-slot / counting lookup is O(n+K), but at small K the gain is negligible, and per-replicate slots are already tracked in ridit_kernel_level_slots.md. The between-level rule now scores by the cumulative mass below (incidental 2 appears fixed in code).
- Batch P.4: Robins–Breslow–Greenland variance (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. Single O(n) flat-accumulator pass (cmh_speedups.cpp compute_extended_robins_block_se_cpp), cached by the R class. Bounded below by reading the data, so nothing is faster.
- Batch P.4: sign test (closed form): no_faster_acceptable_found. Unweighted path is closed form, O(m). The weighted BB path loops over pairs with which(m_vec==pid), which is O(n·m). Precomputing pair row indices once (split/match) makes it O(n) with an identical answer, but that is the already-recorded incidental, not a new algorithm; no external source needed or found.
- Batch Q.1: bootstrap Kaplan–Meier (per-draw sort): no_faster_acceptable_found. The audit's existing Adopt verdict (v1.0.5 TODO-18: presort once with rank0, then an O(n) counting pass per arm, bit-identical) is still the best option found. The new CRAN package FastSurvival 2.0.0 (2026-11-16) gets its claimed ~30x over survfit from presorting and skipping formula overhead in a single C++ pass, which is the same idea as the planned presort, not a new algorithm. Vectorized sorts (vqsort) only change the constant and are tuned for large arrays. Nothing beyond the planned presort.
- Batch Q.1: bootstrap log-rank (per-draw sort): no_faster_acceptable_found. Already Adopt (v1.0.5 TODO-18 log-rank presort + merge). FastSurvival's survdiff_fast uses a two-pointer merge scan over per-arm sorted vectors, which corroborates the planned design but is not a faster one. ExaLT (arXiv 1309.4286) targets the exact permutation p-value, not the bootstrap-randomization (BRT) distribution, so it does not apply.
- Batch Q.1: bootstrap Mann–Whitney (sort + binary search): no_faster_acceptable_found. Ordinal JT bootstrap-randomization (BRT) kernel. The known O(n+K) count-by-level variant (or a presort plus stable partition) is already recorded as a small optional win (TODO 9 / perm-stat-kernel table). No new method found. The fast rank-test literature (e.g. arXiv 2010.09712) targets other statistics.
- Batch Q.1: Breslow estimator (per-event rescan): no_faster_acceptable_found. Still O(nK) in .breslow_hazard (helper_survival_fits.R:582). The audit's Adopt (reverse cumulative sum after the existing sort, O(n log n), same estimator) is the standard optimal fix. Nothing faster exists, because a sort is needed anyway. The verdict holds and the fix is still not implemented.
- Batch Q.1: comparison sort (deaths-before-censorings tie order): no_faster_acceptable_found. record_less is a comparator, O(1). For single evaluations, std::sort at n in the hundreds is fine. vqsort (arXiv 2205.05982) gives up to 20x only on large arrays and needs sorting networks for small ones, and it sorts keys rather than (time, dead, w) records. The real win is removing the per-draw sort (presort), already planned.
- Batch Q.1: comparison sort + linear sweep: no_faster_acceptable_found. CoxData is built once per dataset and cached via XPtr. A comparison sort is a lower bound for unsorted input, and radix or vectorized sorts only help at n in the thousands. Keep.
- Batch Q.1: Greenwood-type delta-method variance: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form after one sort plus suffix sums, O(n log n) once per call, not in a resampling loop. Alternative variance forms (Aalen-type, pseudo-values) would change the estimator, not speed it up. Nothing faster.
- Batch Q.2: hash-map frequency counting: no_faster_acceptable_found. Expected O(n), one pass, with first-occurrence tie-break. Any exact mode has to read all n values. A 3-slot array for logicals or a dense count array for small integer ranges only changes the constant factor, which does not matter for a once-per-call helper.
- Batch Q.2: inverse transform sampling (linear scan): no_faster_acceptable_found. The code still uses which(cumhaz >= tgt), which is O(nK). Replacing it with findInterval (already Adopt, §12 TODO-7) is the right fix. R's docs (fetched) give O(n log N), close to O(n) for sorted x. cumhaz is non-decreasing, so findInterval(tgt, cumhaz, left.open=TRUE)+1 picks the same index, the same answer. A vectorised runif(length(idx)) returns the same uniforms in the same order as per-subject runif(1), so the RNG stream is kept. Nothing faster than O(n log K) without sorting targets, which is the same order.
- Batch Q.2: Kaplan–Meier product-limit sweep: no_faster_acceptable_found. Single evaluation: one comparison sort plus an O(n) sweep, which is optimal for unsorted input. In resampling, the per-draw re-sort is already covered by the presort Adopt (TODO-11/-18). SIMD sorts (vqsort, x86-simd-sort) sort primitive keys and help int32 more than 64-bit keys (djbsort speed notes), and they don't apply directly to EDI's record comparator. The catalog already rejects this sort-swap option.
- Batch Q.2: log-rank sort-and-sweep: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n log n) sort plus one fused O(n) sweep, the same structure as survival::survdiff. The only real gain is the BRT presort/merge already tracked (catalog NEW-resample-presort, TODO-18). Sort-library swaps are constant-factor and already rejected.
- Batch Q.2: mixed-radix level coding: no_faster_acceptable_found. L ≤ 4 levels per column with early exit. std::map vs a small array is a constant factor on a once-per-fit helper, and the catalog already records it as Keep.
- Batch Q.2: Peto–Prentice weighted log-rank sweep: no_faster_acceptable_found. Same O(n log n)+O(n) sweep as log-rank, with the running pooled KM weight. There is no O(n²) form. Any per-draw presort would follow TODO-18. Nothing faster is known.
- Batch Q.3: ridit scoring (sort + binary search): no_faster_acceptable_found. The only faster algorithm is O(n+K) per replicate: precompute each subject's level slot once, then count per replicate, which is counting-sort reasoning (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counting_sort). It is already tracked in ridit_kernel_level_slots.md (v1.4.0, TODO-1/2/3, including a bit-identical tier), so it is not new. The plan should also hoist the per-element `reference == "control"/"treatment"` std::string compares in compute_single_ridit_estimate_cpp (ridit_distr_parallel.cpp:85-97) out of the loop into a bool or enum; that is a constant-factor gain with the same results. A search for a dedicated ridit permutation algorithm found nothing better. FFT permutation p-value methods (arXiv 1807.10046) target exact or tail p-values, not the Monte Carlo per-replicate statistic EDI computes.
- Batch Q.3: row gather: no_faster_acceptable_found. Row gather in match_stats_from_indices_cpp is O(np), which is the cost of reading the data, so nothing can be faster. The symbol is dead code anyway (only the dead kk_bootstrap_loop.cpp calls it). The match-id duplication issue (catalog Incidental #7) affects correctness, not speed.
- Batch Q.3: single-pass gather and summary sums: no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_bootstrap_matching_stats_cpp makes one O(np) pass that gathers the resample and accumulates the pair-difference sums. That cost equals reading the resample, so no algorithm can be faster. Balanced-bootstrap or variance-reduction alternatives are tracked under row J, and they change the resampling scheme rather than this kernel.
- Batch Q.3: sort + unique: no_faster_acceptable_found. init_levels runs sort+unique once per fit, O(n log n) on doubles. The ordinal MLE that follows costs O(npK) per iteration, so this step is negligible. Counting or hashing would need an integer key range and would not preserve the sorted order the thresholds need, so the gain is immaterial at EDI's sizes.
- Batch Q.3: sort + unique, binary search: no_faster_acceptable_found. get_levels/map_y_to_1K run once per fit (or once per shifted y in a CI search, still dominated by the LBFGS fit that follows). They cost O(n log n + n log K) with K ≈ 3–7. Mapping with a hash or a sort-permutation would save at most a log factor on a step negligible next to the fit, and it would add complexity around floating-point key equality.
- Batch R.1: all-pairs comparison (outer product): no_faster_acceptable_found. The weighted category tabulation with cumulative sums (O(n+K)) is already the agreed fix (Adopt, v1.0.5 TODO-1 in the catalog), and nothing beats O(n+K). Code at fast path inference_ordinal_jonckheere_terpstra_test.R:161 still calls outer() at commit fa2fe36d, so the Adopt item is still open. There is no new option to report.
- Batch R.1: Cauchy combination test: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(k) closed form, so nothing is faster. The only known improvement is the tail-accurate form: 1/(p·pi) for p<1e-16 and a Cauchy upper-tail for large statistics, as in Liu & Xie's ACAT code (search this run showed the ICSKAT ACAT implementation). That is already Adopt (v1.0.5 TODO-7) and is still unimplemented: inference_suite.R:4062-4063 uses tan/atan directly.
- Batch R.1: Clopper–Pearson interval: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form via two qbeta calls, O(1); nothing is faster. The p-value/CI convention mismatch (F26) is a coherence issue, not a speed issue, and is already recorded.
- Batch R.1: cumulative log-factorial table: no_faster_acceptable_found. A single O(n) cumulative pass of log sums that is built once. lgamma per entry costs the same or more. Nothing is faster.
- Batch R.1: direct pairwise distance matrix: no_faster_acceptable_found. It is used only by method='ompr' (solve_optimal_blocks), where the MILP has n²B binary z variables and dwarfs the O(n²p) distance build. The GEMM trick (||x||²+||y||²−2x·y) loses accuracy through cancellation and makes results depend on BLAS threading. The Mahalanobis explicit-inverse issue is already tracked in row U (v1.0.5 TODO-29).
- Batch R.1: exact CMH test (convolution of hypergeometrics): no_faster_acceptable_found. R's mantelhaen.test (fetched this run) computes the central convolved density once in C (C_d2x2xk). Every noncentral evaluation inside uniroot is just a log-reweight plus a normalization, O(S). The only faster published alternatives are FFT methods, which Vollset/Hirji/Elashoff report lose accuracy, and arXiv:1508.05803, whose O(K log K) bound only covers a pruning bound for the asymptotic CMH, not the exact test. Nothing faster and acceptable.
- Batch R.2: exact conditional binomial test: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(d) minlike sum over d+1 dbinom terms. R trunk's binom.test now has an O(log n) bisection path (Varadhan) that gives the same minlike p, but R itself uses it only for n >= 1234 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wch/r-source/trunk/src/library/stats/R/binom.test.R). EDI's discordant-pair counts d are far below that, so the per-call cost is microseconds even inside the Zhang CI bisection. Optional only if very large d ever matters.
- Batch R.2: exact conditional test (noncentral hypergeometric): no_faster_acceptable_found. O(S) log-weights plus log-sum-exp. No CDF closed form exists for the noncentral hypergeometric, so a full pass over the support is needed to normalize. Possible constant-factor gains: a ratio recurrence instead of 4 lgamma calls per term, or caching delta-free weights across the CI bisection (already noted in the verdict). Both are trivial at EDI sizes.
- Batch R.2: exact Jonckheere–Terpstra null distribution: no_faster_acceptable_found. This R wrapper delegates to exact_jonckheere_terpstra_pval_cpp. The faster algorithm, a polynomial DP over (level, treated-so-far, partial 2U), is already Adopt (algorithm_choice_audit.md TODO-15, catalog §6 TODO 1). Nothing new beyond that. EDISON-WMW (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4792850/) confirms that a DP over (subset size, rank sum) handles ties exactly.
- Batch R.2: exhaustive composition enumeration (DFS): no_faster_acceptable_found. No new option beyond the already-adopted polynomial DP (algorithm_choice_audit.md TODO-15). Since 2026-11-16 the kernel has an interrupt check and a 1e6-allocation guard (jt_exact_leaf_count_exceeds_limit) that stops and points to asymptotic inference. So until the DP lands, exact JT p-values are unavailable for moderate K. The DP would make those cases exact again in polynomial time. EDISON-WMW (PMC4792850) reports exact tied WMW at n = 100 in 2.87 s with a hash-map DP. EDI's level-count DP has a far smaller state space, so this is consistent with the theoretical sub-second estimate. The guard's own DP is O(K·n_T·max n_k), nothing faster needed.
- Batch R.2: Fisher / Stouffer / min-p p-value combination: no_faster_acceptable_found. The current combination is O(1) after the two component p-values, so there is no speed gap. The open issue is conservativeness with discrete p's (Investigate). New comparator: DPComb 1.0.1 on CRAN (2026-08-22) and Contador & Wu (SJS 2025). This is an estimator change, reported below as such.
- Batch R.2: greedy column addition with quantile binning: no_faster_acceptable_found. Runs once per design to build strata keys, O(k·bins·n log n) on small n. Not a hot path, so nothing worth replacing.
- Batch R.2: greedy nearest-pair blocking (blockTools optGreedy): no_faster_acceptable_found. quickblock (GPL>=3, 0.2.2) claims to be 'more than an order of magnitude quicker' but does threshold blocking: it has a minimum block size, a variable block count and no fixed B or block sizes (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/quickblock/refman/quickblock.html). That is a different design, not a drop-in. blockTools 0.6.6 (2025-01-22) on CRAN shows no algorithm change. O(n²p) is fine at EDI's n.
- Batch S.1: Efron biased coin: no_faster_acceptable_found. The C++ null-draw kernel already simulates the rule with running counts, O(1) per arrival and O(n) per draw, which is optimal. The O(t) recount in the R assign_wt runs once per real arrival and is not hot.
- Batch S.1: Fisher–Yates shuffle (permuted blocks): no_faster_acceptable_found. Block templates are small (a few to tens of entries), shuffled once per block. Batched bounded draws (arXiv 2408.06213) would save at most a handful of MT words per block and change the stream. The per-stratum std::map lookup in random_block_size_redraw_w_cpp costs more than the shuffle. Not worth it.
- Batch S.1: Fisher–Yates shuffle / Bernoulli draws: no_faster_acceptable_found. iBCRD uses a full Fisher–Yates of the template; a partial shuffle was already judged marginal, and the batched-draw idea is covered under 'Fisher–Yates shuffle'. Bernoulli uses one uniform per subject; bit-packing was rejected in the catalog (breaks the stream for no material gain) and I found no new evidence against that. The factorial design's R sample() loop is O(nr) and not hot.
- Batch S.1: independent fair coin per pair: no_faster_acceptable_found. One MSB test per 32-bit word per pair, with per-draw-index seeding (deterministic for any thread count): O(n/2) words per draw, close to minimal without bit-packing, which would change the stream. Seeding a fresh MT19937 per column (~1.3k LCG/state steps) is comparable in cost to the draws when n is small. That is the RNG-throughput question already tracked as performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-149(c), not a coin algorithm choice.
- Batch S.1: inverse transform sampling (AS 241): no_faster_acceptable_found. Bit-for-bit parity with R's 'Inversion' norm_rand is the binding constraint. Ziggurat or Box–Muller are faster but give a different stream, and no hot loop calls norm_rand. Closed form, O(1), keep.
- Batch S.2: largest-remainder (Hamilton) rounding: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed-form apportionment: G divisions plus a sort of G remainders, called once per allocation with G strata. G is small, so the cost is negligible. Any other rounding rule would change the allocation, i.e. an estimator change, not a speedup.
- Batch S.2: linear search: no_faster_acceptable_found. largest_divisor_at_most scans down from floor(sqrt(n)): O(sqrt(n)) modulo ops, run once per design construction, so microseconds. Factoring would not pay off at any realistic n.
- Batch S.2: linear-pass ID relabeling: no_faster_acceptable_found. compute_matching_grouping_cpp makes two O(n) passes over m_vec. Every subject must be read and written, so O(n) is the lower bound.
- Batch S.2: Mersenne Twister MT19937: no_faster_acceptable_found. SFMT (~2x per Wikipedia), xoshiro, PCG and Philox are all faster, but each breaks bit-identity with R's MT stream. The catalog's P1 table already considered and rejected them. Nothing newer than this row's 2026-11-16 review overturns that, and the throughput question is still tracked as performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-149(c).
- Batch S.2: MT19937 seeding via LCG scramble: no_faster_acceptable_found. Its 675 LCG steps (about 1-2 µs) are required to reproduce R's RNG_Init. Matsumoto's improved init_genrand would give a different state, so R parity would be lost. Cost per construction is negligible next to one r-column draw.
- Batch S.2: pair flips + Bernoulli reservoir: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n·r) with one uniform per pair/reservoir subject, which is the minimum for general prob_T. Bit-packing coins only works for dyadic prob_T and changes the stream. The real lever is parallel per-index streams for the serial generate_permutations_matching_internal loop, already covered by TODO-149(c) and the catalog's child-stream verdict.
- Batch S.2: per-column seeded coin flips (SplitMix64 + MT): no_faster_acceptable_found. It uses one MT word per pair (the MSB). Taking 32 coins from each word would be about 32x fewer words but changes the stream. At n·r ≈ 1e5 words (n in the hundreds, r ≈ 501) generation takes well under a millisecond and is already OpenMP-parallel with index-derived seeds, so the win is immaterial at EDI sizes.
- Batch S.3: sequential simulation of Efron biased coin: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(n) per draw: n dependent coin flips with running counts. Each null draw has to output an n-vector, so O(n) is the floor. Exact enumeration of the null is already tracked separately (catalog §7, item 6).
- Batch S.3: permuted-block randomization (random block size): no_faster_acceptable_found. Per-arrival queue pop is O(bs), so the algorithm is optimal. The known problem is correctness (catalog F1/F1b: the generic redraw uses the last subject's stratum, and sample() misbehaves when there is one block size). The fix is already sketched: wire up the existing dead random_block_size_redraw_w_cpp. No new algorithmic evidence.
- Batch S.3: sequential replay of assign_wt: no_faster_acceptable_found. Already Prototype (v1.0.5-TODO-10: C++ urn kernel, O(rn^2) R to O(rn)). Nothing better than per-design C++ kernels that replay the rule with running state. The RandomBlockSize bug is already recorded.
- Batch S.3: remaining-slot probability / Bernoulli coin: no_faster_acceptable_found. The per-arrival O(t) recount in R could be O(1) with running counts, but that is not a bottleneck. Redraws already run in C++ in O(nr), and sequential remaining-slot sampling is equivalent to a uniform permutation of a fixed multiset (Fisher–Yates, O(n)), which is the floor.
- Batch S.3: scaled 32-bit word (R fixup): no_faster_acceptable_found. One multiply and two compares; it must match R's unif_rand bit for bit, so nothing faster or more acceptable exists.
- Batch S.3: seed derivation from one R uniform: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(1), one R::unif_rand() per call. Cost is negligible. Seed-quality and collision concerns belong to the per-index derivation (see the SplitMix64 finding), not this step.
- Batch S.3: R's built-in random variate samplers: no_faster_acceptable_found. Simulation DGP only, O(n) per dataset and not inside resampling loops. Faster samplers (e.g. the ziggurat in dqrng, AGPL-3) would change the stream and break parity with R, for no gain at EDI's sizes.
- Batch S.4: stable sort of index permutation: no_faster_acceptable_found. build_draw (exchangeable_resampling_draws.cpp:187-190) sorts row indices only when preserve_order is set. The only callers are the PRW subsampling paths (inference_ext_prw_subsampling.R:90,402), which run once per subsample beside a full refit. A counting sort keyed on row index (rows ≤ n) would be O(N+n) instead of O(N log N) and would give an identical order. The inventory already records this as immaterial, and nothing found changes that. No web search needed: the textbook alternative is already in the inventory.
- Batch S.4: stratified permuted-block randomization: no_faster_acceptable_found. generate_permutations_spbr_internal uses dense integer stratum ids, one explicit Fisher–Yates shuffle per new block and pop_back. That is O(n) per draw, which is optimal because every draw must write n assignments, and it is already portable (v1.0.5-TODO-23 closed). The R-level assign_wt is O(1) amortized per arrival. spbr_redraw_w_cpp is dead code. Drawing one arrival at a time with a hypergeometric step would change the seed stream and would not be faster.
- Batch S.4: Wei urn design: no_faster_acceptable_found. Pr(T) depends only on the running counts (nT, nC), so a draw is a Markov chain with O(1) work per arrival. The assign_wt recount (sum(private$w == 1), O(t) per arrival) and the O(r n²) generic R draw loop are the known gaps. A C++ urn kernel with running counts would make null draws O(r n) and give the same distribution. That fix is already scheduled as v1.0.5-TODO-21 (§12 TODO-10), now release_v1_2_0.md TODO-22, so it is not new. randomizeR 3.0.2, the comparator package (cran.r-project.org/package=randomizeR, 2023-09-18, NeedsCompilation: no), offers no faster method. The design is unchanged since the row was reviewed.
- Batch S.4: Wichura AS 241 rational approximation: no_faster_acceptable_found. qnorm_std must match R's qnorm.c bit for bit, because RRng::norm_rand reproduces R's Inversion normal generator. Any other algorithm would break seed parity with R. Faster options I checked: Voutier (arXiv 1002.0567) has absolute error around 2.5e-5, which is far less accurate. Shaw & Luu (arxiv.org/abs/0901.0638) report a 2x speedup on GPUs against CUDA's function, not on CPUs, and do not claim full tail accuracy. R's NEWS (cran.r-project.org/doc/manuals/r-release/NEWS.html, read through about R 4.4) shows no qnorm change for non-log p. It is O(1) per call and not a bottleneck.
- Batch T.1: closed-form simple linear regression: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, one O(np) pass, so nothing can be asymptotically faster. The only open issue is numerical: the one-pass Σx²−n·x̄² and the expanded-SSE form cancel badly when |mean|/sd is large (catalog §8 incidental 4). A centered two-pass form costs the same O(np) and is an accuracy fix, not a speedup.
- Batch T.1: forward stepwise score (Rao) selection: no_faster_acceptable_found. This is already the fast design: one reduced fit per step plus O(nm) score work per candidate. The I-1 correctness issue looks fixed at fa2fe36d: the reduced fit now uses WeibullAFTLikelihood with Newton (kk21_stepwise_survival.cpp ~L37-46). The only remaining speedup is the constant-factor GEMM batching the catalog already lists. A side observation, not about speed and low confidence: I_jj.S projects out only the β block (XS'W XS), not the log-σ nuisance. β and σ are not orthogonal in the Weibull AFT, so the statistic may not be the efficient score. Worth a check alongside the inventory 'Keep' verdict.
- Batch T.1: forward stepwise via FWL + Gram–Schmidt: no_faster_acceptable_found. O(np²) total, with O(np) per step for scoring and projection. That matches the cost of QR column-append forward selection. Fast QR-updating work (arXiv 2412.05905v3, revised 2026-03) and Woodbury-update stepwise (arXiv 1706.03265) do not beat O(np) per step when every candidate must be scored. The Gram/sweep alternative was already rejected for squaring the condition number.
- Batch T.1: forward stepwise via Rao score tests: no_faster_acceptable_found. This is the standard fast scheme, the same as SAS PROC LOGISTIC score-chi-square entry and statmod::glm.scoretest: one warm-started reduced fit plus Rao z for every candidate. The only remaining options are constant-factor ones such as BLAS-3 batching of the X_S'W X_unused cross-products.
- Batch U.1: BLAS DSYRK rank-k update: no_faster_acceptable_found. X'X via DSYRK uses half the flops of GEMM and is BLAS-3; nothing better in order or constant at EDI's sizes (n up to ~10^3-10^4, p ≲ 50). I found no CPU source that benchmarks a specialized small-p Gram kernel beating DSYRK. arXiv 2406.19621 (2024) reports that for small BLAS-3 problems thread synchronization dominates; that concerns BLAS threading configuration, not the algorithm.
- Batch U.1: brute-force pairwise distance matrix: no_faster_acceptable_found. The output is n² (or m²), so the brute-force O(n²p) loop is order-optimal. The only faster variant is the GEMM/Gram expansion, which the catalog already rejects for catastrophic cancellation on near-duplicates. scikit-learn's docs (fetched) still describe that cancellation and the loss of exact symmetry for the dot-product form, so there is no new evidence to overturn the rejection. The column-major stride is an implementation detail (incidental 9). The Mahalanobis whitening item is already v1.0.5 TODO-29.
- Batch U.1: column z-score standardization: no_faster_acceptable_found. Closed form, two-pass, O(tp); nothing faster per call. Avoiding the per-arrival recomputation is the incremental plan already tracked (kk14_incremental_covariance.md TODO-1/2). A one-pass/Welford-style running update has the cancellation issues noted in row O.
- Batch U.1: column-pivoted (rank-revealing) QR: no_faster_acceptable_found. Eigen master's new RandColPivHouseholderQR (BQRRP, Gaussian sketch) lifts the BLAS-2 ceiling only for large matrices.
